->This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.<-

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>Handwritten notes or markings made in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>The electronic conversion of images of typed, handwritten or printed text into machine-encoded text</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to the second edition of a scholarly reconstruction of the Greek Old Testament. It establishes the editor's methodology for revising the text and critical apparatus compared to the first edition published four years prior.</meta>

<margin>
Archival Ref: R 348407

ID: 1000051635
</margin>

# HOW THE SECOND EDITION DIFFERS
# FROM THE FIRST.

Since twelve hundred copies were sold within four years <note>1</note>, a new edition of this book had to be prepared. Although it could not be prepared in such a way that every single part assumed the form that seemed best to me after these four years so full of critical studies—since the entire work had been printed from fixed type <note>This refers to "stereotype" printing, where plates are fixed and difficult to change extensively</note> from the beginning—nevertheless, a revision was undertaken of a kind that would not seem unworthy of the zeal of all those who are somewhat more curious about investigating sacred texts. Our care pertained not only to the text itself with notes on parallel passages and the <term>critical apparatus</term> <note>The system of notes documenting variant readings from different manuscripts</note>, but also to the **Prolegomena** <note>Introductory remarks</note> and **Epilegomena** <note>Concluding remarks</note>. For in the text, notes, and apparatus, not only was any typographical error corrected <note>2</note>, but also several corrections were received into the text, whether proceeding from the pen of the Roman editors (such as Psalm 7:5; 13:13) or those which are very easy and highly recommended by critical reason (such as Exodus 12:10, *you shall leave* <note>original: "ἀπολείψετε"</note> instead of *it shall be left* <note>original: "ἀπολείψεται"</note>; Deuteronomy 6:2, *to keep* <note>original: "φυλάσσεσθαι"</note> instead of *you shall keep* <note>original: "φυλάσσεσθε"</note>). Likewise, a fuller and more perfect notation of **New Testament** passages was provided, and the apparatus was corrected in several places. Much more labor, however, was placed in correcting and enlarging the Prolegomena. For Sections I, II, III, IV, V, and VI, which explain the origin, fate, and authority of the **Septuagint** <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> edition, are new; likewise, in Section VII, there is a more copious note on the history of the **Complutensian** and **Venetian** editions. Throughout the sections that follow, from VIII to XV, some things were changed and new material was added here and there. Section XVI, where the more serious changes received into our edition of the Roman text are reported, was changed and amplified in various places, especially because upon repeated examination I found more corrections made by hand in the first edition. Section XVIII, concerning the **Septuagint** text of the prophet **Daniel**, has now finally been added in its entirety; likewise almost all of Section XIX, concerning various repetitions of the Roman text. The matters set forth in Section XXI regarding **Holmes's** apparatus are now more extensive than before. Section XXII, concerning the readings of the **Vatican Codex** <note>One of the oldest and most important manuscripts of the Greek Bible</note> not expressed in the Roman edition, is for the most part new. Clearly indeed—

---

> 1) It first appeared at the end of the year 1850.
> 2) If there are any who, through frequent use, have come to know typographical or other errors in our edition, they would do us a great favor if they would be willing to point them out to us.

<summary>The editor explains the updates made to this second edition of the Septuagint, including typographical corrections, new scholarly introductions (Prolegomena), and expanded research on the Vatican Codex and the Book of Daniel.</summary>
<keywords>Septuagint, textual criticism, Vatican Codex, Prolegomena, biblical scholarship, Robert Holmes, printing history</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface to the second edition, detailing the specific structural changes and additions made since the first publication.</meta>

# VIII
## HOW THE SECOND EDITION DIFFERS FROM THE FIRST.

New sections XXIII and XXIV have been added, where I discuss the entire critical apparatus <note>The collection of manuscripts and variants used to determine the original text.</note> and especially the Greek uncial manuscripts <note>Manuscripts written in large, capitalized letters, typically the oldest and most important.</note>, providing samples of readings from those which were not yet known to Holmes <note>Robert Holmes (1748–1805), a biblical scholar known for his massive edition of the Septuagint.</note>. In various places, the sections concerning the manuscripts used for our continuous apparatus have been significantly altered: sections XXV through XXVIII, XXX and XXXI, and XXXIII and XXXIV. On the other hand, section XXIX is entirely new, in which the Prayer of Manasseh is transcribed primarily from the Codex Alexandrinus <note>A 5th-century Greek manuscript of the Bible, one of the most important in existence.</note>. Section XXXII is also new, where a variety of readings are provided from the fragment of the Codex Frederico-Augustanus <note>The name Tischendorf gave to the portion of the Codex Sinaiticus he discovered in 1844.</note> which I very recently brought to light. Sections XXXV and XXXVI contain supplements of variant readings for two passages that have been lost in the Codex Alexandrinus: <term>1 Kingdoms</term> <note>original: "I Bas." (1 Basileion). In English Bibles, this is 1 Samuel.</note> 12:17 to 14:9 and <term>Psalms</term> 49:19 to 79:12. Finally, a notable supplement has been added to our edition: the text of the prophet Daniel according to the <term>Septuagint</term> <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>, just as it was published from the <term>Chigi Codex</term> and corrected in various places from the Syriac <term>Milan Codex</term>. This matter is explained more accurately in the Prolegomena <note>The introductory preface or scholarly introduction.</note>, section XVIII.

Since this is the case, it is hoped that by this second edition of ours, the studies of many people regarding this so weighty and so holy monument of the ancient faith will be both stimulated and assisted. I have said enough regarding the purpose with which I took this labor of editing upon myself (compare especially the Prolegomena, section VIII); I would wish fair-minded judges to keep this in mind, lest they perhaps argue that I have not provided what I neither intended nor was able to provide at this time. Two things, however, constantly occupied my mind while preparing and revising this edition: first, how vast a field of labor lay open to critics regarding the study of the Greek text of the <term>Old Testament</term>, and second, I understood how much fruit would overflow from there into the study of the <term>New Testament</term>. I say this not only regarding the language in which the books of the Apostles were written, but also regarding the clarifying and illustrating of the entire relationship that exists between the two <term>Covenants</term> <note>The Old and New Testaments.</note>. To measure out this field of labor myself to the best of my ability, if God wills and gives me life and strength, I shall constantly give my effort. For I do not doubt that rigorous studies applied to these sacred texts are not only not inconsistent with the piety of a Christian man, but will bring forth good fruits for the Church itself—to which divine truth is of the highest importance—fruits of the kind that time shall not erase.

->Written at <term>Leipzig</term> <note>original: "Lipsiae"</note> on the 18th day of January, in the year of salvation 1855.<-

<summary>The editor outlines the specific manuscript additions to this second edition, including fragments from the Codex Frederico-Augustanus, and argues that Old Testament textual criticism is essential for understanding the New Testament.</summary>
<keywords>Codex Alexandrinus, Codex Frederico-Augustanus, Septuagint, Daniel, Prayer of Manasseh, textual criticism, Leipzig, Robert Holmes</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
# PREFACE.

**Among** the learned monuments of sacred antiquity, a distinguished place is held by the so-called Septuagint <note>original: "Septuagintaviralis" — referring to the "seventy" translators</note> version of the books of the Old Testament. Regarding its origin, there exists an ancient booklet upon which almost all ancient writers on this subject seem to have depended. It circulates under the name of Aristeas and takes the form of a letter written by Aristeas to his brother Philocrates <note>1)</note>. Aristeas—whom the letter itself testifies was held in great honor by Ptolemy Philadelphus, King of Egypt, and whom Josephus asserts was a bodyguard to the same king—relates that Demetrius of Phalerum, the head of the Alexandrian Library, suggested to King Ptolemy Philadelphus (who reigned from 284 to 246 BC) while the king was inspecting the library (which was his chief care to enlarge) in the presence of Aristeas himself, that the laws of the Jews also <note>original Greek: "καὶ τῶν Ἰουδαίων νόμιμα"</note> should be translated into the Greek language and brought into the royal library. To fulfill this purpose, he promised that the king would send letters to the High Priest of the Jews. However, to prepare for this event, the king—at the persuasion of the same Aristeas—granted freedom with remarkable greatness of soul to all Jews who had been led into captivity either by his father Ptolemy, son of Lagus, or even earlier. Then, following the advice of Demetrius, who testified that the books of the Mosaic Law had been translated rather carelessly and not as they truly were, since they had not received the care of the royal mind <note>2)</note>, the king wrote...

---

1) After the first Greek and Latin edition of the Letter of Aristeas in Basel in 1561, various editions appeared, such as the Frankfurt edition amended according to the Vatican manuscript—though that is not the most famous manuscript of the Bible—from the recension of Eldanus de Parchum in 1610; likewise the Oxford edition of 1692, repeated in the dissertation of Anton van Dale at Oxford in 1705, and in the same place and year by Humphrey Hody as a preface to his four books on the original texts of the Bible. But it is both worthy of and quite in need of new critical labors, which I intend to undertake shortly.

2) <note>original Greek: "τοῦ νόμου τῶν Ἰουδαίων βιβλία σὺν ἑτέροις ὀλίγοις τισὶν ἀπολείπει· τυγχάνει γὰρ Ἑβραϊκοῖς γράμμασι καὶ φωνῇ λεγόμενα· ἀμελέστερον δὲ καὶ οὐχ ὡς ὑπάρχει σεσήμανται, καθὼς ὑπὸ τῶν εἰδότων προσαναφέρεται, προνοίας γὰρ βασιλικῆς οὐ τέτυχε."</note> "The books of the law of the Jews, along with a few others, are missing; for it happens that they are expressed in Hebrew characters and language. They have been recorded <note>original: "σεσήμανται"</note> rather carelessly and not as they truly are, as is reported by those who know, for they have not received royal attention." The word *recorded* <note>original: "σεσήμανται"</note> in this passage is serious and ambiguous. *To me, indeed, according to the context, it seems it should not be understood in any sense other than that of translation.*

<summary>This preface introduces the historical origins of the Septuagint, focusing on the Letter of Aristeas and the role of King Ptolemy Philadelphus and Demetrius of Phalerum in commissioning the Greek translation of Jewish Law.</summary>
<keywords>Septuagint, Letter of Aristeas, Ptolemy Philadelphus, Demetrius of Phalerum, Library of Alexandria, Mosaic Law, biblical translation, Hellenistic Egypt</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion (Prolegomena) regarding the origins of the Greek translation of the Old Testament, specifically focusing on the Letter of Aristeas and early Jewish-Hellenistic testimony.</meta>

# PREFACE

To Eleazar, the High Priest of the Jews, [King Ptolemy sent a request] that he should send to him, along with a copy of the Law, seventy-two elders—six chosen from each tribe—who excelled not only in other virtues but specifically in the skill of translation. When these letters, accompanied by magnificent gifts, were brought to Jerusalem by Andreas and Aristeas, Eleazar complied with the Egyptian King’s wishes in every respect. He honored the King's envoys, who were wonderfully delighted by the affairs of the Jews, with a copy of the Law written in letters of gold and other gifts for the King in return. He then sent them back to Alexandria with seventy-two highly esteemed men, whose names are specifically recorded. Once the Jews were brought to Alexandria, they were splendidly received by the King. Their wisdom, brilliantly demonstrated during banquets, filled the King with admiration. On the eleventh day after their arrival, they began the translation of the Law. This work was organized such that, under the guidance of Demetrius <note>Demetrius of Phalerum, the royal librarian</note>, they crossed over to the island of Pharos. There, by comparing their individual drafts, they worked toward a perfect agreement on every point and dictated the final version to Demetrius. The work was completed within seventy-two days. A day was then appointed for the translation to be read aloud before the Jews and the Egyptians. After the reading, the Jews themselves praised it highly; furthermore, at the request of Demetrius, they pronounced a curse upon anyone who should alter any part of the text. When these things were done, the King rejoiced and, full of admiration for the Jewish Lawgiver <note>Moses</note>, asked why none of the poets or historians had mentioned such a great work. Demetrius replied that because the Law possessed a unique sanctity, it could not be touched by foreigners without danger; he noted that Theopompus and Theodectes <note>Greek authors</note> had been restrained by divine punishments for attempting to do so. Upon hearing this, the King treated the Law with veneration, ordered the books of the translators to be religiously preserved, and sent the translators back to their homeland adorned with royal gifts.

II. This, then, is the summary of the Letter of Aristeas. Now, after Aristeas, the Jewish philosopher Aristobulus—a contemporary of Ptolemy Philometor ¹)—made mention of the Greek translation of the books of the Law. According to the testimony of Clement and Eusebius ²), while [he was discussing] the whole...

---

1) Regarding the age of Aristobulus, one should consult Valckenaer in his discourse on *Aristobulus the Jew*, etc., 1806, page 27 and following. He treats the error of Anatolius found in Eusebius' *Ecclesiastical History* 7, 32 on page 24 and following.

2) As Eusebius testifies in *Preparation for the Gospel* IX, 6 (Gaisford edition, vol. II, p. 356) and XIII, 12 (Gaisford edition, vol. III, p. 310), Aristobulus wrote the following: <note>original Greek: "Φανερὸν ὅτι κατηκολούθηκεν ὁ Πλάτων..."</note> "It is evident that Plato followed our legislation, and it is clear that he carefully studied each of the things said in it. For it [the Law] had been translated by others before Demetrius of Phalerum, even before the supremacy of Alexander and the Persians—specifically the parts concerning the exodus of the Hebrews (our fellow citizens) from Egypt, the fame of all the events that befell them, the conquest of the land, and the detailed explanation of the entire Law... but the complete translation of all the contents of the Law was made in the time of [King Philadelphus]..."

<summary>This page summarizes the legendary origins of the Septuagint as told in the Letter of Aristeas and introduces the testimony of the philosopher Aristobulus, who claimed Plato was influenced by earlier Greek translations of the Torah.</summary>

<keywords>Septuagint, Letter of Aristeas, King Ptolemy II Philadelphus, Eleazar the High Priest, Demetrius of Phalerum, Aristobulus of Alexandria, Plato, Pharos, translation history</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of the Greek translation of the Old Testament (the Septuagint), moving from general historical claims to specific accounts by Philo, Josephus, and the Church Fathers.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA. XI

that the interpretation of the law was made under Ptolemy Philadelphus <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus, King of Egypt (285–246 BCE), a great patron of the Library of Alexandria.</note>, and he asserted that even before then, several various details from the history of the Jews had already reached the knowledge of foreigners.

Nearly two centuries later, Philo the Jew <note>A Hellenistic Jewish philosopher from Alexandria.</note> handed down these same accounts regarding the matter. He wrote that Ptolemy Philadelphus, a man worthy of the highest praise, having conceived **a zeal and longing for our legislation** <note>original Greek: ζῆλον καὶ πόθον λαβὼν τῆς νομοθεσίας ἡμῶν</note> for the sake of translating the law, sent ambassadors to the High Priest of the Jews, who was also their king. The High Priest, greatly delighted by the king's plan as if it were divinely inspired, sent to him the most esteemed men who were skilled in both languages <note>Hebrew and Greek</note>. When they arrived, they were generously received by the king and proved their wisdom by their answers to difficult questions; then, for the purpose of carrying out the translation, they withdrew alone with their books to the island of Pharos, noted for its tranquility. There, with God miraculously favoring their prayers, they translated in such a way that all things agreed entirely among themselves. The virtue of the translation was found to be so great in the judgment of men learned in both Chaldean <note>Here meaning Hebrew/Aramaic</note> and Greek, that they regarded those men not merely as translators, but as priests and prophets (**calling them not translators, but initiates and prophets, whose minds were able to run in accord with the purest spirit of Moses** <note>original Greek: οὐχ ἑρμηνεῖς ἐκείνους ἀλλ’ ἱεροφάντας καὶ προφήτας προσαγορεύοντες, οἷς ἐξεγένετο συνδραμεῖν λογισμοῖς εἰλικρινέσι τῷ Μωυσέως καθαρωτάτῳ πνεύματι</note>). Because of this, a grateful memory of such a great event is celebrated annually on the island of Pharos, not only by Jews but also by many others ^1).

Philo, who saw the light of day around the birth of Christ, was followed by Josephus, born in the year 37 AD. He himself says that he drew the history of the translation of the books of the law from the letter of Aristaeus—for he uses this form of the name, **Aristaeus**, not **Aristeas**—whose text, just as it is read in our ancient written books, he often changed and adapted to his own use; nevertheless, in the substance of the matter, there is no difference between the two ^2).

III. From those writers, we come to the Fathers of the Church, among whom the leader in this cause is Justin Martyr. He not only repeated the story of the translators summoned by Ptolemy Philadelphus, whose number he sets at seventy, but even

---

**the king surnamed Philadelphus --- having displayed the greater ambition, while Demetrius of Phalerum managed these affairs.** <note>original Greek: προσαγορευθέντος Φιλαδέλφου βασιλέως --- προσενεγκαμένου μείζονα φιλοτιμίαν, Δημητρίου τοῦ Φαληρέως πραγματευσαμένου τὰ περὶ τούτων.</note> Almost the same things, with the exception of the last parts, are found in Clement, *Miscellanies* Book I, page 410.
1) See Philo, *On the Life of Moses*, Book II, § 5–7. (Mangey edition, page 139 ff.)

The passage cited by us above from the text of Aristeas is read in Josephus as follows: **I make it known to you that the books of the legislation of the Jews are lacking to us along with others; for being in Hebrew characters and in the national tongue, they are obscure to us. It has also happened that they have been recorded more carelessly than they should be, because they have not yet attained royal care.** <note>original Greek: δηλῶ σοι τὰ τῆς Ἰουδαίων νομοθεσίας βιβλία λείπειν ἡμῖν σὺν ἑτέροις· χαρακτῆρσι γὰρ Ἑβραϊκοῖς καὶ φωνῇ τῇ ἐθνικῇ ἐστὶν ἡμῖν ἀσαφῆ. συμβέβηκε δὲ αὐτὰ καὶ ἀμελέστερον ἢ ἔχει σεσημάνθαι, διὰ τὸ βασιλικῆς οὔπω τετυχηκέναι προνοίας.</note> Immediately following is: **It is necessary that these also be accurately set forth in your collection.** Nor did Josephus omit what Aristeas had handed down concerning Theopompus and Theodectes. # XII
-> PROLEGOMENA <-
<note>Latin: "Prolegomena" refers to a formal introductory treatise or preliminary remarks.</note>

...he so expanded and refined it that he related the interpretation not to the law alone, but to the writings of Moses and the rest of the prophets <insert>1</insert> <note>original: "τὴν τοῦ Μωυσέως καὶ τῶν ἄλλων προφητῶν"</note> and claimed that the seventy translators themselves were enclosed in as many individual cells for the work of translating, and thus prevented from meeting with one another; nevertheless, he claimed they all produced one and the same interpretation, with the King marvelling at this as a divine matter. As proof of this narrative, he testifies that he received these accounts from the inhabitants of Alexandria and that he saw the ruins of those cells on the island of Pharos with his own eyes. Finally, he notes that the same can be learned from other writers, most notably from **Philo** and **Josephus** <insert>2</insert>.

The divine power which **Justin** had attributed to the seventy translators in these matters was confirmed by **Irenaeus** and **Clement**, and after them many others, although they did not all repeat every part of the story—such as the detail about the seventy cells—or did not relate them in exactly the same way. But two in particular are worthy of more careful consideration: **Epiphanius** and **Jerome**. For the former <insert>3</insert> departs from the tradition of **Justin** regarding the cells, in that he says the seventy-two translators were enclosed in thirty-six cells, specifically two in each, and he shapes many other details in that manner. For he says that twenty-seven books—that is, all the books of Holy Scripture—were translated into the Greek language, and he narrates the whole affair so as to prove it was carried out "in a marvelous way" and by the "singular providence of God and the consensus of the Holy Spirit." In particular, it has seemed serious to many that the things which **Ptolemy Philadelphus** wrote to Jerusalem—first for the books of the prophets <insert>4</insert>, and second for the purpose of sending translators of the Hebrew text to him—differ greatly from what is read in the booklet of **Aristeas** and even in **Josephus**, although **Epiphanius** appeals to **Aristeas** himself in that matter <insert>5</insert>.

**Jerome** viewed it far differently. His own words in the prologue to Genesis are these:

> "I do not know what first author constructed the seventy cells
> at Alexandria by his own lie, in which they, being divided,
> wrote the same things, since **Aristeas**, the <term>defender</term> <note>original Greek: ὑπερασπιστής (hyperaspistēs), meaning a shield-bearer or protector</note> of the same **Ptolemy**,
> and **Josephus** a long time later, reported no such thing, but
> write that they, gathered in a single hall, conferred together,
> rather than prophesied."

1) <gloss>of Moses and the other prophets.</gloss>
2) See *Exhortation to the Greeks*, chapter 13. Again in another place (in *Apology I*, section 31), touching upon the same history, he confirms that the books of the prophecies <note>original: "τὰς βίβλους τῶν προφητειῶν"</note> were requested by King **Ptolemy**; in that account, however, he confuses the dates by saying that the Egyptian legates were sent to King **Herod**.
3) See **Epiphanius**, *On Weights and Measures*, volume II, page 161 (Cologne edition, 1682).
4) <gloss>books of the prophets concerning God and the creation of the world.</gloss>
5) <gloss>as Aristeas set forth in his treatise.</gloss>

# PROLEGOMENA. XIII

> "For it is one thing to be a prophet, and another to be an interpreter: in the former, the Spirit predicts things to come; in the latter, learning and an abundance of words translate those things which he understands." <note>original: "Aliud est enim vatem, aliud interpretem esse..." This famous distinction by St. Jerome emphasizes that translators rely on skill and study rather than divine revelation.</note>

Likewise, he repeatedly refutes the opinion of those who believed all the books of the Old Testament were translated by the Seventy interpreters, as he states in his commentary on Ezekiel, Book V:

> "Although Aristeas, Josephus, and the entire school of the Jews assert that only the five books of Moses were translated by the Seventy." <note>The "Seventy" refers to the Septuagint (LXX), the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>

IV. And indeed, these points are drawn from Greek and Latin sources. We must also see what the Hebrews thought on the same matter: although, if we seek the truth, it appears that the origin of the Greek version should be learned from the Hellenists <note>Greek-speaking Jews</note> rather than the Hebrews, since most of the Hebrews, led by a veneration for the holy language, held that version in contempt <gloss>1)</gloss>. However, the Hebrew tradition concerning our subject is found chiefly in two Talmudic passages, one of which belongs to the Babylonian Talmud and the other to the Jerusalem Talmud. In the former (Babylonian Talmud, Megillah 9), Rabbi Judah mentions seventy-two elders summoned by King Ptolemy into as many separate cells; by his command, and with God marvelously favoring them, they translated the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible</note> with the highest consensus. But he censures fifteen passages because they exhibit a change from the holy text. In the second passage (Jerusalem Talmud, Megillah 6), the same matter is treated, but nothing is read concerning King Ptolemy as the chief author of the translation, nothing of the seventy elders, nothing of the cells, nor is the translation said to have applied only to the Pentateuch—although the thirteen passages cited as differing from the Hebrew are taken only from the Pentateuch <gloss>2)</gloss>. Therefore, the latter passage is simpler than the former and seems more sound to the Hebrews; but we must see rather which one better represents the opinions that were circulating in antiquity itself.

V. Having set forth all these things, the question is what faith should be placed in them. For more than fifteen centuries, full credit was given to the booklet of Aristeas <note>The "Letter of Aristeas," a primary source claiming the Septuagint was divinely commissioned</note>, and critical men, such as the great Jerome, departed from the common tradition only in that they rejected with sound judgment those details by which the narrative of Aristeas was later embellished and enlarged. However, from the time that the credibility of Aristeas's booklet fell under suspicion by Juan Luis Vives in his commentary on Augustine's *The City of God* (Basel, 1522), at Book XVIII, chapter 42, there have been many who would prove both that the entire booklet is a forgery and the event itself a fiction. This opinion was acutely and [powerfully argued] by Humphrey Hody...

---

1) See Humphrey Hody, *On the Original Texts of the Bible*, page 218 ff., especially page 227.
2) See the Hebrew text of these passages in Frankel: *Preliminary Studies on the Septuagint*, page 25 ff.

# PROLEGOMENA.

page XIV

...learned defense <note>original: "docte defensa"</note> ^1) was soon so widely accepted that there was scarcely anyone who disagreed. Therefore, the opinion of the vast majority today is that the name of Ptolemy Philadelphus <note>Ptolemy II, King of Egypt (285–246 BCE), traditionally credited with commissioning the Greek Bible</note> did not pertain to the common Greek version in any way, nor did the Seventy translators agree in a miraculous manner—or even exist at all—nor did anything divine occur during the translation or truly reside within it ^2). Nor is there any doubt that many things are rightly said against the reliability of the *Letter of Aristeas* and those traditions celebrated by Christian writers ^3). Nevertheless, one must be careful not to conclude too easily from the variety of forms that a tradition takes that the entire tradition is a lie: for it often happens that, out of a desire for skepticism, truths or at least probabilities are discarded along with the fables. Even if the booklet of Aristeas is considered apocryphal <note>meaning here "of doubtful authenticity"</note>—such that it is not by the author it claims to be and contains many things entirely foreign to historical truth—it does not follow that the entire history it relates must be placed in the category of mere myth, nor that the origin of the booklet must be pushed back to much more recent times, as Anton van Dale ^4) suggests. Indeed, if one asks about the age of the Greek version itself, the very fact that it is said to have been made under Ptolemy Philadelphus (about which there is great consensus among ancient writers) is not at all far from the truth. This is especially true since Aristeas, Aristobulus, Philo, and Josephus speak only of the Pentateuch <note>the first five books of the Bible</note>, which, because of its exceptional importance in worship, had to be translated before all other books ^5). Many things favor this view, especially the long-standing captivity of the Jews brought into Egypt by Ptolemy son of Lagus, and the remarkable zeal of the Ptolemies for propagating Greek literature: although it is more likely that the translation was made for the sake of serving the needs of these Jews rather than for the King's curiosity—

---

^1) This was first done in the dissertation against the history of Aristeas regarding the 70 translators, Oxford 1685; again in the four books on the original biblical texts, the Greek versions, and the Latin Vulgate, Oxford 1705. <note>Referring to Humphrey Hody</note>
^2) Among those who even today feel otherwise, a leader is found in Constantine Oiconomos, an Athenian, who recently published four volumes full of erudition and zeal under this title: *Four books concerning the 70 translators of the ancient divine scripture. Compiled by the presbyter and steward of the ecumenical patriarchal throne, Constantine Oiconomos. Athens, 1844* <note>original Greek: Περὶ τῶν ο' ἑρμηνευτῶν... Ἀθήνησιν. αωμδ'</note> et seqq. Indeed, the learned Englishman Grinfield, author of the book *An Apology for the Septuagint, London 1850*, seems hardly to yield to Oiconomos.
^3) Cf. above all others Montfaucon in those things which he placed before the *Hexapla* of Origen in the Preliminary Chapters, ch. III, sect. II, § VI ff.
^4) In his dissertation on Aristeas, Amsterdam 1705, pages 4-5, he sought to prove that this "Pseudo-Aristeas" appeared between the times of Philo and Josephus, but was entirely later than Philo. If the book of Aristeas had been fabricated at a time when all the sacred books had long been read in Greek, the author would not have been satisfied to say that only the Pentateuch was translated by those interpreters.
^5) Against the evidence of the text itself (see above), Oiconomos and others, such as Valckenaer and Haevernick, contend that the oldest tradition deals with the translation of the entire Old Testament.

# XV

-> PREFACE <-

that the version was undertaken. To this point, the prologue of the book of **Jesus son of Sirach** <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus</note> also carries some weight, where the translator, in the thirty-eighth year of **Ptolemy Euergetes** (that is, the year 131 before Christ), provides testimony of a Greek version of all the sacred books ^1); and indeed, he does so in such a way that he seems to present the matter not as something new, but as something very well known to everyone through actual use. Added to this testimony is the subscription <note>A concluding note or signature in a manuscript</note> to the additions to the book of **Esther**, shown in all our most ancient codices—including the **Frederico-Augustanus** <note>A portion of the Codex Sinaiticus discovered by Tischendorf</note>—in which that book is said to have been translated into Greek under **Ptolemy and Cleopatra**, which to most seems to refer to **Ptolemy Philometor** between 181 and 145 before Christ. Clearly, by this time, the canonical books of the Jews, together with their *hagiographa* <note>term: Hagiographa; the "Holy Writings," the third section of the Hebrew Bible including Psalms, Esther, etc.</note>, seem to have already been translated; for the book of Esther, as is established from **Josephus** and **Jerome**, not only held the very last place of all, but was also accustomed to be written and preserved separately from the volumes of the Law, the Prophets, and the *hagiographa*. Now indeed, if it must be maintained that around the beginning of the second century before Christ a Greek version of the sacred books was in use among the **Hellenists** <note>Greek-speaking Jews</note>, it is not inconsistent with belief that the beginning of that version was made with the **Pentateuch** <note>The first five books of the Bible</note> fifty or sixty years earlier.

VI. But while these things may be said with no small probability for conjecturing the age of the Greek version, there is a more difficult question concerning the true authors and the laws which they followed in translating. This, indeed, cannot be doubted: that not all the same men, but different people translated different books at various times ^2). For from this it has happened that individual books differ greatly among themselves in the quality of the translation and in their entire character and method; just as the five books of **Moses** ^3) were certainly, for the most part, much better [translated]...

***

1) This is most clear and certain; nor was there any reason for **Frankel** (*Preliminary Studies on the Septuagint*, page 22) to doubt whether the words of the prologue: <note>original Greek: "οὐ γὰρ ἰσοδυναμεῖ... ἐν ἑαυτοῖς λεγόμενα"</note> "For things spoken in themselves [in Hebrew] do not have the same force [when translated into another tongue]" were to be understood as referring to the Chaldean <note>Aramaic Targums</note> or the Greek version of the sacred books. For since the translator himself writes in Greek and speaks of his own Greek interpretation, what he says concerning the Law, the prophets, and the rest of the books cannot be understood otherwise than as referring to those translated into Greek and circulated in that same language. Similarly, the same learned Jewish master takes offense at the fact that "the prophecies" <note>original Greek: αἱ προφητεῖαι</note> was written instead of "the prophets" <note>οἱ προφῆται</note>. How poorly he distinguished these may be learned from **Justin**, who (see above, page XII, note 2) spoke in exactly the same way of "the books of the prophecies."

2) That there were several translators is clearly demonstrated by the fact that the same Hebrew words are not expressed in the same way in all books. See some examples of this matter in **Eichhorn** in his *Introduction to the Old Testament*, 1823, vol. I, page 467 ff. To this also belongs the difference in spelling with which proper names are read in various books: although we must be careful not to impute the inconsistency of the manuscripts to the translators themselves.

3) **Frankel** has recently and learnedly investigated the individual authors who seem to have translated the Pentateuch itself: *On the Influence of Palestinian Exegesis on Alexandrian Hermeneutics*, pages 4–227, with a critical review on page 228 ff.

<summary>This page discusses the historical dating of the Septuagint, citing the Prologue of Sirach and the Book of Esther as evidence for a 2nd-century BC Greek canon. It also argues that the translation was the work of multiple authors with varying styles rather than a single hand.</summary>

XVI

->PREFACE<-

than the prophets were rendered into Greek. However, we are far from knowing how many individual translators' efforts can be distinguished within our texts. It is established by suitable arguments that most of them were Alexandrian Jews <note>referring to the Jewish community in Alexandria, Egypt, which was a center of Greek scholarship</note> 1). It is also possible that the text, once first published, underwent a corrective hand to create a greater unity among the individual parts.

It is truly very difficult to say from where the translators drew those elements which, in their work, differ from the Hebrew text. There have been those who said the Greek flowed from a Chaldean <note>Aramaic</note> version or paraphrase rather than from the Hebrew text itself 2); to others, it seemed sufficient to say that the Hebrew text itself was marked with no vowel points and was therefore prone to error. Others have derived at least the Pentateuch <note>the first five books of the Bible</note> from a Samaritan codex, with which the Greek version actually agrees in nearly two thousand places; others, such as Gesenius 3), determined that both the Samaritans and the Greek translators drew the books of Moses from the same source; conversely, Frankel believes the Samaritan text was conformed to the Greek 4).

These theories, whether you adopt one or the other, are not of such a kind that they sufficiently explain the relationship between the Greek text and the Masoretic Hebrew <note>the traditional Hebrew text compiled by Jewish scholars between the 6th and 10th centuries</note>; for it is not enough to investigate the character of the manuscripts from which the translation flowed, but great importance is also placed on the hermeneutical laws <note>principles of interpretation</note> which the translators followed. All these matters are of such a nature that they require new and varied labors to reach clarity and actual probability; nor, in my opinion, is there any subject more worthy of the study, industry, and diligence of learned theologians—after the holy books of the New Covenant—than the Greek text of the Old Testament. For no one can doubt why it holds such great authority in sacred matters. It holds it because, often even when the Hebrew text itself is reluctant, it was applied with extraordinary weight to the writings of the holy apostles. That authority, which was already greatly [celebrated] by Philo and Josephus 5)...

---

1) Eichhorn (in the work cited above, vol. I, p. 472 ff.) set forth this matter following Hody (*On the Original Texts of the Bible*, p. 110 ff.), from whom he borrowed much. Frankel (in *Preliminary Studies*, etc., p. 40 ff.) attacked Hody's examples; nevertheless, he himself concedes that most of the books were translated by Alexandrian Jews, and claims only a part for the Asians. As for his wish in that passage that the title of the first book of Moses: *The Genesis of the World* <note>original: "γενεσις του κοσμου"</note>, was not placed there by the translators themselves, that stems from a desire to argue against certainties from uncertainties—a tendency which that most learned man indulges too much here and there. Moreover, in both of the small books we have repeatedly seen, he has probably explained many things that lead to a more correct understanding of the character of the whole translation and the method of the individual translators.
2) On this whole matter, one should consult Frankel: *Preliminary Studies*, etc., p. 32 ff.
3) Cf. his dissertation *On the Origin, Nature, and Authority of the Samaritan Pentateuch*. 1815.
4) Cf. *On the Influence of Palestinian Exegesis*, etc., p. 238 ff.
5) Philo constantly, and Josephus for the most part, used the Greek text, not the Hebrew, in their writings.

# PROLEGOMENA
<note>Latin: "Prolegomena" - Preliminary remarks or introduction.</note>

XVII
<b>b</b>
<meta>Catchword: SEPTUAGINTA.</meta>

once approved, it received a distinguished increase; just as it fostered the belief of many Fathers and that superstitious diligence regarding the ancient tradition of the translation's origin—which Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the scholar who translated the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> had already rejected—so it could not fail to be confirmed by the faith and practice of the Church itself. This matter is clearly evidenced by the translations of that same Greek text which appeared from the first centuries after Christ in both the eastern and western parts of the Christian world. For the Syrians and Armenians, Copts and Ethiopians, Arabs, Iberians <note>Referring here to Georgians from the Caucasus.</note>, and Slavs, just like the Latins, sought their Old Testament either solely or primarily from the celebrated Greek text. And although Jerome had substituted a new translation drawn from the Hebrew source in place of the text used by his countrymen (which he himself had previously corrected with great zeal), and while that new version gradually gained great authority in the Latin Church, it was not powerful enough to cause the Greek text to lose its ancient dignity or be driven out of common use.

VII. Hence it happened that, as soon as the new light of letters arose after the long darkness of the Middle Ages, not only did learned and pious men apply strenuous efforts to preparing editions of the Seventy interpreters <note>The Septuagint, often abbreviated as LXX.</note>, but the Roman Pontiffs themselves, acting for the good of the Christian world, took the care of publishing them upon themselves. For in the year of salvation 1587, the edition of Sixtus V appeared; even before he had ascended the apostolic throne, he had stood forth as the advisor and promoter for Gregory XIII to undertake the work. 1) This edition soon attained such fame that it was preferred everywhere to both the Venetian and the Complutensian <note>The Complutensian Polyglot (1514–1517), the first printed multilingual Bible.</note> editions, which had been made public more than sixty years earlier; nor was it later displaced from its preeminence by the Alexandrian Codex <note>Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century manuscript.</note> brought to light through the studies of Ernest Grabe. This preeminence it has held since then—

***

> 1) Pius V had already turned his mind to this same matter, although no mention of him is made either in the dedicatory letter of Antonio Carafa to Sixtus V, nor in the preface to the reader, nor in the Pope's decree. However, Joseph Silos bears witness to this in his *History of the Regular Clerics*, Part I, Book XIII, for the year 1575: "The holy Council of Trent <note>The ecumenical council (1545–1563) that defined Catholic doctrine in response to the Reformation.</note> took accurate care for this splendor of the Divine Scriptures, and according to its decrees, Pope Pius V attempted their correction; and since Agellius <note>Girolamo Agelli (1532–1608), a scholar of Greek and Oriental languages.</note> was then flourishing in Rome with distinguished praise for his erudition and literature in such sacred bibles, being especially equipped with skill in languages, these matters of the gravest importance could not be discussed without him being consulted among the few and applied to the work. Therefore, as he himself testifies in a letter given to Latinus Latinius, this business was entrusted to him along with the most learned men: Mariano Vittorio, Bishop of Rieti; Paulinus of the Dominican family; and Father Emmanuel Sa of the Society of Jesus." Lest these words seem to refer to the Latin bibles (to which the decrees of the Council of Trent certainly look) rather than the Greek, the things that follow—where the Seventy themselves are mentioned—forbid it. For the rest, compare below (section X, middle) what Antonio Carafa writes in the dedicatory letter concerning the acts of the Council of Trent not yet made public.

# XVIII
-> PREFACE <-

...because the Roman editors declared that they used the very ancient Vatican manuscript as the foundation of their edition, and indeed they largely did so, whereas both of the editions that preceded it had been shaped according to the reliability of later manuscripts—and not without arbitrary judgment <note>The author is criticizing earlier editors for making subjective changes to the biblical text rather than following the oldest sources.</note> 1); the **Grabe edition** 2), on the other hand, not only valued the Alexandrian manuscript more than was just...

---

1) The **Complutensian Edition** is found in the **Complutensian Polyglot**: **The Hebrew, Chaldean, Greek, and Latin Bible**, now printed for the first time in the University of Alcalá <note>original: "Complutensi universitate"</note> by the command and at the expense of **Francisco Jiménez de Cisneros** (Archbishop of Toledo) by the industry of **Arnaldo Guillén de Brocar**, 1514–1517, in 6 volumes. (But it was finally published only a short time after the year 1522.) There is a remarkable disagreement regarding this edition among editors and those who examined it later. For while the editors themselves boasted that they had printed from very ancient and highly corrected copies sent to Alcalá by **Leo X** from the Apostolic Library itself, and that they had employed many others, including the most refined manuscript of **Bessarion** <note>Basilios Bessarion (1403–1472), a Greek scholar and Cardinal whose manuscript collection was legendary.</note>: so **Walton**, in the **Prolegomena to the Polyglot Bible**, IX, 28, said it was inferior to all others and stood furthest of all from the genuine Septuagint <note>original: "LXX"</note> version; for he claimed it was a new and mixed version, patched together partly from the Septuagint, partly from Origen’s additions from Theodotion, and partly from the words of Aquila, Symmachus, and other translators, and even from Greek commentators, so that in this way it might better correspond to the Hebrew text across the columns. Thus **Walton** judged, and many others have judged similarly. This reproach seems to be more unfair than is just; for certainly the vast majority of what was published at Alcalá is confirmed by manuscripts. Nevertheless, because of the doubtful reliability of that edition, it is much better to test the Complutensian readings against the manuscripts themselves than to grant them a manufactured authority. Nonetheless, two centuries ago, the Complutensian text was reprinted several times: at Antwerp (by the study of **Livineius** and **Canter**, with the Latin by **Arias Montano**) in the **Royal Polyglot Bibles**, 1572; from the Sanctandreana office in the Polyglot usually called **Vatablus's**, 1586 (the same edition is said to have appeared in other copies in 1587, and in others in 1599 at Heidelberg, likewise in 1616 from the Commeliniana office. Regarding this matter, see **Andreas Gottlieb Masch's Sacred Library**, part I, chapter III, section VIII); at Hamburg in 1596 in **Wolder’s Polyglot Bible** (**Masch** noted in the **Sacred Library**, part II, vol. II, sect. I, § LII, against **Fabricius**, that this text was not shaped according to the Aldine edition); at Paris in the Polyglot (chiefly by the author **D. Michael de Jay**) 1645. — As for the one who very recently made use of the Complutensian edition in the polyglot edition of **Theile** and **Stier** (namely **Boeckel**), I judge him to be entirely foreign to sound critical reasoning.
The **Venetian or Aldine Edition**, in three volumes which comprise both the Old and New Testaments in Greek, came from the house of **Aldus** and his father-in-law **Asulanus** in the year 1518. Although this was long said to be purer than the Complutensian and closer to the Roman edition, it too deviated in various and serious ways from the truth, as **Ussher** <note>James Ussher (1581–1656), Archbishop of Armagh, famous for his biblical chronology.</note> has already noted in these words:

> "I have observed that it sometimes follows not the Septuagint <note>original: "LXX"</note> but the 
> "readings of Aquila; many glosses <note>Explanatory notes originally written in margins that later slipped into the main text.</note> also, first noted in the margins of 
> "manuscripts and later admitted into the text, occur here, and these are 
> "sought not only from various editions and versions, but even in places 
> "which the apostles cited that differ from the common Greek reading of the Seventy <note>original: "τῶν ο'" (the 70).</note>."

This edition also (further deformed by very many typographical errors) is of doubtful reliability; for it is not sufficiently clear from which manuscripts it flowed, nor with how much diligence or religious care the editors followed the authority of the manuscript books. The Aldine text was reprinted at Strasbourg in 1526 by **Wolfgang Köpfel** <note>original: "Wolph. Cephaleo"</note> (with a new title at Strasbourg and, as is elsewhere, at Basel, 1529); at Basel in 1545 by **Johann Herwagen**, and in the same place in 1550 by **Nicolaus Brylinger** (issued again in 1582); at Frankfurt am Main by the Wechelian press in 1597 (again at Venice in 1687).

2) Regarding the **Grabe** edition, and likewise the Zurich edition of **Breitinger**, see below under the Alexandrian manuscript.

<summary>This page provides a critical historical overview of the earliest printed editions of the Greek Old Testament (Septuagint), specifically the Complutensian and Aldine editions, noting their textual inaccuracies and reliance on later manuscripts compared to the Roman edition.</summary>
<keywords>Septuagint, Complutensian Polyglot, Aldine Edition, Codex Vaticanus, Francisco Jiménez de Cisneros, James Ussher, textual criticism, biblical manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's preface (Prolegomena), specifically discussing the rationale behind selecting the base text for this new edition of the Greek Old Testament.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA.

but it would also fail to properly align with the likeness of Origen’s *Hexapla* edition <note>The *Hexapla* was a massive six-columned comparative Bible compiled by the Church Father Origen in the 3rd century.</note>. Nevertheless, among learned men who possess a deep knowledge of the sacred texts, it is well established that each of these three editions has its own virtues—especially the Alexandrian—while the Vatican edition itself is by no means perfect in every respect.

VIII. Since this was the case, what was to be done when planning a new edition recently? If we possessed a critical apparatus <term>critical apparatus: a system of notes at the bottom of a page documenting variations in different ancient manuscripts</term> of such a kind that it both embraced many of the oldest witnesses and reported the variety of readings fully and accurately, the arduous task of revising the text ought to have been undertaken; for it certainly could have been. We are so far from having such a resource that one must not even begin from the famous work of Holmes <note>Robert Holmes (1748–1805), who began a massive collation of Septuagint manuscripts</note> if one wishes to construct such an apparatus. Indeed, it would have been a matter of no small rashness to prepare a new revision of the Septuagint text <note>original: "Septuagintaviralis"</note> while knowing well how imperfect were the supports available for a "perfect" revision. Furthermore, I was obliged to refrain from revising the text even more now because the edition I was planning was intended for common use, not for the learned curiosity of a few men. However, if I could contribute anything by a new manual edition to cautiously but truly advance critical study, I deemed it should not be refused. Therefore, I have entered upon this path: to reproduce the text most approved through these last three centuries, changing only those things which most needed change, while adding a full variety of readings from the three oldest manuscripts, which were almost the only ones that could be used confidently since they had been published.<sup>1)</sup> For in this way, I seemed to ensure that things in the Vatican text which were incorrect or poor would not be sold as true or certain, nor would anything be changed rashly or one doubt be merely replaced by another.

But I must speak more explicitly regarding both the laws we followed in reproducing the Roman text and which passages we have provided in a changed form, as well as which manuscripts we used for the apparatus and in what manner. In this place, an explanation will also be given concerning the entire critical apparatus used by the Greek Old Testament, and supplements to our apparatus will be brought forward from certain very important manuscripts. Before all these things, however, it seems necessary to report a little more fully on the Vatican edition itself.

IX. The Roman edition has this title: *The Old Testament according to the Seventy, published by the authority of Sixtus V, Supreme Pontiff.* <note>original Greek: η παλαια διαθηκη κατα τους εβδομηκοντα δι αυθεντιας ξυστου ε' ακρου αρχιερεως εκδοθεισα</note> The Old Testament according to the Septuagint—

---

1) This applies especially to the year 1850, when this stereotyped edition first appeared. Regarding the remarkable fragments of the most ancient text which I myself discovered very recently in the East, see below. Supplements to our apparatus will also be brought from there.

<summary>The editor explains his decision to use the established Roman (Vatican) text as a base while correcting it against the oldest manuscripts, rather than attempting a completely new and potentially flawed revision. He emphasizes that this edition is intended for general use rather than just for a small circle of specialists.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory prolegomena, specifically describing the physical characteristics and scholarly apparatus of the Sixtine edition of the Septuagint.</meta>

# PREFACE

...issued by the authority of Pope Sixtus V. The book is of the largest format <note>original: "formae maximae," likely a large folio</note>, consisting of 783 pages, with two additional pages for additions and corrections, which we shall discuss below. On every page, the text is divided into two columns, each containing approximately 55 lines. The division of chapters is observed, but not that of verses. Within the text itself, neither proper names nor any other words, nor the beginnings of sentences, are marked with a capital or initial letter. At the end of a chapter, notes are usually added <sup>1</sup>), in which the readings of Aquila, Symmachus, Theodotion, the so-called Fifth and Sixth editions, and other Church Fathers, interpretations, or manuscripts are cited; likewise, more obscure passages are explained. Thus, under the first chapter of Genesis, the following are read:

α [And the earth was invisible and unfinished] <note>original Greek: "Ἡ δὲ γῆ ἦν ἀόρατος καὶ ἀκατασκεύαστος"</note> Aquila: emptiness and nothing. Symmachus: idle and indistinguishable. Theodotion: empty and nothing. β [Let the water be gathered] <note>original Greek: "Συναχθήτω τὸ ὕδωρ"</note> Aquila and Symmachus: let it be established. γ [Sowing seed] <note>original Greek: "Σπεῖρον σπέρμα"</note> Aquila: seeding according to their kind. δ [For light upon the earth] <note>original Greek: "Εἰς φαῦσιν ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς"</note> Other manuscripts: for the light of the earth.

Likewise, under Exodus chapter VIII: α [And I will send them forth] <note>original Greek: "Καὶ ἐξαποστελῶ αὐτούς"</note> Other manuscripts: the people. β [Gnats] <note>original Greek: "Σκνίφες"</note> So the various readings and Saint Augustine. Other manuscripts: *sknipes* <note>a spelling variation</note>; which writing is found in Origen and in Philo, book 1 of *The Life of Moses*: "a swarm of gnats was poured out." Scholium <note>marginal or explanatory note</note> from another source: lice. γ [Dog-fly] <note>original Greek: "Κυνόμυιαν"</note> Scholium: the Samaritan version: a raven. However, that it should not be read as "common-fly" <note>original Greek: "κοινόμυιαν"</note> but "dog-fly" can be understood from the etymology which Philo explains in book 1 of *The Life of Moses*. δ [Do not continue, Pharaoh, to deceive] <note>original Greek: "Μὴ προσθῇς ἔτι φαραὼ ἐξαπατῆσαι"</note> Aquila and Symmachus: to deal falsely.

Likewise, under Psalm III: α [A Psalm.] <note>original Greek: "Ψαλμός"</note> Scholium: a song, and similarly throughout the whole book. β [Musical pause] <note>original Greek: "διάψαλμα" (selah)</note> Scholium: Theodotion and Symmachus: a musical pause. But the Fifth Edition: forever. And they have translated it similarly as "forever" throughout the whole book for the musical pause. This matter was discussed by Saint Jerome in his letter to Marcella. γ [From his holy mountain.] <note>original Greek: "ἐξ ὄρους ἁγίου αὐτοῦ"</note> Aquila: from his sanctified [mountain]. Symmachus: from his own holy [mountain]. δ [He will sustain me] <note>original Greek: "ἀντιλήψεταί μου"</note> Justin reads: he sustained me. And it is thus in the Chaldean paraphrase <note>the Targum</note>, the Ethiopic Psalter, and the Arabic. Likewise in the Latin translations: he received me.

---

<note>Footnote at bottom of page</note>

1) **Pierre Morin** <note>original: "Petrus Morinus"</note> professes that he contributed especially to these notes, writing the following in a letter to Silvio Antoniano: *To me alone was given the province of unrolling and thoroughly reading the Vatican commentaries on the Old Testament, which are called "chains" <note>term: catenae - biblical commentaries made of a "chain" of quotes from Church Fathers</note>, so that I might extract from them the various readings and various interpretations of Aquila, Symmachus, and Theodotion, as well as the Fifth and Sixth editions, and transfer them into the annotations which I had likewise undertaken to write. I exhausted that immense labor of reading so many things over several years and corrected many things based on conjecture from the Hebrew language.* Concerning the others who had a part in preparing the edition, see below on page 24, note 1.

# PROLEGOMENA

The following items are placed before the actual text:
1. The order of the books of the Old Testament in this edition. <gloss>1)</gloss> <note>original: "τάξις τῶν τῆς παλαιᾶς διαθήκης βιβλίων ἐν τῇδε τῇ ἐκδόσει"</note>

3. A preface to the reader.
4. The decree of Pope Sixtus V concerning this edition.
X. In that letter placed in the second position, Antonio Carafa writes the following to Pope Sixtus V:

It is now nearly the eighth year since Your Holiness, out of your singular zeal for serving sacred literature, urged Pope Gregory XIII of blessed memory to ensure that the most holy Bible of the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note>—which both the Greek and Latin Churches have used since the times of the Apostles—be corrected according to the faith of the most approved manuscripts. For Your Holiness, through your careful diligence in reading the divine Scriptures, had noticed that almost infinite passages were not cited by ancient sacred writers in the same way they were circulated in the common Greek editions of the Bible. You judged that this variety of readings flowed from no other source than the multiple and confused ancient interpretations. You most rightly decided that one must appeal to copies of the highest quality, from which, as far as possible, the true and sincere writing of the Seventy Interpreters might be drawn out. Because of this, I greatly admire not only your piety but also your wisdom; for I see that the same idea for polishing the Greek Bible occurred to Your Holiness many years later that I discovered those holy Fathers gathered at Trent <note>The Council of Trent (1545–1563)</note>—led by authority and reverence for the true and pure interpretation of the Seventy—once considered, as I learned from the acts of that Council not yet made public. When the task of organizing this refinement was entrusted to me by Gregory XIII (whose thoughts were chiefly aimed at the widest possible propagation of the Christian religion), I took care that the best copies were sought out in the more famous libraries of Italy, and that the variations of readings transcribed from them were sent to me. These were examined very often by the industry and judgment of the learned men I had chosen for this task, and by the counsel of Cardinal Guglielmo Sirleto of illustrious memory (whom I had proposed to consult on more obscure passages because of his excellent learning and skill in multiple languages), and compared with your Vatican library manuscript (to which your kindness [appointed] me...

# PREFACE.

<page-num>XXII</page-num>

(recently appointed) having been diligently compared with the copy; we understood both from the comparison itself and from the consensus of the ancient holy writers, that the Vatican codex <note>The Codex Vaticanus, one of the oldest extant manuscripts of the Greek Bible</note> surpasses the others not only in age but also in quality; and what is most important, it comes closest—if not in the entire book, then certainly for the greater part—to that very **Septuagint** <note>The Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, traditionally attributed to seventy-two Jewish scholars</note> interpretation which we were seeking. Since this was evident to me by many other arguments, and even by the title of the book itself, which is *According to the Seventy* <note>original: "κατὰ τοὺς ἑβδομήκοντα"</note>, I took care, by the advice and opinion of those whom I named above, that the edition of this book be emended according to the Vatican copy; or rather, because its authority was so highly approved, that the copy itself be represented word for word, having first been accurately revised where necessary and even augmented with notations. It has happened, surely by divine providence, that what was begun at your Holiness's persuasion during the time of your Cardinalate, though interrupted several times for various reasons, was completed almost at the very beginning of your Pontificate; namely, so that this illustrious work, dedicated to your Most Holy Name, might be a kind of perpetual monument among all good men, both of your goodwill toward the Christian Commonwealth and of my respect toward your Holiness.

XI. Indeed, the Preface to the reader, which it is well known that *Pierre Morin* <note>A noted French biblical scholar and member of the commission for the Sixtine Septuagint</note> wrote, begins as follows:

All those who are more accurately versed in the most holy scriptures confess that the Greek edition of the **Septuagint** Interpreters is by far older and more approved than all others used by the Greeks. For it is well known that those Interpreters—Jews by nation, but learned in Greek—three hundred and one years before the coming of Christ, when *Ptolemy Philadelphus* was reigning in Egypt, being full of the Holy Spirit, translated the Holy Bible; and that this interpretation, from the first moments of the nascent Church, was both publicly set forth in the Churches for reading, and privately received and explained by the Ecclesiastical writers who lived before *Blessed Jerome*, the author of the Latin Vulgate edition. For *Aquila of Sinope*, who was the second after the Seventy to convert those same books from Hebrew into Greek and flourished much later under the Emperor *Hadrian*, his interpretation has long since been disapproved of in some places by right-thinking men (even though it was contained in the *Hexapla* <note>A six-columned edition of the Old Testament compiled by Origen</note>), because he enveloped those things which had been predicted in the scriptures concerning Christ in a deceitful obscurity, translating differently than the Seventy in order to find favor with the Jews. Those who followed him, *Symmachus* and *Theodotion*—the one a Samaritan under *Lucius Verus*, the other an Ephesian under the Emperor *Commodus*—both were considered untrustworthy interpreters (although they too were circulated in the *Hexapla*). *Symmachus*, because having been offended by the Samaritans, in order to please the Jews, he corrupted more than one passage of holy scripture by disturbing the sense;

<summary>This page justifies the use of the Codex Vaticanus for the Septuagint edition and begins a historical overview by Pierre Morin regarding the superiority of the Septuagint over later Greek translations by Aquila, Symmachus, and Theodotion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory prolegomena, discussing the history and corruption of various Greek biblical translations prior to the Sixtine edition.</meta>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSE

Theodotio <note>A 2nd-century translator of the Hebrew Bible into Greek</note>, because as a follower of the heretic Marcion, he distorted rather than translated the sacred books in several places. Besides these, there were two other editions of uncertain authority among the Greeks: one found at Jericho during the reign of Emperor Antoninus Caracalla, and the other found in jars at Nicopolis under Alexander Severus. Because these occupied the fifth and sixth places among the Greek editions in the *Octapla* <note>An eight-columned polyglot Bible</note>, they retained the names of the "Fifth" and "Sixth" editions. But even these were not considered sufficiently faithful translations. To these is added another edition by Saint Lucian the Martyr, who lived under the Emperors Diocletian and Maximian; while it was indeed highly approved, it can in no way be compared with the Seventy Translators <note>The Septuagint</note>, as even the Greek writers themselves testify and Nicetas confirms in these very words in his commentary on the Psalms: <note>original Greek: ἡμεῖς δὲ καὶ τὴν τοιαύτην ἔκδοσιν σεβαζόμενοι, τῇ τῶν ἑβδομήκοντα προσκείμεθα μάλιστα, ὅτι διῃρημένως τὴν τῆς διαλέκτου μεταβολὴν ποιησάμενοι μίαν ἐν ἑκάστοις ἔννοιαν καὶ λέξιν ἀποδεδώκασιν.</note> "While we respect such an edition, we adhere most closely to that of the Seventy, because, having performed the change of language separately, they rendered one and the same meaning and wording in every instance."

To such an extent was the edition of the Seventy Translators held in high renown among all; for it surely came to light for the good of the human race, having been labored upon by a certain divine inspiration. But even this version reached us in an impure state and very much unlike itself. This happened because Origen first arranged it in the *Hexapla* <note>A six-columned Bible comparing different versions</note> so that other editions were placed alongside it for easier comparison; then, variations from those other versions began to be noted within the Septuagint text using <term>obelisks and asterisks</term> <note>Marks used by ancient editors to indicate suspected or added text</note>. As these marks were erased by age, the text became corrupted. With the interpretations of others inserted everywhere, and in some places double or even triple interpretations of the same sentence thrust in—and being poorly handled by copyists besides—it lost its brilliance and integrity. From this arose those varieties of readings that completely disagree with one another and the discrepancies of the manuscripts, not only among themselves but also from the ancient writers, which has long tormented the minds and wits of the most learned men. This evil, at first ignored by many and later neglected by others, creeping further every day, defiled with no light stains the foremost Book, upon which the whole Divine Law and Christian institutions depend.

On this account, it cannot be said how much all good men owe to Pope Sixtus V. For because he spent almost his entire life in the sacred scriptures, from which he drew his most holy doctrine, and because he applied a singular diligence in comparing this book with the writings of the ancients, he was the first to see by what method this evil should be cured. He not only saw it, but by his authority, he brought it about that the Supreme Pontiff Gregory XIII took care to restore the Greek Bible of the Seventy Translators to its original splendor, employing diligent correction. This task...

<summary>This page outlines the historical corruption of the Septuagint through the inclusion of inferior translations and editorial marks, eventually leading to the restorative efforts commissioned by Popes Gregory XIII and Sixtus V.</summary>
<keywords>Septuagint, Theodotio, Origen, Hexapla, Lucian the Martyr, Pope Sixtus V, Pope Gregory XIII, biblical textual criticism, Greek Bible</keywords>
