<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion regarding the history of the Roman edition of the Septuagint, specifically focusing on the commission established under Cardinal Antonio Carafa.</meta>

# PREFACE

...when he had entrusted a certain task to Antonio Carafa, a Cardinal and a man of ancient holiness and a cultivator of all honorable arts, the latter, without any delay, made a selection of the most learned men 1) who, on fixed days at his own home, were to compare the manuscript copies which he had sought out in great numbers from everywhere, and from them elicit all the best readings. When these were then frequently and diligently compared with the codex of the Vatican library, it was understood that this codex was by far the best of all those in existence, and that it would be worth the effort if this new edition were prepared according to its authority.

But now that the plan for the correction has been explained, the actual method used in the correcting must now be disclosed, and first of all, the Vatican book must be described, according to whose prescription this edition has been polished. That codex, as far as can be conjectured from the shape of the characters—since it is written in the larger letters which they call truly ancient—seems to have been written before the twelve-hundredth year <note>The author likely means 1200 years before his own time, placing it in the 4th century</note>, that is, before or not much later than the times of Blessed Jerome 2). Moreover, out of all the books that were in hand, this one alone helped the established correction in a wonderful way above the others, because it was seen to consist of the Septuagint <note>The Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> version, if not in the whole book, certainly for the greater part; after it, however, two others which...

<column-break/>

1) In elaborating the Roman edition, under the presidency of Cardinal Antonio Carafa, those who primarily applied their studies—already under Pius V, who first undertook the matter—besides Guglielmo Sirleto (whom Carafa mentioned in the dedication of the book itself), were Antonio Agelli, Latino Latini, Mariano Vittorio, Paulinus the Dominican, Emmanuel Sa, and Pedro Parra; then under Gregory XIII, there were added, besides Pierre Morin (whom we have already seen testifying himself concerning his labor), Pedro Chacón (occupied chiefly with Latin books), Francisco Torres, Paolo Comitoli, Fulvio Orsini, Lelio the theologian of Carafa, Juan Maldonado, Jean de Livineus, Bartolomé Valverde, Robert Bellarmine, and Francisco Toledo.

2) From the time these things were published in Rome, many things have been disputed by many concerning the age of the Vatican book, although only those can know anything in these matters who have themselves seen as many books of the highest antiquity as possible and have observed with all diligence whatever factors serve to define their age. The argument for greatest antiquity which the Roman editors took from the letters of the Vatican codex is not sufficient in itself, even if it truly uses a perfect type of uncial letters <note>term: uncial letters (large, rounded capital letters used in Greek and Latin manuscripts from the 4th to 8th centuries)</note> hardly different from some of the Herculaneum writings; but more points are added by which their opinion, by which that codex is assigned to the fourth century, is greatly confirmed. Of such a kind is the simplicity and extreme rarity of punctuation, likewise that the writing does not yet have what they call initial letters larger than the rest. Other arguments are taken chiefly from the New Testament. Concerning all of which I have recently set forth in the *Heidelberg Theological Studies and Critiques* 1847, Vol. I, page 429 ff. and in the *Prolegomena* of my *Codex Frederico-Augustanus* 1846. The same opinion was also defended by Hug in the program "On the Antiquity of the Vatican Codex," Freiburg im Breisgau, 1810. He had already corrected the serious errors of Birch regarding this codex.

<summary>This page details the scholarly commission led by Cardinal Carafa to produce the Roman edition of the Septuagint, identifying the Vatican Codex (Codex Vaticanus) as the primary and most ancient source. It includes a list of the prominent Renaissance scholars involved and a paleographic defense of the manuscript's 4th-century origin.</summary>

<keywords>Antonio Carafa, Codex Vaticanus, Septuagint, manuscript studies, paleography, uncial letters, Roman Edition, biblical criticism, 4th century manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical introduction (Prolegomena) regarding the primary manuscript sources used for this edition of the Greek Old Testament.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA.

Approaching the antiquity of this manuscript—though following it at a great distance—are two others: one is the Venetian codex from the library of Cardinal Bessarion <note>Johannes Bessarion (1403–1472), a Greek scholar and Cardinal who contributed greatly to the revival of letters</note>, which is also written in uncials <note>original: "grandioribus litteris," referring to large, majuscule script</note> ¹); the other was brought from Southern Italy <note>original: "Magna Graecia"</note> and now belongs to Cardinal Carafa ²) <note>Antonio Carafa (1538–1591), head of the Vatican printing press</note>. This latter book agrees so entirely with the Vatican codex in all respects that it may be believed to have been copied from the same original model <note>term: "archetypo" (archetype) - the common ancestor from which manuscripts are derived</note>. Besides these, books collated from the Medici library in Florence were of great use, as they either confirmed or clarified the Vatican readings in many places. However, the excellence of the Vatican book is perceived not so much from the remarkable agreement of these codices, as from those passages which are partly cited and partly explained by the ancient sacred writers. These authors almost never fail to exhibit and restore the readings of this copy, except where they cite a passage from other translators rather than the Seventy <note>The Septuagint (LXX)</note>. Since their edition was to be polished with a new correction, it was rightly refined according to the standard of this book, which is by far the oldest of all and is the only one ³) inscribed "according to the Seventy"; or rather, most correctly, the book itself was expressed literally, as far as was possible despite the ancient orthography or the slips of the scribe. For that ancient and now obsolete spelling of that age was not represented in some places; yet in all others, unless a manifest slip of the scribe appeared, there was not a departure of even a "breadth of a fingernail," as they say, from the authority of this book ⁴), not even in those matters which, if not clearly faulty, certainly seemed not to lack the suspicion of error.

---

1) In the manuscript library of St. Mark <note>original: "Marciana"</note> (Bassano 1802), Jacopo Morelli lists this as Codex I, which he described in volume I, pages 3–6. It contains the book of Job from chapter 30, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, Song of Solomon, Wisdom of Solomon, Sirach, all the Prophets, Tobit, Judith, and three books of Maccabees. I examined it myself in the year 1843 and transcribed several things from it worthy of publication. Morelli, with others, thought it was written in the ninth century; I do not know if the eighth might be more likely. Holmes and Parsons <note>Editors of a famous 18th-century edition of the Septuagint</note> produced variant readings extracted from it in the apparatus of the Oxford edition; since they realized too late that it was written in uncial letters, it happened by error that they designated the codex as number 23. Furthermore, this codex seems to have been especially valuable for publishing the books of Maccabees, since those are entirely missing in the Vatican codex itself.

2) A catalog of the manuscript books of Cardinal Carafa is kept in the Vatican library. Cf. Blume in *Itineris Italici*, volume III. From this, it is probable that the manuscript books themselves passed from the hands of Carafa into that library. It is credible that this codex was used primarily for publishing Genesis, the far greater part of which is known to have perished in the Vatican codex itself. Indeed, a part of the Psalms is also missing: if the Carafa book provided this along with Genesis, it must be believed to have contained the entire Old Testament.

3) The subscription <note>A signature or concluding note at the end of a text</note> of the Proverbs of Solomon in the "Codex of Ephraem the Syrian Rescript" <note>term: "rescripti" (palimpsest) - a manuscript where the original text was scraped off and written over</note>, which was unearthed by us, is quite remarkable: *The Proverbs of Solomon according to the Seventy* <note>original Greek: "παροιμιαι σολομωντος παρα εβδομηκοντα"</note>. For in this way, the Codex of Ephraem the Syrian has become a sharer in that same praise which the Roman editors attributed to the Vatican codex alone.

4) With what right Morinus <note>Jean Morin (1591–1659), a French theologian and scholar of the Septuagint</note> says this will be proved below, where variant readings from the Vatican codex itself will be presented.

<summary>This page discusses the secondary manuscript sources used to supplement the Vatican Codex, specifically the Venetian and Carafa codices, and justifies the editors' strict adherence to the Vatican's ancient text.</summary>
<keywords>Septuagint, Vatican Codex, Cardinal Bessarion, Cardinal Carafa, Codex Ephraemi Rescriptus, manuscript collation, uncial script, biblical scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface (Prolegomena) regarding the editorial principles used for the Vatican Codex edition, specifically addressing textual integrity and the order of the biblical books.</meta>

# PREFACE

It seemed better to leave passages that are in any way suspect (for it is impossible that some blemish would not remain even in a thoroughly purged copy) exactly as they are found in the original <term>archetype</term><note>The primary manuscript from which this edition is derived.</note>, rather than to have them corrected based on anyone’s personal wit or conjecture. This is because many things in this codex which at first seemed either faulty or mutilated were later found to be true and genuine when compared with other books. For in the books of the Prophets—which in this copy (with the exception of Daniel alone) most closely reflect the pure <term>Septuagint</term><note>The ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> edition—it is remarkable how many things are not present; yet it was understood that these are rightly absent and do not belong to those Translators, both from the Greek and Latin commentaries of ancient writers and from manuscript books in which those items were added under <term>asterisks</term><note>Symbols used by ancient editors like Origen to mark text present in the Hebrew but missing in the original Greek Septuagint.</note>.

And this same reasoning was preserved in the notations as well. Since many things in them are sought from Greek commentaries—which circulate in manuscript codices as partly mutilated or variously written in certain places—these have been transcribed exactly as they are found in the original copies, so that they might be restored by each person's judgment with the help of other books. Nor should this be omitted, which likewise pertains to the notations: not everything has been represented in them that could have been brought forward from common writers to confirm the Vatican readings, or from the editions of others to fill in what is missing in the Septuagint. Since these may be read in common books, anyone can provide them for themselves from there without trouble. However, those things found in manuscript books which seemed to pertain either to indicating the varieties of ancient readings and interpretations (sometimes reported under the name of a <term>scholium</term><note>An explanatory note or comment inserted into the margin of a manuscript.</note> because their authority was uncertain) or to stabilizing the Vatican text and illustrating its more obscure passages—those things certainly have not been passed over.

The order of the books in the Vatican copy, while almost the same as that which circulates among the Greeks, nevertheless varies from the common editions in this: it first contains the twelve Prophets, and these themselves are arranged differently; then the remaining four, just as they are commonly published. And we understand this order to be the true one from the fact that the ancient Ecclesiastical writers recognize and approve it. And although there is no division of chapters in the entire copy (for in the new edition, the convenience of readers has been consulted), nevertheless in the book of the four Prophets a certain faint distinction appears, almost similar to that described by Saint Dorotheus the martyr, who lived under Constantine the Great.

The books of the Maccabees are absent from this copy, and likewise almost the entire book of Genesis; for the parchment having been consumed by long age, it is mutilated from the beginning of the book up to chapter 47.

<summary>The editors explain their decision to favor manuscript accuracy over speculative correction and describe the unique book order and physical condition of the Vatican Codex, noting the loss of most of Genesis.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, Septuagint, Codex Vaticanus, Prophets, Dorotheus the martyr, Constantine the Great, book order, manuscript lacunae</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory remarks (Prolegomena) regarding the restoration of the Septuagint text, specifically addressing the sources used to fill gaps in the Vatican manuscript and the editorial philosophy of the project.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA.

and likewise the Book of Psalms, which from Psalm 105 to 138 is mutilated due to excessive age. However, these parts have been corrected through the collation <note>Collation is the process of comparing different manuscripts to identify variations or errors.</note> of other codices 4).

But if some things in this edition seem, as Saint Jerome says, either mangled or inverted—because they were not supplied and marked by Origen under obelisks and asterisks <note>Origen (c. 184–253 AD) used these symbols in his Hexapla to indicate text present in the Greek but missing in the Hebrew, or vice versa.</note>; or if they seem obscure and disordered because they do not agree with the Latin Vulgate, and are found more clearly and expressly in some other editions; you, the reader, must be advised that the labor of this refinement did not aim to create an edition composed of mixed interpretations from those named above (similar to the one Saint Jerome writes was called the *Koine* <note>original: "κοινήν"</note> by the Greeks and the "Common" by our people) that would correspond word-for-word to the Latin Vulgate edition—that is, to the Hebrew. Rather, the goal was to approach as closely as possible, through the use of ancient books, that version which the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> published following the authority of the Holy Spirit. No one who accurately compares this version with the Vulgate will doubt that this edition—now clarified by new emendations and increased by the surviving fragments of other translators—will be of no small benefit for understanding the Latin Vulgate.

If these efforts are approved by learned and piously minded men, as is right, it remains for them to give thanks to Pope Sixtus V, the author of this great good, and to ask of Almighty God in public prayers that He preserve this excellent Prince for us in prosperity for as long as possible. Since he has devoted all his cares and thoughts to amplifying and adorning the dignity of the Church, there is no doubt that the Christian Republic <note>The "Respublica Christiana," referring to the collective body of Christian nations and people.</note>—reformed by him through the best laws and most holy institutions, and restored to its splendor in religion and piety by the revival of ancient rites—will also be aided by him in this public cause: that the ancient sacred books, corrupted by human negligence or wickedness, might be vindicated from every stain by his extraordinary benevolence and published in the most corrected form possible.

**XII.** Finally, the decree of Pope Sixtus V is added, given at Rome on the 8th day of October, 1586, the first parts of which read as follows:

> For a perpetual memorial of the matter. Desiring, as much as lies within us, to provide for the salvation of the flock committed to us by every means and way, we strongly judge it to pertain to our pastoral care that the books of Holy Scripture, which contain the doctrine of salvation, be published whole and pure, purged of all stains. This we performed to the best of our ability through our zeal and diligence while we were established in a lower rank <note>Referring to his time as a Cardinal before being elected Pope.</note>,

---

4) See above, page 25, note 2. However, it was not stated in those exact words that the gaps in the Vatican manuscript were filled from those specific codices.

# PREFACE

<page-num>XXVIII</page-num>

->**PROLEGOMENA**<-

...and placed by God upon this highest watchtower, we do not cease to gaze assiduously with the eyes of our mind. Therefore, when in previous years Pope Gregory XIII <note>Pope Gregory XIII (reigned 1572–1585), best known for the Gregorian Calendar.</note> of pious memory, our predecessor, at our suggestion, ordered the Greek Old Testament according to the edition of the **Seventy Interpreters** <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>—which the Apostles themselves sometimes used—to be polished according to the faith of the most corrected manuscripts, the care of this matter was entrusted to our beloved son Antonio Carafa, Cardinal Priest of the Holy Roman Church, and to several learned men chosen by him for that purpose. And now that this polishing is complete—having diligently compared and maturely examined many copies from various libraries of Italy and especially from our own **Vatican** library—we will and decree, for the glory of God and the utility of the Church, that the Greek Old Testament according to the Seventy, thus recognized and polished, be received and retained by all, so that they may use it especially for the understanding of the Latin Vulgate edition and the ancient Holy Fathers.

XIII. At the end of the book, two pages are presented: *Additions to the notations* and *Things to be noticed*; likewise *Corrections to the notations of the Psalter* and several other places. These latter pertain not only to the notes but also to the text; however, they have been for the most part overlooked by those who later published the Vatican text, such as by Reineccius, by Holmes and Parsons, by Leander van Ess, by Jager, and even by Breitinger in his index of Vatican readings. The more important of these are: 4 Kings 4:31 <note>original: "Βασ. δ' 4, 31"</note>, it is said that *placed upon* <note>original: "ἐπέθηκε"</note> should be read instead of *put away* <note>original: "ἀπέθηκε"</note>; likewise Psalm 94:8, *of temptation* <note>original: "πειρασμοῦ"</note> instead of *of bitterness* <note>original: "πικρασμοῦ"</note>; Jeremiah 22:19, *he shall be buried* (singular) <note>original: "ταφήσεται"</note> instead of *they shall be buried* (plural) <note>original: "ταφήσοται"</note>; Daniel 8:11, *was broken* <note>original: "ἐράχθη"</note> instead of *was disturbed* <note>original: "ἐταράχθη"</note>, placing *was disturbed* (which is also in the **Alexandrian manuscript**) in the variant readings. Added to these are two corrections of the Psalms which most have accepted; for in Psalm 118:173, it is written that *I have chosen* <note>original: "ἡρετισάμην"</note> should be read instead of *I have chosen* (with a different spelling) <note>original: "ἑρετισάμην"</note> and in 143:12, *established* <note>original: "ἱδρυμένα"</note> instead of *sweating/moist* <note>original: "ἱδρουμένα"</note>.

Furthermore, other emendations are also found in what seem to be all copies of the first edition. For there are places in the text itself corrected by the hand of the editors with a pen. Nor did those who took care to repeat the Vatican text observe these more diligently. We have indicated this here and there in the apparatus <note>The "critical apparatus," the set of notes at the bottom of a page explaining textual variations.</note>, noting that something was corrected by pen in the Roman edition, as in volume II, page 60, Psalm 46:5; volume II, page 234, Micah 7:16; volume II, page 241, Jonah 4:8; volume II, page 264, Malachi 2:17. But we shall look more accurately at all these corrections later.

XIV. Now it must be stated in what ways we have departed from the Vatican edition itself in reproducing the Vatican text. And first of all, the punctuation <term>interpunctio: the system of pauses and stops in a text</term> throughout the entire book seemed to demand a corrective hand. For the whole system is of such a kind that you might call it as obsolete as it is inconvenient for readers.

XXIX

# PREFACE.

This is evident both in the frequency of marks <note>original: "signorum," referring to punctuation marks</note>, which is quite foreign to the most ancient Greek manuscripts, and in the fact that the mark for a period is very often placed where it poorly suits our logic. Any page can serve as an example. Thus, Genesis 23:1 and following are read in the Roman edition <note>The "Roman edition" refers to the 1587 Sixtine edition of the Septuagint, which was the standard text for centuries</note> as follows:

<note>original Greek: "’Εγένετο δὲ ἡ ζωὴ σάρρας..."</note> And the life of Sarah was one hundred and twenty-seven years. And Sarah died in the city of Arbok, which is in the valley. This is Hebron in the land of Canaan. And Abraham came to mourn for Sarah, and to weep. And Abraham rose up from his dead. And Abraham spoke to the sons of Heth, saying. I am a stranger and a sojourner among you. Give me therefore the possession of a burying-place among you, and I will bury my dead out of my sight. And the sons of Heth answered Abraham, saying. Not so, lord. But hear us. Thou art a king from God among us. In our chosen tombs bury thy dead. For no one etc.

In our work, these same things are written in this manner:

<note>original Greek: "’Εγένετο δὲ ἡ ζωὴ Σάρρας..."</note> And the life of Sarah was one hundred and twenty-seven years. ^2^And Sarah died in the city of Arbok, which is in the valley; this is Hebron in the land of Canaan. And Abraham came to mourn for Sarah and to weep. ^3^And Abraham rose up from his dead; and Abraham spoke to the sons of Heth, saying, ^4^"I am a stranger and a sojourner among you; give me therefore the possession of a burying-place among you, and I will bury my dead out of my sight." ^5^And the sons of Heth answered Abraham, saying, ^6^"No, lord; but hear us. Thou art a king from God among us; in our chosen tombs bury thy dead; for no one etc."

Furthermore, there are many places in the Roman edition that seemed incorrectly punctuated, and some even badly so. In these cases, we have corrected the lighter errors without warning the reader of the difference from the Roman edition; however, very frequently, and especially through the latter part of the work, the Roman punctuation itself is shown in the notes. Thus, in Exodus 19:15, instead of *Be ready, for three days do not approach a woman,* we have written *Be ready for three days, do not approach a woman.* Likewise in Exodus 22:30, instead of *So you shall do. Your calf and your sheep and your beast of burden; seven days it shall be under its mother etc.,* we have written *So you shall do with your calf and your sheep and your beast of burden; seven days it shall be under its mother etc.* Likewise in Exodus 30:36, instead of *from where I shall be known to you from there. It shall be a holy of holies to you incense. According to this composition you shall not make for yourselves etc.* we gave *from where I shall be known to you from there; it shall be a holy of holies to you. ^37^Incense according to this composition you shall not make for yourselves etc.* The Roman punctuation has been recorded for the sake of example in Ezekiel 25:9 and 10; 26:2; 27:25 and 26.

Nevertheless, in emending the punctuation, we have so restrained ourselves that we would not change anything unless the change was of the greatest probability

# PREFACE

->PROLEGOMENA<-

<note>original: "Prolegomena" — a formal Greek term for a preliminary treatise or introduction.</note>

...it might have the appearance. Hence, for example, in 2 Samuel 22:12 <note>original: "II Βασ. 22, 12"</note>, we have left untouched the phrase: *around him his pavilion was the darkness of waters; he made it thick with the clouds of the air* <note>original Greek: "κύκλῳ αὐτοῦ ἡ σκηνὴ αὐτοῦ σκότος ὑδάτων. ἐπάχυνεν ἐν νεφέλαις ἀέρος"</note>, even though it seemed better to follow the Alexandrian Codex <note>A famous 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</note> by joining "darkness of waters" with what follows, as the Hebrew text itself favors this. Nor did we touch other such passages that could not be amended without changing the reading itself, such as Ezekiel 21:12.

Furthermore, we have adopted the use of initial capital letters—primarily for writing proper names—from other editors of the Roman text, although it is not present in the Roman text itself. Likewise, we decided to mark the beginnings of speeches, questions, answers, and similar instances with a capital letter to serve as a form of punctuation. Although this was not the practice in ancient manuscripts, we hope it will be approved by learned men.

In dividing the verses, we have mostly followed the example of previous editors, who were accustomed to punctuate the Greek text according to Latin models (a custom which also passed from them to the Hebrew). However, the Greek text demands its own system; where it seemed appropriate, we satisfied this by displaying both the Latin and Greek verse orders with double numbering. We used a similar method where a difference in chapters was found. This especially concerns the second part of the Book of Jeremiah; great care was taken so that readers could easily compare it with the Hebrew text, the sections of which follow a very different order from the Latin.

XV. I turn now to the accents. Much labor was spent in correcting these. To provide some examples, in the case of nouns, we have written:
*Apis* for *apis*; *coal-pan* for *coal-pan*; *lambs* for *lambs*; *north wind* consistently for *north wind*; *the earths* for *the earths*; *weasel* for *weasel*; *griffin* and *vulture* with a long 'u' <note>original: "γρῦπα et γῦπα pro -ύ-"</note>; *gomor* <note>a dry measure</note> for *gomor* (for both appeared in the Roman edition); *of rulers*, likewise *of satraps*, for *of rulers* and *of satraps*, and similar forms; *direction* for *direction*; *ambush* for *ambush*; *fire-tongs* for *fire-tongs*; *sacrifice* for *sacrifice*; *stain* for *stain*; *ladder* for *ladder*; *of a base* for *of a base*; *anointing* everywhere (the Roman edition has it sometimes as *chrima* and sometimes *chrîma* <note>1</note>); *hollow* for *hollow*; *tempest* for *tempest*; *gull* for *gull*; *woman in childbirth* for *woman in childbirth*; *grandees* for *grandees*; *a fly* for *a fly*; *abomination* for *abomination*; *loins*, which was restored by pen in the Roman edition itself, for *loins*; *flood* for *flood*; *grass* always (the Roman edition has both *poia* and *poiá*); *foot* always (the Roman edition fluctuates between *poús* and *poûs*).

---

<note>1</note>) It is well known that both forms are found in ancient books, just as Aeschylus lengthened the 'iota' while Nonnus shortened it. The former seems to have pleased Greek antiquity, the latter the Alexandrian age.

*Sale* <note>original: πρᾶσις</note> with a circumflex instead of an acute; *elders* <note>πρεσβῦται</note> instead of *presbýtai*; *spoils* <note>σκῦλα</note> always, for it was commonly *skýla*; *gnats* <note>σκνῖφες</note> instead of *skníphes*; *bindweed* <note>σμίλαξ</note> instead of *smîlax*; *emerald* <note>σμάραγδος</note> instead of *smarágdos*; *pillars* <note>στῦλοι</note> instead of *stýloi*; *swine* <note>σῦς</note> instead of *sýs*; *wasps' nest* <note>σφηκιάν</note> instead of *sphēkían*; *seal* <note>σφραγῖδος</note> instead of *sphragîdos*; *hammer* <note>σφῦραν</note> instead of *sphýran*; *craftsmen* <note>τεχνῖται</note> instead of *technítai*; *land* <note>χώρα</note> instead of *chôra*; *anointing* and *unction* <note>χρῖσμα and χρῖσις</note> with a long "i"; *cold* <note>ψῦχος</note> instead of *psýchos*, etc.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In adjectives:
*heavy* <note>βαρσῖα</note> instead of *bareía*; *red* <note>ἐρυθράν</note> instead of *erythrân*, which is often found in the Roman edition. *Linen* <note>λινᾶ and λινᾶς</note> with a circumflex; *neighbors* <note>πλησίοι</note> instead of *plēsioi*; *bronze* <note>χαλκοῖ, χαλκοῦς</note> and *golden* <note>χρυσοῖ, χρυσοῦς</note> instead of the uncontracted forms; *beautiful* <note>ὡραία</note> instead of *hōraîa*, etc. Add the pronoun *These* <note>Αὕται</note> instead of *Haûtai* in Genesis 10:1.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In verbs:
*you wove* <note>ἔνειρας</note> instead of *eneíras*; *to stand* <note>ἑστάναι</note> instead of *hestânai*; *they might show* <note>ἐκφάνωσι</note> instead of *ekphanôsi*; *find!*, *speak!* <note>εὑρέ, εἰπόν</note> instead of *eûre, eipon* <note>1) Either accent in these imperatives can be defended by the authority of ancient manuscripts. Nor were many other things discussed here marked with the same accent everywhere by ancient scribes.</note>; *loosen!* <note>λῦε</note> instead of *lýe*; *do!* <note>πρᾶσσε</note> instead of *prásse*; *they surrounded* <note>περιέσχον</note> instead of *períeschon*; *understanding* <note>συνιῶν, συνιεῖς</note> etc. (as elsewhere *synieîn*), *they may let go* <note>ἀφιῶσι</note> (as elsewhere *aphioûsi*) is constantly written <note>2) It is better to take these as contracted forms from the root -eō, although *syníontos* (which I have put instead of *synióntos*) is not unheard of in the Septuagint; cf. 2 Chronicles 26:5.</note>; *having pushed* <note>ὥσας</note> instead of *hōsas*. Likewise *to gladden*, *to magnify*, *to multiply*, *thinning*, *dragging* <note>ἱλαρῦναι, μεγαλῦναι, πληϑῦναι, λεπτῦνον, σῦρον</note> with a circumflex; *to shut the eyes*, *to proclaim*, *to be strong*, *to prevail*, *to strengthen* <note>καμμῦσαι, κηρῦξαι, ἰσχῦσαι, κατισχῦσαι, ἐνισχῦσαι</note> with a circumflex; *to lean*, *to afflict*, *to crush*, *throw!*, *to anoint* <note>ἐκκλῖναι, ϑλῖψαι, συντρῖψαι, ῥῖψον, χρῖσαι</note> with a circumflex; *to defile*, *to embitter*, *to grow old* <note>μιᾶναι, πικρᾶναι, γηρᾶσαι</note> with a circumflex. Likewise *mixing*, *swearing*, *extinguishing* <note>κεραννύντες, ὀμνύντες, σβεννύντι</note> with a short "u"; *to force out*, *to pluck out*, *to hang* <note>ἐκβιάσαι, ἐκσπάσαι, κρεμάσαι</note> with a circumflex; *to insult* <note>καϑυβρίσαι</note> with a circumflex; likewise many others of that kind <note>3) From *peiráō* and *peirázō* (to try/test) we have admitted both *peirâsai* and *peirásai*; likewise *hamarteîn* (to sin) and *hamartōn* (sinning), just as in 2 Chronicles 6:39 it is *hamartōnti*.</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In adverbs: *carefully* <note>ἐπιμιλῖ</note> instead of *epimelē*, etc.
Also relevant here are instances such as Exodus 11:9, *that I may multiply* <note>ἵνα πληϑύνω</note> where the Roman edition has *hina plēthynô*; Deuteronomy 8:8 and 9, *to send* and *to raise up* <note>ἀποστεῖλαι and ἀναστήσαι</note>, where the Roman edition incorrectly has *-steîlai* and *-stêsai*.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Many things had to be corrected in gentile names <note>names of tribes or nations</note>, such as *Spartans* <note>Σπαρτιᾶται</note> instead of *-átai*, and *Moabites, Ammonitess, Gileaditess, Shunammitess* <note>Μωαβῖται, Ἀμμωνῖτις, Γαλααδῖτις, Σωμανῖτις</note>, which were written everywhere with the circumflex. He uses a similar method for *Levites* <note>Λευῖται</note>, which used to be published as *Leuítai*.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;No small amount of effort was also spent so that proper names would be published constantly with the same accent and breathing, such as *Amram* <note>4) The Roman edition at Numbers 26:58 has *amram* (lowercase/different accent) and 59 *amrám*. Conversely, Exodus 6:18 has *Amrám*.</note>, *Barnea, Ezion-Geber, Deborah, Edrei, Elealeh, Eliab, Elisaph, Elisaphan, Elisur, Heshbon, Hiram, Esau, Zacchaeus, Jericho, Ismini, Jesus, Jehu, Jozabdus, John, Jotham, Cyrus, Migdol, Maron, Noah, Huldah, Oziel, Salmona, Caleb*.

<summary>This page details the specific philological corrections made to Greek accents and spellings in this edition of the Septuagint, focusing on nouns, verbs, and biblical proper names. The editor justifies these changes by citing ancient manuscript authority and correcting inconsistencies found in previous standard editions like the Roman Sixtine text.</summary>

<keywords>Septuagint, philology, Greek grammar, accentuation, orthography, biblical names, Prolegomena, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical preface (Prolegomena) regarding the orthographic and grammatical principles used in editing the Greek biblical text, specifically focusing on consistency in accents and word division.</meta>

# PREFACE.

Likewise, I have ensured that the same names do not sometimes have diaeresis marks <note>dots placed over a vowel to indicate it is pronounced separately, e.g., ë</note> and sometimes not, such as *Amisadai*, *Armadaim*, *Ephraim* ^1), and *Ieiel*. In all of these, it is impossible to say how great the inconsistency is in the Roman edition <note>The "Editio Romana" of the Septuagint, published in 1587</note> and others; it will be proven below that this inconsistency pertains not only to accents and breathings but to the very letters themselves ^2). Hence, although many things have been corrected by us, one cannot hope that no traces of the Roman inconsistency remain now.

Furthermore, it often happened that the Roman editors and those who followed them marked the genitive cases of proper names ending in *-as* with the accent of the nominative cases in *-ás*. Therefore, just as it is correctly written in the Roman edition itself as *Zelphas* (*Genesis 46:18*), we have restored *Adas* (*Genesis 36:10 ff.*), *Ballas* (*Genesis 46:15*), *Melchas* (*Genesis 24:15 and 24*), *Masekkas* (*Genesis 36:36*), *Olibemas* (*Genesis 36:14 and 18*), and *Sophas* (*1 Chronicles 7:36*). Likewise *from Marisa* (elsewhere found as *Marisa*, *Maresa*) and similar forms.

In enclitic words <note>words that "lean" on the preceding word for their accent</note>, the logic of which fluctuates greatly even in ancient manuscripts, we believe we have followed the most probable course ^3).

In the *Roman edition*, it is written as *anaméson* (between), *diapantós* (continually), *kaíge* (and yet), etc. We preferred to write them as separate words: *anà méson* (in the midst), *ap' archēs* (from the beginning) ^4), *aph' hóte* (since), *dià pantós* (continually), *dià kenēs* (in vain), *dià tí* (why), *eis aúrion* (for tomorrow), *epì tò autó* (together), *hína tí* (why), *kaí ge* (and indeed), *prìn ē* (before), *tò deúteron* (the second time), *tò deilinón* (the evening), *tò próteron* (formerly), *tò prōí* (in the morning), *tò táchos* (quickly), and others of this kind; although it is difficult to say what limits that rule has. For it will certainly not be right to write similar words like *kathólou* (entirely), *diólou* (wholly), or *oukéti* (no longer) as separate words in every instance ^5).

---

1) After the *Roman edition* used *ephraïm* a few times, it mostly follows with *ephraïm*. We have published it everywhere as *Ephraïm*; in the Alexandrian Codex (likewise in the *Codex Sinaiticus* at *1 Chronicles 12:30*) it is mostly *ephraim*, which we have not noted everywhere. See below.
2) Here and there, a difference in accent will seem to be suggested by a difference in spelling, as in *Kison*, *Keison*, *Kisson*; in *Phison* and *Pheison*; in *Geon* and *Gēon*. Nevertheless, in these cases too, we have applied the same accent.
3) In various places, the interrogative *tís* (who/which), where it takes the place of a relative pronoun, seemed to be confused with the indefinite *tis* (someone/anyone), as in *Leviticus 14:35, 21:17*, where we printed *tínos* (for *tinòs*) *autoū* and *tíni* (for *tiní*) *eán*. Nor was it more tolerable in *Numbers 22:38*: "I shall be able to speak *tí* (something)." We wrote *lalēsaí ti* (to speak something), as the Hebrew text itself suggests.
4) But in *1 Samuel 2:29*, we preserved *aparchēs* since it seemed to be derived from *aparchē* (first-fruits). In *Wisdom of Solomon 14:23*, we wrote *exállōn* (from *exallos*, "strange/frantic") instead of *ex állōn* (from others).
5) Thus also instead of *mē dè*, which is frequent in the *Roman edition*, we wrote *mēdè* (nor/not even). This also pertains to the spelling of *proselthōn* (having approached), *eiselthōn* (having entered), etc., with the letter *s* [sigma] not *σ*. We have preserved the custom of most editions in this matter. But the spelling found even in the first *Roman edition*—*proselthōn*, *eiselthōn*, etc.—has more authority from the manuscripts; except that for the sake of consistency, based on the authority of the manuscripts, forms like *eis*, *pros*, *hōs*, and similar words should be printed as they are when they stand alone.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Greek orthography and breathing marks in the Roman edition of the Septuagint.</meta>

# PREFACE.

We have already touched upon the variety of the breathing <note>original: "spiritus"; refers to the marks used in Greek to indicate an "h" sound or its absence</note> when we spoke of proper names. Beyond others (as we have written *helikta* <note>original: ἑλικτά</note> for *elikta* <note>original: ἐλικτά</note> in Leviticus 6:21), the reflexive pronoun *hautou, hautō* <note>original: αὑτοῦ, αὑτῷ</note> etc., belongs here. Where you might expect these forms according to the custom of most editors of the New Testament and other Greek works, the Roman editors have most correctly accustomed themselves to placing the oblique cases of the demonstrative pronoun *autou, autō* <note>original: αὐτοῦ, αὐτῷ</note> etc. <note>The author is arguing that the "h" sound (rough breathing) was often dropped in the reflexive pronoun in later Greek.</note> For it is very probable that the so-called "reflexion" was much more often neglected than observed in those times to which both the Greek text of the Old Testament and the books of the New Testament are owed <note>1</note>. This is proved both by the authority of ancient manuscripts supplied with breathings and accents from about the eighth century onwards, and especially by the fact that in many places where it might be doubtful whether *hautou* or *autou* <note>original: αὑτοῦ / αὐτοῦ</note> should be read, the prepositions *ap, ant, ep, kat, met* <note>original: ἀπ’ ἀντ’ ἐπ’ κατ’ μετ’</note> are found prefixed, rather than *aph, anth, eph, kath, meth* <note>original: ἀφ’ ἀνϑ’ ἐφ’ καϑ’ μεϑ’</note>. Regarding this matter in the books of the Seventy Translators <term>Septuagint: the Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</term>, compare 1 Kings 9:5 *met autou*; 18:13 *ap autou*; 2 Kings 13:19 *ep autēs*; 4 Kings 3:27 *ant autou*; Judges 3:23 *kat autou*; 1 Chronicles 15:15 *ep autous* (the Alexandrian manuscript <note>Codex Alexandrinus</note> has *eph heautous* <note>2</note>), and what is of the same reasoning, 2 Chronicles 29:9 *ouk autōn* (the Alexandrian manuscript has *ouch*). The most significant of these examples are those where you would most especially expect the opposite, such as 4 Kings 3:27 and Judges 3:23 <note>3</note>.

Indeed, other examples are added, even with no preposition attached, by which the reasoning of the Roman editors in these matters is clearly proved, such as Sirach 27:25: *He who throws a stone on high throws it upon his own head* <note>original: Ὁ βάλλων λίϑον εἰς ὕψος ἐπὶ κεφαλὴν αὑτοῦ βάλλει</note>. Since these things are so, we have deemed that those places where they departed from their own rule <note>4</note> should also be restored to the same standard. Therefore, *autou* has been restored for *hautou* in Genesis 41:11, 46:1; 2 Kings 1:11, 15:14; *autēs* for *hautēs* in 2 Kings 11:4, 13:19; *autō* for *hautō* in Deuteronomy 12:18, 17:16, 18; 29:13; *autōn* for *hautōn* in Deuteronomy 25:2, 2 Kings 13:19; and the same in a few other places, with one exception—and that the only one unless I am mistaken—in 2 Chronicles 8:1, where the reflexive form seemed to have singular importance.

Other changes have been made, not indeed in the breathing itself, but nevertheless depending on the breathing. For several places are found in the Roman edition where *ouk* <note>the form used before a non-aspirated vowel</note> is published before aspirated syllables, and conversely *ouch* <note>the form used before an aspirated vowel</note> before non-aspirated ones. Although this is primarily from the Vati—

---

1) I fear that too many have judged the usage of even the more ancient Greeks based on insufficiently accurate editions and on manuscripts of a more recent age.
2) Namely, where a later age desired a reflexive pronoun, it seems to have preferred the fuller form *heautou* to the shorter *autou*. Compare also Genesis 39:6 where instead of *kath auton* in the Roman edition, the Alexandrian manuscript has *kath heauton*.
3) It is rare for the opposite to occur, as in 3 Kings 11:18 *meth autōn* (the Alexandrian manuscript has *met autōn*), as it was certainly published.
4) It even happened that they corrected themselves with a pen, as in 1 Maccabees 8:3, where instead of *ta skeuē hautou*, *t. sk. autou* was restored by the editors' pen.

->SEPTUAGINT. c<-

# XXXIV
## PREFACE.

It seems to have flowed from a certain book, just as the authority of the Alexandrian manuscript <note>The Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</note> not infrequently happens to agree (as in Exodus 16:27, **they did not find** <note>original: "ουκ ευρον"</note>), and can be defended by learned reasons; nevertheless, it follows that these occurred against the will of the editors because their own pen corrected them here and there. For example, in Exodus 12:49, where **shall be found** <note>original: "εὑρεθήσεται"</note> was restored from **not** <note>original: "οὐχ"</note> to **not** <note>original: "οὐχ"</note>; likewise in Job 31:26, where **we do not see** <note>original: "οὐκ ὁρῶμεν"</note> was corrected from what had first been written as **we do not see** <note>original: "οὐκ ὁρῶμεν"</note> (with an omicron). Hence we have also taken care to amend the remaining passages. They are mostly these: Genesis 34:7, **not so**; Deuteronomy 21:7, **they have not seen**; 1 Kings 8:46, **he shall not sin**; 1 Esdras 8:66, **he did not separate**; 2 Esdras 3:13, **it was not** and 9:1, **it was not separated**; Nehemiah 13:26, **there was not**; Psalm 105:11, **not one was left**; Proverbs 29:7, **it does not exist**; Sirach 44:19, **he was not found** and 48:13, **he was not proud**. Added to these are those of the same kind: 2 Esdras 6:11, **according to me** and 3 Maccabees 2:22, **upon the ground**.

I pass over those things concerning the *iota subscript* <note>A small 'i' written under a vowel in Greek</note> which were published by us differently than by the Roman editors, such as **to live** <note>original: "ζῆν"</note> instead of **to live** <note>original: "ζῇν"</note>, **he took up** <note>original: "ἦρεν"</note> instead of **he took up** <note>original: "ᾗρεν"</note>, etc.; likewise, we did not place the *iota adscript* <note>An 'i' written next to a capital letter</note> which was mostly <gloss>1)</gloss> published by them in "**Let us sing**" and "**Ode**" <gloss>2)</gloss>.

The *movable nu* <term>nu ephelkystikon: a 'n' added to the end of Greek words to avoid two vowels clashing</term> has exercised us sufficiently; it was often added or omitted in the Roman edition contrary to custom and, I believe, not without negligence. For example: Deuteronomy 1:21, **he has delivered to us**; 4:20, **God took**; 23:22, **it is in you**; Joshua 24:27, **Joshua said**; 6:22, **he said to those who had scouted**; Judges 19:14, **it is in**; 1 Kings 17:8, **he cried out to**; likewise Exodus 14:14, **the Lord led out**; 23:22, **it is all**; 26:18, **twenty columns**; 38:10, **he made for the**; Numbers 22:11, **it has covered the**; Joshua 18:3, **the Lord gave**. In these cases, we have not left them untouched unless they seemed capable of being defended either by the prevention of the flow of speech or some other suitable cause, as in Job 36:16, **he has misled you**. Likewise, we have everywhere restored **twenty** <note>original: "εἴκοσι"</note>, even if a vowel follows. Although the Roman edition observes this well in 4 Kings 16:2, **twenty years old** and 2 Samuel 3:20, **twenty men**, it does not observe it in several other places such as Genesis 6:3, Judges 4:3, 1 Samuel 4:18, and 14:14 <gloss>3)</gloss>. Indeed, I have been taught that it is held thus constantly, with very few exceptions, in all the most ancient written books, although Ludovicus Dindorf, along with the *Etymologicum Magnum* page 297, 51, has decided otherwise; see Stephen’s *Greek Thesaurus* under **twenty** and **twenty-nine**.

**XVI.** We must now report on corrections of greater importance. A not insignificant part of these has been shaped according to the pen...

---

1) mostly, but not constantly; just as on page 475, **Ode** is read three times, and **Oide** once.
2) That spelling is foreign to the Alexandrian and other very ancient manuscripts; for everywhere it is written **Let us sing**, **Ode**.
3) In this place and elsewhere, Grabe incorrectly published **twenty** <note>original: "εἴκοσιν"</note> from the Alexandrian manuscript. Again, elsewhere, as in 4 Kings 16:2, he accurately published **twenty years old** <note>original: "εἴκοσι ἐτῶν"</note>.

# PROLEGOMENA.

...emendations ¹) shown in the Roman edition; those which we have not adopted, we will indicate at the same time. Other things which we have corrected were so clearly written in error in the Roman edition that you might wonder why most of those who reproduced the Vatican text took care to faithfully reproduce them. Finally, it will be certain to no one that other things were emended for suitable reasons by us and others ²), for we were the first to provide only a few.

**Genesis** 19:5. we wrote *to us* <note>original: "πρὸς ἡμᾶς"</note> instead of *to you* <note>original: "πρ. ὑμᾶς"</note>; 20:14. and 16. *didrachma* <note>a double drachma coin</note>, just as it is in 23:15, 16. and elsewhere, instead of *didragma*; 21:21. the Roman reading retained by Morin, Reineccius, Van Ess, the 1822 Venetian editor, and others ³) reads thus: *in the desert. And his mother took for him a wife from Pharan of Egypt.* But *Pharan* was added by pen after *desert*, and *from the* <note>original: "ἐκ τῆς"</note> was emended instead of *from Pharan*. Both corrections not only come close to the Alexandrian manuscript (which has *in the desert of Pharan* and *from the land of Egypt*), but are also recommended by many other witnesses, except that most, along with the Alexandrian, add *the* and have *land* instead of *the*. 23:8. we wrote *Ephron*, as follows immediately in verse 20, instead of *Ephrom*; 27:45. *of you two* instead of *of us two*; 33:18. *he encamped* instead of *he received*; 36:37. *Samada* from a pen emendation instead of *Samaa* ⁴). 41:1. *upon the river* instead of *upon and river*; 50:23. *Machir*, as it is twice in 46:20, instead of *Macheir*. **Exodus** 10:26. *to our God* instead of *to your God*; 12:10. we published *you shall leave* <note>original: "ἀπολείψετε"</note> instead of *it shall be left*. The Alexandrian Codex has *you shall leave for yourselves*. 16:22. we published *Moses* <note>original: "Μωυσεί"</note> with the Roman editors, but among them it was corrected by pen to *Moses* <note>original: "Μωυσῇ"</note>. It is well known that ancient books often fluctuate between both forms even within the same writers. 30:33. *whoever gives* we wrote from a pen emendation instead of *as if he gives*; likewise 31:5. *stone-working* <note>original: "λιθουργικά"</note> from a pen emendation instead of *liturgical* <note>original: "λειτουργικά"</note>; 33:16. *with us* instead of *with you*; 34:11. *I command*, as has already been corrected by pen in the Roman edition, instead of *I may command*; 35:7. *dyed red*, as preceded twice, instead of *erydrodanomena* <note>a misspelling of the Greek word for "dyed red"</note>; 35:13. I changed nothing; but the second *the*, which is missing in several of Holmes's witnesses, was struck out by pen in the Roman edition. **Leviticus** 8:26. *he took*. The Roman editors had placed *and* before it, but again deleted it with a pen. 18:3. *upon it* <note>feminine</note> we published from a pen emendation instead of *upon it* <note>genitive</note>. **Numbers** 1:10. we wrote *Phadassur*, as it is four times in chapters 2 and 7, instead of *Phadasur*; 1:13. *Phageel*,

1) See above, section XIII.

# PREFACE. XXXVII

<note>original: "ἀνέβαινον"</note> *they went up* instead of *they were going up* 9, 23. <note>original: "ἠθέτησαν"</note> *they rejected*, as corrected by pen in the Roman edition, instead of *they set aside*. Regarding this, which was foolishly retained by him, Holmes <note>Robert Holmes (1748–1805), a biblical scholar known for his massive edition of the Septuagint.</note> notes no variation in the reading. 11, 24. <note>original: "κληρονομήσομεν"</note> *we shall inherit* from a pen emendation instead of *let us inherit* 16, 9. We have placed <note>original: "στυππίου"</note> *of tow/flax* instead of *of coarse fiber*, having compared 15, 14. Leviticus 13, 47. and 59. The Alexandrian codex <note>Codex Alexandrinus: a 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible.</note> indeed constantly upholds the second form, which we also have not changed in the prophets. 18, 14. <note>original: "ὅ τι"</note> *whatever* (Alexandrian codex: *something*) instead of *that* we have edited 20, 6. <note>original: "ἐμέλισα"</note> *I dismembered*, which the pen already restored to the Roman edition, instead of *it mattered to me* 21. We have always edited <note>original: "Ἰαβεῖς Γαλαάδ"</note> *Jabesh Gilead*. The Roman edition in the same chapter has sometimes *-bis* as in verses 9, 14, and sometimes *-beis* as in verses 8, 10, 12. Ruth 2, 13. <note>original: "εὕροιμι"</note> *may I find*, as it was already corrected by pen, instead of *I might find* 6, 11. We wrote <note>original: "Δειαν"</note> *Deian*, as everywhere before, instead of *Dian*.

I Kings <note>The Greek Septuagint titles the books of Samuel and Kings as I-IV Kingdoms (Basileion).</note> 1, 15. we wrote *by day* <note>original: "ἡμέρᾳ"</note> instead of *day* 3, 21. We retained *of the prophet* <note>original: "τοῦ προφήτης"</note> from the Roman edition, although it was corrected by pen to *of the prophets*: which correction indeed seems to be due to an error 8, 20. We edited *he shall judge* <note>original: "δικάσει"</note> from a pen emendation in the Roman edition instead of *he might judge* 10, 5. We restored with Reineccius, Ess <note>Leander van Ess (1772–1847), a German Catholic theologian who produced a famous edition of the Septuagint.</note> and others <note>original: "ἀνάστημα"</note> *height/outpost* (so also the Alexandrian codex) instead of *monument* 25, 10. *multiplied* <note>original: "πεπληθυμμένοι"</note> from a pen emendation in the Roman edition instead of *increased* 25, 29. We corrected *clothed* <note>original: "ἐνδεδυμένη"</note> instead of the dative form, which Ess repeated however foolishly 25, 35. <note>original: "ᾑρέτισα"</note> *I have chosen* instead of *I preferred*, just as in the emendations shown at the end of the Roman edition for Psalm 118, 173. *I chose* was restored. II Kings 3, 10. *Beersheba*, as often elsewhere, instead of *Bersebee* 18, 4. *of you* <note>plural</note> instead of *of us* 23, 4. *where*, which the pen already restored in the Roman edition but which Ess nonetheless neglected with others, instead of *not*. III Kings 2, 23. *commander-in-chief* <note>original: "ἀρχιστράτηγος"</note>, as elsewhere, instead of the variant spelling 3, 3. *Solomon* instead of *Salomon* from a pen emendation in the Roman edition 3, 36. *Build!* <note>imperative</note> instead of *he built* 4, 12. *from Beth-shan* instead of *Ekbethshan* 7, 35. *the ladles* instead of the variant spelling 8, 37. *famine* <note>original: "λιμός"</note> instead of *pestilence* <note>original: "λοιμός"</note> from a pen emendation. In verse 41 of the same chapter, between *your* and *this*, *not* had been poorly edited, but the pen deleted it. 12, 24. *to your brothers* instead of *our* 13, 11. *he was dwelling* instead of the variant spelling 13, 20. *upon the table* instead of *upon the and table*. In the Roman edition, the pen deleted the "and". 15, 23. *dominion* <note>original: "δυναστεία"</note>, just as follows immediately in 16, 5. and 28., instead of the variant spelling 16, 28. *in Syria Nasib; and* instead of *in Syria. Nasib and* ¹) IV Kings 4, 31. *he placed upon*, as corrected at the end of the Roman edition, instead of *he put away* 5, 17. between *of mules* and *that not*, there had been edited *and you shall give to me from the red earth*, but the pen deleted all those things, with a note written below the text in this manner:

---

1) III Kings 20, 20. we did not change *the female dogs* <note>original: "αἱ κύνες"</note>. Others, such as Reineccius and Holmes, edited *the male dogs*.

# XXXVIII

->**PREFACE**<-

"In several books there follows *and you --- red*." <note>original: "καὶ σὺ --- πυῤῥᾶς"</note> At 8:8 and following, we have twice placed *weaknesses*. <note>original: "ἀῤῥωστίας"</note> In the Roman Edition <note>The 1587 Editio Romana of the Septuagint</note> at verse 8 it is *-ias*, and at verse 9 it is *-eias*. At 16:10, we corrected *Thalgathphellasar* in place of *Thalgaphthellasar*. At 17:16 and 32 (in the first instance), *for themselves* <note>original: "ἑαυτοῖς"</note> was corrected by pen from *of herself* <note>original: "ἑαυτῆς"</note>, which was nonsensical. At 18:34, we published *Seppharuaim* instead of *Seppharuraim*. For certainly in all other places it is written *-rua-*, not *-ura-*. At 19:25, we restored *they gathered* <note>original: "συνήγαγον"</note>, which the pen had already restored in the Roman edition, in place of *they led*. At 19:28, *in the* <note>original: "ἐν τοῖς"</note> instead of *from the* <note>original: "ἐκ τοῖς"</note>. (Essius <note>Leander van Ess, a 19th-century biblical scholar</note> changed nothing.) At 19:30, *of the house* <note>original: "οἴκου"</note> from a pen emendation instead of *the house* <note>accusative: "οἶκον"</note>. At 21:4, *the name* <note>nominative: "τὸ ὄνομα"</note> instead of *to the name* <note>dative: "τῷ ὄνομα"</note>. (Essius faithfully repeated the error.) The same correction was made by pen in the Roman edition itself at 2 Chronicles 26:8. At 22:19, *as many things as I spoke* instead of *as many things as you spoke*. The letter *s* <note>the sigma at the end of "elalesas"</note> had already been deleted by pen in the Roman edition, yet Essius retained it.

**1 Chronicles** 2:9. We gave *Jerahmeel*, as it is read in verses 26 and 27 and was already corrected here by pen, instead of *Jeramael*. At 7:6, we deleted *and* <note>original: "καί"</note> before *Bela*, just as the pen had already erased it in the Roman edition. At 5:23, *Baal Hermon*, as elsewhere, instead of *Baal, Hermon*. At 18:1, *Gath*, as elsewhere, instead of *Ged*. At 27:16, *Reuben* from a pen emendation instead of *Reubin*. **2 Chronicles** 3:16, *Dabir*, as follows in 4:20 and 5:9, instead of *Davir*. At 5:7 and 8, *Cherubim* three times from a pen emendation instead of *Cherubin*. At 18:7, *let him speak* <note>original: "λαλείτω"</note> instead of *let him speak* <note>original: "λαλήτω", a different vowel spelling</note>. At 18:21, *false* instead of *not false*. But the *a* <note>the prefix "a-" meaning "not"</note> has already been erased by pen. At 30:18, *Issachar and Zebulun* instead of *Issachar. Zebulun*. At 36:22, *of Cyrus*, as the pen already restored, instead of *of the Lord*.

**1 Esdras** 1:33. The pen restored *him* <note>original: "αὐτὸν"</note> in the Roman edition instead of *he*. At 4:56, we corrected *they guard* <note>original: "φρουροῦσι"</note> after others, instead of *phrorousi*. At 5:69, *and counsels and* was printed in the Roman edition, but the pen erased the second *and*. At 6:21, we gave *it may be found* <note>original: "εὑρίσκηται"</note> from a pen emendation instead of *you may find*, while retaining the *a* for most. At 9:12, *let them stand* <note>original: "στήτωσαν"</note> instead of *stetotan*. **2 Esdras** 2:61, *he was called* <note>original: "ἐκλήθη"</note> instead of *ekklethe*. At 4:10, *the remnant* <note>original: "τὸ κατάλοιπον"</note> instead of *to katalipon*. **Nehemiah** 1:9, *your dispersion* instead of *our d.* At 2:18, *they were strengthened* <note>original: "ἐκραταιώθησαν"</note> instead of *ekkra-*. At 3:22, we wrote *men of Ekchechar*. It seems to be written the same way in the Roman edition (*ekchechar*), but Essius with others published it as *from Chechar*. At 4:11, we corrected *let us kill* <note>original: "φονεύσωμεν"</note> instead of *phon-*. At 10:30, *of the land* from a pen emendation instead of *to the land*, which Holmes <note>Robert Holmes, editor of a major Septuagint edition</note> among others retained. **Tobit** 1:7, *them in Jerusalem* instead of *them into Jerusalem*. At 2:7, *having dug* <note>original: "ὀρύξας"</note>, as corrected by pen, instead of *oruxas*. At 3:9, *us* instead of *you*. At 3:10, *so that* <note>original: "ὥστε"</note> from a pen emendation instead of *who also* <note>original: "ὅς τε"</note>. <sup>1)</sup> **Judith** 6:5, *Ammon* instead of *Ammao*. At 9:6, *they said* from a pen emendation instead of *I said*. At 12:1.

---

1) Tobit 7:3. I have not changed *from Nineveh*. Holmes, following the Alexandrian Codex, placed *in Nineveh*, but he cites *from* <note>original: "ἐκ"</note> from the Vatican Codex itself.

# PREFACE.

<note>original: "PROLEGOMENA"</note>

We have corrected the order to "of him and he composed," as this word order was indicated by a pen-stroke in the Roman edition itself. **Esther 2:7**: "in form" <note>original: "τῷ εἴδει"</note> instead of "the form" <note>original: "τὸ εἴδει"</note> (which Essius retains). **8:14**: "in Susa" instead of "from Susa" (also retained by Essius).

**Job 3:25**: "I feared" <note>original: "ἐδεδοίκειν"</note> instead of "I was in fear" <note>original: "ἐνεδοίκειν"</note>. **5:15**: we corrected "they might perish" <note>original: "ἀπόλοιντο"</note> instead of "they perished" <note>original: "ἀπώλοιντο"</note>. **9:4**: we published "with the mind" <note>original: "διανοίᾳ"</note>. The same appears in the Roman edition, but the iota subscript <note>A small mark under a Greek vowel indicating an 'i' sound</note> had almost vanished. Hence Holmes, Essius, and others published "by the mind" <note>original: "διανοία"</note> without the iota! **9:14**: "he will judge" instead of "he judges" <margin>1)</margin>. **18:11**: "pains" <note>original: "ὀδύναι"</note>, as corrected by the pen, instead of "birth-pains" <note>original: "ὠδύναι"</note> (retained by Essius). **18:17**: "he might perish" instead of "he perished." **19:2**: "you will do" <note>future tense</note> instead of "you might do" <note>subjunctive mood</note>. **21:31**: "he will repay" instead of "he shall repay" <note>correcting a spelling error in the Greek future tense</note>. **25:5**: "moon" instead of "moon" <note>correcting an accent</note>. **30:14**: "he has used" from a pen correction instead of "he has been judged." **39:24**: "with wrath" <note>dative case</note> instead of "wrath" <note>nominative case</note>. **42:8**: "you" <note>plural</note> instead of "us."

**Psalms 6:2**: "you might rebuke" instead of "you might re-rebuke" <note>correcting a double 'l' typo</note>. **7:5**: "then from the..." from a pen correction instead of "then the...". **9:29**: "in an ambush," as Essius already correctly has; the Roman edition has "by an ambush," while Bos, Reineccius, and Holmes have "ambushes." **13:3**: we added "under" before "the lips" from a pen correction. **17:41**: "you destroyed" restored by pen in the Roman edition instead of "you utterly destroyed." **21:9**: we corrected "let him save" instead of "let him save" <margin>2)</margin>. **34:26**: "speaking great things" instead of "speaking great things" <note>correcting a spelling variant</note>. **42:1**: "of a man" from a pen correction instead of "of a face." **44:6**: with Walton, Bos, and others, we omitted "my" before "of the king," just as it is also missing in the note below the text in the Roman edition itself. **47:5**: we published "the kings" instead of "the kings of the earth." For that addition, although it is in the **Alexandrian Codex** <note>A famous 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</note>, the editors' own pen deleted it. **62:12**: we corrected "he shall be praised" instead of "he shall be praised" <note>removing an extra 's'</note>. **67:8**: "the desert" <note>feminine</note> instead of "the desert" <note>masculine</note>. **67:22**: "of hair" instead of "of hair" <note>correcting a breathing mark</note>. **77:1**: "give heed" instead of "give heed" <note>accent correction</note>. **88:1**: "to an Israelite" instead of "to an Israelite." Likewise **verse 50**: "you swore" instead of "you swore" <note>correcting an 'o' to an 'omega'</note>. **89:14**: we left "may we be glad" untouched along with Walton, Bos, etc. (Reineccius punctuated it thus: "of us. May we be glad, instead of"). But the Roman edition has it struck through with a pen and does not recognize it in the notes. See the Alexandrian Codex <margin>3)</margin>. **101:4**: "they were parched" instead of "they were parched" <note>spelling correction</note>. **104:28** and **103:29**: "they shall fail" instead of "they shall fail" <note>removing a double 'k'</note>. **107:7**: "your" after "by the right hand" added by pen in the Roman edition; likewise **121:1**: "upon" before "the [things]" and **131:16**: "with exultation" before "they shall exult" <margin>4)</margin>. **Proverbs**—

---

1) **Job 9:11**: I decided "not even so" <note>original: "οὐδ᾽ ὥς"</note> should not be changed, although Holmes and Essius and perhaps others published "not even as" <note>original: "οὐδ᾽ ὡς"</note>.
2) More of this kind, such as "toward" for "toward," "may it be," "breadth," "man," "man" etc., are not recorded in this list. Nor are all those whose faulty writing they corrected with a pen, such as "having been cast away" [the syllable 'be' added by pen] in **Isaiah 1**, or "you shall hear" [the 'se' deleted by pen] in **Jeremiah 37**.
3) **Psalm 94:1**: we did not change "we shall exult." Holmes and others published "let us exult."
4) **Psalm 118:129**: I did not touch "he searched out." Holmes thought it should be corrected to "he searched out" <note>a different Greek augment</note>. See the note on page 41 regarding **Jeremiah 13:19**.

# PREFACE. XLI

"Those who advise," <note>original: οἱ συνεβουλεύοντες</note> which in the Roman edition was changed by a pen-stroke from "those who are advising" <note>original: οἱ συμβουλεύοντες</note> 34, 2. We corrected "the nations" <note>original: τὰ ἔθνη</note> instead of "the nations" <note>original: τὰ ἔθνει, a misspelling</note> 38, 2. "Wall" <note>original: τοῖχον</note> instead of "wall" <note>original: τεῖχον</note> 40, 18. "In the likeness" <note>original: ὁμοιώματι</note> instead of "in the likeness" <note>original: ὠμοιώματι</note> 41, 23. "Coming upon" <note>original: ἐπερχόμενα</note> instead of "holding back" <note>original: ἐπεχόμενα</note> 42, 24. "Who" <note>original: τίς</note> instead of "to whom" <note>original: οἷς</note> 43, 28. "To destroy" <note>original: ἀπολέσαι</note> instead of "to destroy" <note>original: ἀπωλέσαι</note> 53, 10. Having preserved "you may give" <note>original: δῶτε</note> <gloss>1)</gloss> it seemed necessary to write "of you" <note>plural</note> instead of "of us" 61, 3. "Garment" <note>original: καταστολήν</note> instead of "according to the robe" <note>original: κατὰ στολήν</note> in Jeremiah 11, 20. Instead of "before you" <note>original: πρὸ σέ</note> we corrected it to "toward you" <note>original: πρὸς σέ</note> <gloss>2)</gloss> 15, 11. We placed "of those directing" <note>original: κατευθυνόντων</note> instead of "of those sleeping" <note>original: καθευδυνόντων</note> 19, 6. We preserved "Ennom" <note>original: Ἐννώμ</note> but according to a pen-correction and with the agreement of the Alexandrian and Frederick-Augustus codices, it should have been written "Ennom" <note>original: Ἐννόμ, with a short 'o'</note> 27, 21. We published "I command" <note>original: ἐντέλλομαι</note> from a pen-correction instead of "I might command" 31, 33. "They did not do; and these..." <note>original: οὐκ ἐποίησαν αἰδὲ</note> <gloss>3)</gloss>. "From" <note>original: ἀπό</note> instead of "they did not do, but those... from" 39, 17. Again "by the strength" <note>original: τῇ ἰσχύϊ</note> instead of "by the strength" <note>original: τῇ ἰσχύει</note> 45, 27. "They asked" <note>original: ἠρώτησαν</note> instead of "they were asked" in Lamentations 3, 26. "For the soul" <note>original: ψυχῇ</note> instead of "the soul" 3, 31. "He will have mercy" <note>original: οἰκτειρήσει</note> instead of "he will have mercy" <note>original: οἰκτηρήσει</note> 4, 21. "You will pour out" <note>original: ἀποχεεῖς</note> instead of "you pour out" in the Epistle of Jeremiah 1, 19. "The clothing" <note>original: τὸν ἱματισμόν</note> instead of "the clothing" <note>neuter gender error</note> in Ezekiel 17, 17. "In the casting up of a mound." <term>Van Ess</term> <note>Leander van Ess, a 19th-century biblical scholar</note> was careful not to add the <term>iota subscript</term> <note>a small Greek letter 'i' written under a vowel</note> himself, which either the Roman editors or the printers had omitted. 18, 25. "He directs" <note>original: κατευθύνει</note> in the second place instead of "he will direct." For verse 25 ought to be corrected according to verse 29. 23, 21. "You visited" <note>original: ἐπεσκέψω</note> instead of "I will visit" 25, 7. "Of regions" <note>original: χωρῶν</note> instead of "of hands" 26, 4. "Smooth rock" <note>original: λεωπετρίαν</note>, as follows in verse 14, instead of "smooth rock" <note>original: λεοπετρίαν</note> <gloss>4)</gloss> 39, 2. We published "the mountains of Israel" <note>original: τὰ ὄρη τὰ Ἰσρ.</note>, as follows immediately in verse 4, instead of "the mountains for Israel." The Alexandrian Codex has "of Israel" in both places. 40, 22. 26. 31. 34. likewise 43, 17. "By the steps" and "the steps" <note>original: κλιμακτῆρσιν / κλιμακτῆρες</note> from a pen-correction instead of a misspelling. 41, 15. "Behind" <note>original: κατόπισθεν</note> instead of "behind" <note>original: κατώπισθεν</note> 45, 7. "The boundaries" <note>original: τὰ ὅρια τά</note> instead of "the boundaries" <note>original: τὰ ὅρια τάς</note> 45, 17. "In the sabbaths" <note>original: ἐν τοῖς σαββάτοις</note> instead of "in the sabbaths" <note>feminine gender error</note> in Daniel 5, 4. "Of stone" <note>original: λιθίνους</note> instead of "of stone" <note>original: ληθίνους</note> 5, 23. "Of his house" <note>original: τοῦ οἴκου αὐτοῦ</note> instead of "of the house." The editors themselves added "his" with a pen. 6, 25. "In all the earth" <note>original: ἐν πασῇ τῇ γῇ</note> instead of "in all [plural] the earth" (which Van Ess faithfully preserves).

1 Maccabees 2, 66. "He shall fight the battle" <note>original: πολεμήσει πόλεμον</note> instead of "the battle shall fight" 3, 37. "Those left behind" <note>original: τὰς καταλειφθείσας</note> instead of "those left behind" <note>misspelling</note> 3, 45. "Lodging" <note>original: κατάλυμα</note> instead of "lodging" <note>original: κατάλυμμα</note> 3, 49. "He leaped in" <note>original: ἐνεπήδησεν</note> instead of "he leaped in" <note>misspelling</note>. It seems—

<gloss>1) That there is no negligence underlying this is clear from the commentary of the Roman edition, which states: "[if you give for sin]. So Saint Jerome reads, and so also Cyril. However, several books have 'it may be given,' which Justin Martyr also has."</gloss>

<gloss>2) Jeremiah 13, 19. "He was deported" <note>original: ἀποικίσθη</note> was changed neither by the Roman editors nor by us. # XLII

-> PREFACE <-

Indeed, in the Roman edition itself, there was an *eta* [η], not an *iota* [ι], except that half of the letter vanished in the printing type 1). 15, 10. *the powers* <note>original: αἱ δυνάμεις</note> instead of *the powers* <note>original: αἱ δυνάμις</note> in 2 Maccabees 3, 13. *entirely* <note>original: πάντως</note> from a correction of the pen instead of *of all* <note>original: πάντων</note> 9, 24. *the country* <note>original: τὴν χώραν</note> instead of *the country* <note>original: τ. χώρην</note> 11, 4. *with the ten-thousands* <note>original: ταῖς μυριάσι</note> instead of *with the ten-thousands* <note>original: τ. μυριάσει</note> 13, 15. *holding the first place* <note>original: πρωτεύοντα</note> instead of *holding the first place* <note>original: πρωτεύνοντα</note> 14, 8. *belonging to* <note>original: ἀνηκόντων</note> instead of *belonging to* <note>original: ἀνηκότων</note> 14, 42. likewise 3 Maccabees 3, 16. *to the sinners* <note>original: ἀλιτηρίοις</note> instead of *to the sinners* <note>original: ἀλητηρίοις</note> 15, 4. In the word *having declared* <note>original: ἀποφηναμένων</note> the letters *phen* [φην] were written over an erasure. What was there before, I do not see. 15, 30. *protagonist* <note>original: πρωταγωνιστής</note> instead of *protagonist* <note>original: πρωταγονιστής</note> 3 Maccabees 2) 1, 23. *boldly* <note>original: θαρσαλέως</note> instead of *boldly* <note>original: θαββαλλέως</note> 4, 2. *destructive* <note>original: ὀλεθρίαν</note> from a correction of the pen instead of *destructive* <note>original: ὀλετρίαν</note> 4, 5. *covered* <note>original: πεπυκασμένων</note> instead of *covered* <note>original: πεπηκασμένων</note> 5, 19. *to have led* <note>original: ἀγηοχέναι</note> instead of *to have led* <note>original: ἡγηοχέναι</note>. Perhaps, however, there will be those who think the form *hegeochenai* <note>original: ἡγηοχέναι</note>, to which the reading of the Alexandrian manuscript *hegiochenai* <note>original: ἡγιοχέναι</note> comes very close, should not be entirely rejected.

Furthermore, in several places we have restored *Moses* <note>original: Μωυσῆς</note> (as in Jeremiah 15, 1; Micah 6, 4), *courage* <note>original: ἀνδρεία</note>, *service* <note>original: λειτουργία</note> (as in 1 Chronicles 23, 24, 26, 28; 24, 3), which the Roman edition usually has, instead of *Moses* <note>original: Μωσῆς</note>, *courage* <note>original: ἀνδρία</note>, *service* <note>original: λειτουργεία</note>; likewise *ninety* <note>original: ἐνενήκοντα</note> instead of *ninety* <note>original: ἐννενήκοντα</note> in Genesis 5, 9 (that very form follows in verse 17 of the same chapter). But I regret that we have not restored the same everywhere. Likewise, it would have been better, unless I am mistaken, not to admit the double forms *ninth* <note>original: ἔνατος and ἔννατος</note> and *ever-flowing* <note>original: ἀέναος and ἀένναος</note>. But most or all things of that kind cannot be reduced to one and the same form unless a new and accurate revision of the entire text is undertaken. For although the variation seen in *capturing alive* <note>original: ζωγρεία and ζωγρία</note>, *idleness* <note>original: ἀργία and ἀργεία</note>, *pride* <note>original: ὑπερηφανία and ὑπερηφάνεια</note> 3), *cakes* <note>original: χαυῶνες and καυῶνες</note>, *ornaments* <note>original: χλιδῶνες and χλιδόνες</note>, *discovery* <note>original: εὕρεμα and εὕρημα</note>, *system* <note>original: σύστημα and σύστεμα</note>, *to guard* <note>original: φυλάσσειν and φυλάττειν</note>, and similar words may seem slight: the strange variety of proper names presents no small difficulty. This variety is so widespread that you might doubt whether one name matches the other; it is mostly conspicuous in the very free exchange or doubling of letters (especially related ones) as well as the reversal of syllables. Examples are: *Abineem* and *Abineim*, *Abessai* and *Amessai*, *Ambram* and *Amram*, *Achimelech* and *Abimelech*, *Achinaam* and *Achinoom*, *Aunan* and *Aun*, *Baltam* and *Baltan*, *Baithleem* and *Bethleem*, *Bethsamys* and *Baithsamys*, *Barimoth Kadesh* and *Marimoth Kadem*, *Geon* and *Gion*, *Gerson* and *Gedson*, *Edrain* and...

---

1) There is a similar case in 1 Maccabees 14, 9, where Essius edited *they sat* <note>original: κάθηντο</note>. The Roman edition, however, has *they were sitting* <note>original: ἐκάθηντο</note>, except that the *e* [ἐ] is unusually pale and almost vanished, at least in our copy.
2) The Roman edition does not have the third book of Maccabees divided into chapters.
3) Since in the earlier books *pride* <note>original: ὑπερηφάνεια</note> is usually read, we have noted later in the remarks on the reading of the Alexandrian manuscript <note>original: ὑπερηφανία</note> that the same form is in the text itself. Moreover, even the Alexandrian manuscript does not always have the same form; cf. Amos 8, 7.

# PROLEGOMENA.

Edraein, Eliakim and Eliakeim, Ephod and Ephoud, Thiras and Theiras, Jezrael and Jezrael, Jessemou and Jessaimou, Kitieon and Kitiaion, Kison, Kisson and Keison, Mageddo and Mangedo, Madiam and Madian, Malaa and Maala, Maresa and Marisa, Melchiel and Melchil, Merari and Merarei, Medeia and Media, Modein and Modeeim, Nineveh and Ninevi, Nadinm and Nadanm, Omousi, Omousei and ho Mousi, Rapsakes and Rabsakes, Ragau and Ragan, Sepharvaim <note>original: Σεπφαρουαϊμ</note> $^1$), Sepharvain, Empharvaim $^2$), Selom and Selo, Sidon and Seidon, Solomon, Salomon, Salomon, Phallos and Phallous, Phison and Pheison, Chetieim, Chettieim, Chetteieim, Charmi and Charmei. Although this variety carries enough weight regarding the question of the unity of the entire Greek translation of the Old Testament <note>The Septuagint</note>, and furthermore, since it is not sufficiently certain whether books written originally in Greek should be held to the same standard as those translated into Greek, I nevertheless trust that most of these names will eventually emerge much differently than they are now after a diligent revision. For now, one had to be satisfied with the fact that the readings of the **Alexandrian Codex**, as well as those of the **Friderico-Augustanus** and the **Parisian Palimpsest** <term>rescriptus: a "palimpsest" or erased and reused manuscript</term> placed beneath the **Vatican** text, have often shown an easy path for emendation; although men devoted to textual criticism will well know that even the most excellent codices do not always exhibit the same names or the same words in the same way.

XVII. Having received these emendations into the text, let us subjoin some other readings which are scarcely in doubt as being superior to the Roman ones. Most are of such a nature as to prove how great the excellence of the **Alexandrian Codex** is in correcting the Roman reading $^3$); and indeed, not a few already [pleased] those same...

---

<note>1) 2 Kings 18:34. We have certainly corrected the spelling to -roua-, which is read consistently elsewhere, instead of -oura-.</note>
<note>2) We have restored the ending -aim in Isaiah 36:19 and 37:13 instead of -alm, since the same name in 2 Kings 18 and 19 was published everywhere with diaeresis points. Moreover, a strange variety of readings is visible in these parallel passages: 2 Kings 18–19 and Isaiah 36–37. In this manner, they certainly could not have been written by the same translator.</note>
<note>3) I will briefly touch upon two very difficult passages which the sharp mind of Grabius also approached with conjectures: Genesis 31:7 and 41; Amos 3:12. In the former passages of Genesis, one reads "ten lambs" <note>original: δέκα ἀμνῶν</note> (one manuscript in Holmes reads "minas" <note>original: μνῶν</note>) and "ten female lambs." Jerome already taught that this was a poor reading with these words: <quote>"Instead of what we have placed as 'ten times,' the Seventy translators <note>The Septuagint</note> placed 'ten lambs,' led by I know not what opinion."</quote> But Grabius responds to this: <quote>"Yet it is not the Seventy but the scribes who are at fault, if our emendation is correct."</quote> For he emends it to "ten minas" <note>units of weight/currency</note> and "ten minas," comparing 1 Kings 10:17, Ezra 2:69, Nehemiah 7:71 sq. (Ezekiel 45:12) where the Seventy seem to have rendered the Hebrew *manim* <note>original: מנים</note> as "minas" <note>original: μνᾶς</note> just as in these Genesis passages. If these are correct, for a similar permutation of vowels one should compare the famous passages of Psalm 87:11 and Isaiah 26:14. In the other passage of Amos, "priests" <note>original: ἱερεῖς</note> is usually read, joined with "hear ye." Grabius published "Jereis," joined with "in Damascus." (In which matter he did poorly because he did not note that in the Alexandrian Codex "priests" is clearly joined with "hear ye.") For Grabius [believed] the Seventy rendered the Hebrew *ers* (which to Lucian would be "couch") as "Jereis"...</note>

It includes specific philological debates regarding the translation of Hebrew units of measure and nouns in Genesis and Amos.</summary>
