# XLIV
## PROLEGOMENA.

...they have appeared, with which, as we have noted, we ourselves have corrected our text in various places.

*Exodus* 2, 8. One must certainly read "the young woman" <note>original Greek: ἡ νεᾶνις</note>, as it is praised from the Vatican codex itself in the work of Holmes. 8, 29. After "the dog-fly" <note>original Greek: ἡ κυνόμυια</note>, the phrase "from you" <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ σοῦ</note> has unluckily dropped out, which the *Alexandrian* codex and many others testify to. The same words are said to be read before "the dog-fly" in the Vatican itself according to Holmes. As for what many other witnesses have: "from Pharaoh" <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ Φαραώ</note>, that seems to have been introduced into the text after the genuine "from you" had already fallen out. *Leviticus* 5, 4. The strange reading "the lawless she" <note>original Greek: ἡ ἄνομος ἡ</note>, which the *Alexandrian* codex clearly confirms (for over the first "the" a small line expresses the breathing mark, and before the second "the" there is punctuation), must be corrected from many other witnesses without changing a single letter to: "or if he should swear" <note>original Greek: ἢ ἂν ὁμόσῃ</note>. 11, 5. With the *Alexandrian*, read "because it brings up" <note>original Greek: ὅτι ἀν'γε</note> instead of "because it does not bring up" <note>original Greek: ὅτι οὐκ ἀνάγει</note>, just as Nobilius, the editor of the old Latin text, already says in his notes on this passage that "not" <note>original Greek: οὐκ</note> crept into certain Greek books through error. 26, 22. With the *Alexandrian* codex, one should read "I will send" <note>future tense: ἀποστελῶ</note> instead of "I am sending" <note>present tense: ἀποστέλλω</note>. *Numbers* 22, 34. "To please" <note>infinitive: ἀρέσαι</note>, the reading of the Alexandrian codex, is much superior to the Roman reading "it will please" <note>future: ἀρέσει</note>. 30, 7. Before "whom he defined" <note>original Greek: οὓς ὡρίσατο</note>, the words "and the definitions" <note>original Greek: καὶ οἱ ὁρισμοί</note> seem entirely missing, although Holmes cites them from no codex. Otherwise, the *Alexandrian* codex with many others heals the passage by substituting "whatever things" <note>original Greek: ὅσα</note> for "whom" <note>original Greek: οὓς</note>. *Deuteronomy* 19, 19. With the *Alexandrian*, read "the evil man" <note>masculine: τὸν πονηρόν</note> instead of "the evil thing" <note>neuter: τὸ πονηρόν</note>. 21, 12. With the same, read "you shall bring in" <note>indicative: εἰσάξεις</note> instead of "you might bring in" <note>subjunctive: εἰσάξης</note>.

*Joshua* 10, 21. "Healthy" <note>plural: ὑγιείς</note> can indeed be tolerated; but since ancient manuscript books so often confuse the vowels "ei" and "e" <note>epsilon-iota and eta</note>, I consider that "healthy" <note>singular: ὑγιής</note> ¹) should be restored. 15, 8. Correct with the *Alexandrian* to "toward the north" <note>accusative: ἐπὶ βορρᾶν</note> instead of "upon the north" <note>genitive: ἐπὶ βορρᾶ</note>. 22, 32. With the *Alexandrian*, read "from the land of Gilead" <note>using the noun: ἐκ γῆς Γαλαάδ</note> instead of "from the [land] of Gilead" <note>using the article: ἐκ τῆς Γαλαάδ</note>.

*Judges* 6, 32. I prefer "him" <note>masculine: αὐτόν</note> instead of "it" <note>neuter: αὐτό</note>. 15, 6. I do not know whether "to the [one] of the friends" <note>using the Ionic/Epic form: τῳ</note> is better than "to the [one] of the friends" <note>standard form: τῷ</note>, if indeed that Attic form is to be tolerated among the Seventy <note>the Septuagint translators</note>.

*I Kings* <note>I Samuel</note> 4, 3. Restore "he struck" <note>ἔπαισεν</note> instead of "he stumbled" <note>ἔπταισεν</note> (which even Grabe did not change). 23, 1. "The threshing floors" <note>τοὺς ἄλω</note>, which is in the *Alexandrian* codex itself, seems faulty for "the threshing floors" <note>standard forms: τοὺς ἄλως or ἅλωας</note>. *II Kings* <note>II Samuel</note> 6, 17. With the *Alexandrian*, read "and peace offerings" <note>καὶ εἰρηνικάς</note>. *III Kings* <note>I Kings</note> 2, 28. For "having called" <note>κεκληκώς</note>, correct with the *Alexandrian* to "having turned aside" <note>κεκλικώς</note> ²) 2, 30. With the *Alexandrian*, read "I will go" <note>πορεύσομαι</note> instead of...

---

...they seemed. This is sufficiently confirmed by similar instances. But then again, Jerome already testified to a corrupt interpretation of the passage in these words: *“What is placed at the beginning of the chapter according to the Septuagint as ‘priest,’ is not held in the Hebrew; but instead of this word is read* ערשׂ <note>Hebrew: 'eres'</note>, *which Aquila translated as ‘couch’ [grabatum].”* And I think the Seventy themselves put down the Hebrew word, which some, not understanding, replaced with "priests" <note>Greek: ἱερεῖς</note> instead of "couch" <note>Hebrew: ערשׂ</note>. Hence, the conjecture of Jerome himself, which Grabe published, is commended.

<margin>
1) Grabe and Breitinger did not correctly draw this from the *Alexandrian*; for it confirms "healthy" <note>ὑγιείς</note>.
2) It is strange that this reading was not only not admitted into the text by Grabe, but was not even transcribed from the Alexandrian codex.
</margin>

# PROLEGOMENA

I go <note>original: "πορεύομαι"</note> 8, 59. Following the Alexandrian manuscript <note>The Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century Greek manuscript of the Bible</note>, read "whom" <note>original: "οὓς"</note> instead of "as" <note>original: "ὡς"</note> 15, 30. I would prefer to read "as" <note>original: "ὡς"</note> instead of "who" <note>original: "ὃς"</note>, comparing the passage 16, 13. IV Kings 5, 21 <note>In older scholarship, IV Kings refers to the book of II Kings in modern Bibles</note>. After "and he said, Peace," the second "and he said, Peace" has entirely fallen out, which the Alexandrian codex correctly adds. 17, 6. one should write "he settled" <note>original: "κατῴκισεν"</note> instead of "he dwelt" <note>original: "κατῴκησεν"</note> ¹); likewise 17, 11. "he removed" <note>original: "ἀπῴκισε"</note> with the Alexandrian instead of "he dwelt apart" <note>original: "ἀπῴκησε"</note>.

I Chronicles 21, 10. "I choose" <note>original: "αἱρῶ"</note> is published. It seems "I take up" <note>original: "αἴρω"</note> should be written, as it is in II Kings 24, 12. However, in the Hebrew text itself, in one place it reads "stretch out" <note>original: "נטה"</note>, and in the other "stretch out" <note>original: "נטה"</note>. II Chronicles 28, 3. "in the valley of Gehenna" <note>original: "ἐν γέ."</note> is strange. The Alexandrian reading "in the land" <note>original: "ἐν γῇ"</note> is better.

Job 8, 20. it is better to read "he will reject" <note>original: "ἀποποιήσεται"</note> with the Alexandrian than "he might reject" <note>original: "ἀποποιήσηται"</note> 28, 8. "her" <note>original: "αὐτὴν"</note> seems to need correction with manuscripts A and C <note>Codex Alexandrinus and Codex Ephraemi Rescriptus</note> instead of "him" <note>original: "αὐτόν"</note> 28, 12 and 13. I wonder whether "it was found" <note>original: "εὑρέθη"</note> should be exchanged with "it might be found" <note>original: "εὑρεθῇ"</note>. 34, 20. "of those being shut out" <note>original: "ἐκκλεινομένων"</note> is published. It seems either "of those being excluded" <note>original: "ἐκκλειομένων"</note> with the Alexandrian or "of those turning away" <note>original: "ἐκκλινομένων"</note> should be restored. 36, 29. instead of "if he understands the spreading out" <note>original: "ἐὰν συνῇ ἀπέκτασις"</note> ²) it seems "if he understands the spreadings out" <note>original: "ἐὰν συνῇ ἀπεκτάσεις"</note> should be written. The Roman edition <note>The 1587 Sixtine edition of the Septuagint</note> itself notes below the text: "Some books have 'extensions' <note>original: "ἐπεκτάσεις"</note>" 37, 3. "a voice shall cry" <note>original: "βοήσεται φωνή"</note> seems preferable to "he shall cry with a voice" <note>original: "β. φωνῇ"</note>.

Psalms 4, 7. "to us" <note>original: "ἡμῖν"</note> with the Alexandrian instead of "to you" <note>original: "ὑμῖν"</note>, likewise 113, 5. it is better to read "to you" <note>original: "σοί"</note> instead of "you" <note>original: "σύ"</note>. 104, 21. "of creation" <note>original: "κτίσεως"</note>, although the Alexandrian and others have the same (but Parsons <note>James Parsons, an 18th-century editor of the Septuagint</note> seems to have confused it), is published due to an itacism <note>A common spelling error in Greek where different vowels are pronounced the same</note> for "of possession" <note>original: "κτήσεως"</note> ³). 130, 2. I would prefer "recompense" <note>original: "ἀνταπόδοσις"</note> (Alexandrian: "recompenses" <note>original: "ἀνταποδοσεις"</note>) over the received reading "you will repay" <note>original: "ἀνταποδώσεις"</note> 138, 9. correct with the Alexandrian to "at dawn" <note>original: "κατ’ ὄρθρον"</note> instead of "rightly" <note>original: "κατ’ ὀρθόν"</note> 142. in the inscription I wonder if "he pursued" <note>original: "κατεδίωκεν"</note> is better than "he pursues" <note>original: "κατεδιώκει"</note>.

Proverbs 8, 8. with the Alexandrian read "in them" <note>original: "ἐν αὐτοῖς"</note> instead of "themselves" <note>original: "ἑαυτοῖς"</note> 9, 18. read "eye" <note>original: "ὄμμα"</note> with the Alexandrian instead of "name" <note>original: "ὄνομα"</note> 12, 18. "with a sword" <note>original: "μαχαίρᾳ"</note> seems to be restored instead of "swords" <note>original: "μάχαιραι"</note>. The Alexandrian codex has "a sword" <note>original: "μάχαιραν"</note>. 16, 30. restore "he considers" <note>original: "διαλογίζεται"</note> or with manuscripts A and C "he reckons" <note>original: "λογίζεται"</note> instead of "he will consider" <note>original: "διαλογίσεται"</note> 18, 4. both the Roman edition and the Alexandrian codex support "it gushes up" <note>original: "ἀναπηδύει"</note>. However, it is probable that this was placed by itacism for "it springs forth" <note>original: "ἀναπιδύει"</note>. 24, 73. (31, 5.) "they forget" <note>original: "ἐπιλάθονται"</note> (concerning which form see Stephanus's Thesaurus <note>A famous 16th-century Greek dictionary</note>) seems to be changed to "they might forget" <note>original: "ἐπιλάθωνται"</note>.

Wisdom of Solomon 19, 4. with manuscripts A and C read "they might fulfill in addition" <note>original: "προσαναπληρώσωσι"</note> instead of "they might fulfill beforehand" <note>original: "προαναπληρώσωσι"</note>.

Sirach 40, 10. read "physician" <note>original: "ἰατρόν"</note> [accusative] for "physician" <note>original: "ἰατρός"</note> [nominative] 37, 5. for "of war" <note>original: "πολέμου"</note> substitute "of an enemy" <note>original: "πολεμίου"</note> 38, 22. the Alexandrian reading "thus" <note>original: "οὕτως"</note> is better than the Roman "even as" <note>original: "οὕτω ὡς"</note>; likewise 38, 28. "it will melt" <note>original: "τήξει"</note> in the Alexandrian codex is better than the received "it will fix" <note>original: "πήξει"</note> 40, 1. with manuscripts A and C one should read "of burial" <note>original: "ἐπιταφῆς"</note> instead of "at the burial" <note>original: "ἐπὶ ταφῇ"</note> 43, 23. the reading confirmed by A and C themselves is strange:

---

1) The Alexandrian codex also supports "he dwelt" <note>original: "κατῴκησεν"</note>, not "he settled" <note>original: "κατῴκισεν"</note> as Grabe <note>John Ernest Grabe, a 17th-century biblical scholar</note> reports.
2) In this place I have retained the punctuation of the Vatican edition, since the passage did not seem able to be restored even by changing the punctuation.
3) Regarding Psalm 103, 24. I feel differently. Grabe indeed thinks "of possession" <note>original: "κτήσεως"</note> should be emended again, after a learned commentary placed in the Prolegomena, which see.

<meta>This page continues the critical prolegomena (preface), focusing on specific textual emendations for the Greek Old Testament (Septuagint) based on various manuscripts and scholarly conjectures.</meta>

# PREFACE

"Jesus planted it." <note>original: ἐφύτευσεν αὐτὴν Ἰησοῦς</note> It should be read as "He planted islands in it" <note>original: ἐφύτευσεν ἐν αὐτῇ νήσους</note> 43, 31. I am unsure whether "he will magnify" <note>original: μεγαλυνεῖ</note> is better than "he magnifies" <note>original: μεγαλύνει</note> 45, 24. It should be read "to preside" <note>original: προστατεῖν</note> instead of "protector" <note>original: προστάτην</note>.

In Hosea 14:1, correct it according to the Alexandrian Codex <note>A 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible, one of the most important for textual criticism</note> to "you have grown weak" <note>singular; original: ἠσθένησας</note> instead of "they have grown weak" <note>plural; original: ἠσθένησαν</note>. In Habakkuk 2:18, instead of "a carved image, something..." <note>original: γλυπτὸν, τι</note> (as it clearly appears in the Roman edition), it seems it should be read "a carved image, because..." <note>original: γλυπτὸν ὅτι</note> following the Alexandrian Codex. In Isaiah 30:22, following the same, instead of "you might make... and you might winnow" <note>subjunctive; original: ποιήσῃς κ. λικμήσῃς</note> one should restore "you will make... and you will winnow" <note>future indicative; original: ποιήσεις κ. λικμήσεις</note>. In 30:30, it seems "he will show" <note>original: δείξει</note> should be written instead of "to show" <note>original: δεῖξαι</note>, even though the Alexandrian Codex is against it. In 53:10, "it is given" <note>original: δῶται</note> seems it should be restored along with "our" <note>original: ἡμῶν</note>. Concerning this passage, see above. In Jeremiah 22:15, read "you shall reign" <note>original: βασιλεύσεις</note> with the Alexandrian and the <term>FA</term> <note>likely referring to the Codex Friderico-Augustanus, part of the Codex Sinaiticus</note> instead of "you might reign" <note>original: βασιλεύσῃς</note>; likewise in 22:18, instead of "And upon" <note>original: Καὶ ἐπί</note> read "Woe upon" <note>original: Οὐαὶ ἐπί</note> with the Alexandrian (in FA neither is present; see the commentary). In 23:32, omit "not" <note>original: οὐ</note> with the Alexandrian and FA. In 36:28, the first "he sent" <note>original: ἀπέστειλε</note> seems it should be deleted. In Ezekiel 16:41, "you should give" <note>original: δῷς</note> should be put back from the Alexandrian instead of "I will give" <note>original: δώσω</note>; likewise in 17:16, "he will lead" <note>original: ἄξει</note> instead of "you might lead" <note>original: ἄξῃ</note>. In 40:29, correct "the porch" <note>original: τῷ αἰλαμμών</note> to "the porches" <note>original: τὰ αἰλ.</note> with the Alexandrian. In Daniel 9:16, the reading of the Alexandrian Codex "in all mercy" <note>original: ἐν πάσῃ ἐλεημοσύνῃ</note> is better than the received one: "in all [things] mercy" <note>original: ἐν πᾶσιν ἐλεημοσύνῃ</note>.

Furthermore, many other things have been brought forward for the purpose of emending the Greek texts of the Old Testament, both by several others and by Schleusner <note>Johann Friedrich Schleusner (1759–1831), a German biblical scholar known for his lexicons</note> (in his critical minor works on the Greek versions of the Old Testament, 1812). Although he may seem here and there not to have restored the translators themselves but to have corrected them from the Hebrew source, he will nevertheless greatly assist anyone undertaking a new recension of the text; for he observed not a few things that are not only clever but are also recommended by great probability. To provide the means of proving this, I will add some examples, even though I have already noted here and there things that did not escape his sharp mind. These were either first proposed by him or confirmed by his recommendation.

In Genesis 19:33 and 35, he rightly prefers the reading of many manuscripts "her" <note>feminine; original: αὐτὴν</note> over the received "him" <note>masculine; original: αὐτόν</note>. In Exodus 16:14, he proposes "fine" <note>original: λεπτόν</note> instead of "white" <note>original: λευκόν</note>. In Judges 5:16, he strives to heal the text in three ways; for he wants "you sat," "double-corners," and "of flocks" <note>original: ἐκάθισας, διγωνίας, ἀγελῶν</note> to be read (the last has already been substituted for the Vatican reading by Lambert Bos <note>A Dutch scholar (1670–1717) who edited the Septuagint</note> and his followers). In 1 Samuel 11:7, he rightly recommends "they went" <note>original: ἔβησαν</note> instead of "they cried out" <note>original: ἐβόησαν</note>, and again in 13:4, "they cried out" <note>original: ἀνεβόησαν</note> instead of "they went up" <note>original: ἀνέβησαν</note>. In 2 Kings 23:5 and 11, he thinks "he stopped" <note>original: κατέπαυσε</note> should be read instead of "he burned" <note>original: κατέκαυσε</note> (cf. below on Job 3:17). In 1 Chronicles 4:40, he prefers "fat" <note>original: πίονας</note> rather than "more" <note>original: πλείονας</note> (very often written as <note>original: πλίονας</note>); he applies this same thing to Isaiah 17:4 and Psalm 77:35. In Job 4:6, he recommends "innocence" <note>original: ἀκακία</note> instead of "evil" <note>original: κακία</note>; in the same book at 11:12, "is sober" <note>original: νήφεται</note> instead of "swims" <note>original: νήχεται</note>; 13:16, "deceitful" <note>original: δόλιος</note> instead of "deceit" <note>original: δόλος</note>; 42:18, "thus" <note>original: οὕτως</note> instead of "this man" <note>original: οὗτος</note>. In Psalm 47:9, he proposes "of the temple" <note>original: ναοῦ</note> instead of "of the people" <note>original: λαοῦ</note>, likewise in Sirach 49:12, "temple" <note>original: ναόν</note> instead of "people" <note>original: λαόν</note> ¹) 58, 4.

---

1) Thus in Psalm 26:4, in the very ancient London papyrus codex recently edited by me in the *New Collection of Unedited Sacred Monuments*, "his people" <note>original: τον λαον αυτου</note> is written instead of "his temple" <note>original: τον ναον αυτου</note>.

# PROLEGOMENA.
<note>Latin: "Prolegomena" refers to the introductory remarks or preface of a scholarly work.</note>

He wishes to read *they directed* instead of *he directed*. At 63, 7, he recommends *you shall search out* instead of *he shall search out*. For my part, I would prefer *a searching out* <note>original: "exereunesin"</note> (which is the very word found in the ancient Verona Psalter), since the "n" in *exereunesin* (as it is often written) could very easily have been absorbed by the following "p". At 69, 1, he proposes: *O God, attend to saving me; O Lord, attend to my help.* For my part, departing hardly at all from the published text, I think it should be read thus: *To save me, O Lord; O God, attend to...* etc. At 78, 9, he wants *and* to be restored in place of *O Lord*. Most correctly; for often the abbreviations for "Lord" and "and" <note>original: "κ̅ε̅ and και"</note> are confused in handwritten books. At Proverbs 9, 12, he wants to read *you shall draw out* or *you shall endure* or, with Grabe, *you shall bear up*. For my part, I side with Grabe. At 15, 10, he wants *instruction of the wicked* to be restored instead of *instruction of the innocent*, an emendation which presents no difficulty according to the laws of criticism. At 26, 7, he wants *proverb* instead of *lawlessness*; likewise at 28, 28, *they cover* instead of *they groan*. At Canticles 1, 7, he recommends *of others* instead of *of companions*, which is the very reading in Codex C. At 6, 11, he recommends *I knew* <note>first person</note> instead of *he knew* <note>third person</note>. Certainly the ancient forms of the letters "eta" and "upsilon" are very similar. At Jeremiah 5, 31, he prefers *they prevailed* rather than *they clapped*; and again at Amos 6, 5, *clapping* instead of *prevailing*; at Nahum 3, 12, *against the rulers* instead of *ruling*; at Malachi 1, 10, *you shall kindle* <note>active</note> instead of *it shall be kindled* <note>passive</note>. 

Repeatedly he shows that words consisting of transcribed Hebrew letters were distorted by scribes, not by the original translators: for the latter gave, at 3 Kings 18, 32, 35, 38, *thaalan* <note>a trench</note>, not *thalassan* <note>the sea</note>; at 2 Chronicles 3, 4, *aulam* <note>porch</note>, not *ailam*; at Esther 9, 26, 28, 29, *phourai* <note>Purim</note>, not *phrourai* <note>guards</note>; at Isaiah 66, 19, *Phoul*, not *Phoud*; at Jeremiah 17, 26, *manaa* <note>offering</note>, not *manna*. Indeed, he orders many things to be received into the text which the Roman editors already showed in their notes, such as Genesis 15, 15, *buried* instead of *nourished* (The Roman Edition says: "All ancient manuscripts have *nourished*; nowhere is *buried* found." They therefore determined that the fault was not that of the scribes but of the translators, nor has a codex been found that departs from the common reading. Nevertheless, the Complutensian edition has *buried*, and from there, on the authority of Boeckel, the recent Polyglot edition of Theile has it too.); Job 3, 17, *they rested* instead of *they burned*; Psalms 88, 21 and 91, 10, *with oil* instead of *with mercy*; Ecclesiastes 7, 19, *do not let go* instead of *do not defile*.

## XVIII.

And these remarks indeed concern individual passages of the Vatican text which either demand a critical hand or have already experienced one. However, an account must also be rendered regarding the book of the prophet Daniel, with the additions concerning Susanna and concerning Bel and the Dragon, which we have added at the end of the Vatican text. Jerome is witness to the fact that even in Christian antiquity, the Greek text of the book of Daniel was usually read not from the translation of the Seventy <term>Septuagint: The Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</term> but according to the edition of Theodotion <term>Theodotion: A 2nd-century scholar who produced a Greek version of the Old Testament</term>; in Jerome's preface to Daniel, these words are read:

> "The churches do not read the prophet Daniel according to the Seventy translators, using instead the edition of Theodotion; and why this happened, I do not know."

The cause of this matter, which Jerome professed himself to be ignorant of in the final words we have cited, [continues...]

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly introduction (Prolegomena) regarding the textual history of the Book of Daniel, specifically focusing on the transition from the original Septuagint version to the version by Theodotion.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA

There have been those who claimed the reason for this <note>the loss of the original Septuagint version of Daniel</note> was that Origen <note>A 3rd-century Christian scholar and theologian</note> did not include the Septuagint version of Daniel in his *Hexapla* <note>original: "hexaplare opus"; a massive six-columned comparative Bible</note> because it differed so significantly from the Hebrew original and other translations. Instead, he substituted the text of Theodotion, which was more similar to the other versions. Since the practice of the entire Church depended so heavily on that famous work of Origen, it happened that Theodotion’s text was soon received into common use, while the other was neglected by most. However, it was not entirely neglected by Origen himself, but was included in the *Tetrapla* <note>A four-columned version of Origen's work</note>, "where," in the words of Simon de Magistris, "since the Hebrew text was absent, that variation, even if detected, could not be criticized." Yet the *Tetrapla* was accustomed to being copied much less frequently than the *Hexapla* text. This explanation suffers chiefly because it relies on a doubtful distinction between the *Hexapla* and the *Tetrapla*. This much is certain: it is thanks to Origen himself that, in the previous century, the Septuagint version of Daniel could be brought to light in Rome from the Chisian manuscript <note>Codex Chisianus</note> after a long period of oblivion; for the subscription <note>a concluding note by a scribe</note> attached to the last chapter of Daniel testifies that this manuscript was drawn from Origen’s *Tetrapla*. See page 642 of our edition.

Therefore, since the text of Daniel in the Vatican edition was presented according to Theodotion (and repeated by us accordingly), while all other books were taken from the so-called Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint (LXX)</note>, it seemed appropriate to us to add that text of Daniel which is attributed to those same Seventy Interpreters as an appendix. In this matter, following the lead of others,¹ we have followed: 1) the Roman edition of Simon de Magistris, 2) but corrected by a collation of the manuscript itself presented in Holmes, 3) and not neglecting those things which, sixteen years later, from the Syriac text—itself also from the *Tetrapla*—from the Mediolanean <note>Milan</note> manuscript...

---

1) I refer to Michaelis (Göttingen 1773, again 1774), Segaar (Utrecht 1775), Parsons (in volume II of Holmes’s edition of the Septuagint, 1818), and the Oxford editor (1848).

2) *Daniel according to the Seventy, now published for the first time from the Tetrapla of Origen from the unique Chisian manuscript of more than 1100 years.* Rome, 1772. <note>original: "annorum supra IↃCCC" refers to the age of the manuscript</note>. Furthermore, from the same manuscript in the year 1840, the book of Ezekiel appeared in the same place, provided with a learned apparatus. I saw the printed book in the Chisian library itself in the year 1843; but at that time no copy had left the borders of Italy.

3) This is Holmes’s manuscript no. 88. Regarding its age, Holmes, or rather Parsons, was not quite consistent. For after he said regarding Daniel that it was of the 11th century (did he mean the 9th?), he notes regarding Jeremiah: "It seems to have been transcribed in the year 880." To me, examining it myself in the year 1848, it seemed to be of approximately the eleventh century. Regarding the relationship of the Roman edition to the manuscript itself, this note by James Parsons is significant: "expressed, as far as the text is concerned, as it seems, the Septuagint version from a copy of my manuscript 88, drawn up for the use of the illustrious Francesco Bianchini." Hence, those things in the Roman edition which differ from the Holmes collation seem to be based on a mere error of the copyist. They certainly seem so.

-># PREFACE.<-

published in Milan by Gaetano Bugati <note>1) original: "Caietano Bugato"</note>), could be drawn upon to correct the Chisian text. However, we have employed the Syriac version chiefly under the guidance of Heinrich August Hahn, who, in his recent edition of Daniel <note>2)</note>, was not content merely to correct the Greek from the Syriac here and there, but also learnedly attempted to restore passages that were corrupted in both manuscripts <note>3)</note> <note>4)</note>. For our own part, according to our established plan, we have preferred to publish for the most part the Greek readings of the Chisian manuscript—noting the Origenic signs beneath the text along with the variations in the Syriac—rather than publishing a critically reconstructed version of the original text itself. This is because, due to the scarcity and nature of the witnesses, such a reconstruction can be achieved with some probability in places, but not everywhere.

Regarding the Origenic signs <note>Technical symbols used by the scholar Origen in the 3rd century to mark differences between the Hebrew Bible and the Greek translation</note> expressed by us in the commentary, the following must be noted. The most frequent is the ※ **asterisk**: Origen placed this before those passages which, although they were read in the Hebrew, had not been rendered by the Seventy translators <note>The "Seventy" refers to the Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>; for that reason, they were adopted by Origen himself from other translators, especially from Theodotion. He intended the ÷ **obelus** to have the opposite function; for he placed that before passages which the Seventy translators included despite the Hebrew text. While everyone agrees on these two signs, there is great uncertainty regarding the ÷ **lemniscus** and the ⸮ **hypolemniscus** <note>5)</note>. If Isidore of Seville is to be believed, they were placed for the purpose of indicating that the translators agree not in individual words, but in the same sense. If we proceed from this opinion, the lemniscus and hypolemniscus—to mention one theory among many—could differ from each other in that one signified that all three (Aquila, Symmachus, and Theodotion) agreed with the Seventy, while the other signified that only one of them departed from them <note>6)</note>. The remaining sign is /, which they called the **metobelum** <note>A mark used to indicate the end of a passage affected by an asterisk or obelus</note>. It is never by itself—

***

1) *Daniel according to the edition of the Seventy translators taken from the Tetrapla. Edited in Syriac from the Syro-Estrangelo manuscript of the Ambrosian library, translated into Latin and illustrated with critical notes by Gaetano Bugati. Milan 1788.*
2) *Daniel according to the Seventy. Edited from the Chisian manuscript after Segaar, revised according to the Syro-Hexaplaric version, and illustrated with critical and philological annotations by Henr. Aug. Hahn, Doctor of Philosophy and Candidate of Theology. Leipzig 1845.*
3) Among these, chapter VIII, verse 11 stands out.
4) We have rarely seen the most distinguished Hahn err in noting a reading of the manuscript, such as in II, 9; III, 21; IV, 5; XII, 14. Wherever we have printed the "movable nu" <note>A grammatical feature of Greek where the letter 'n' is added to the end of a word for sound</note> before a consonant, it has been repeated from the Roman edition, but I suspect it is found in the manuscript itself much more often than in the edition. In the Roman edition and among later editors, *Arioch* was poorly edited as *Arioche* and *Arioches* in II, 14 and 15; nor did we think *Ethiopia* in III, 1 should be repeated with them. On the other hand, we left *stypiou* <note>original: "στυπίου" - referring to tow or flax</note> untouched in III, 46, a word in which there is great variety in spelling.
5) In the Chisian manuscript, a <image-desc>horizontal line with dot above</image-desc> is also found, according to the Roman editor. This seems to be from a scribal error; indeed, we have sufficiently indicated through the earlier chapters how great the scribe's negligence was in placing all those signs.
6) Compare the arguments discussed by Montfaucon in the *Preliminaries to the Hexapla of Origen*, chapter IV, section IV (in Bahrdt’s edition, page 80 and following).

-># PREFACE.<-

is placed, but another sign which preceded it follows; it has, moreover, such force that it indicates the end of those words to which the prefixed sign belongs. <note>The author is describing the "metobelus," a mark used by ancient editors like Origen to show where a specific textual correction or addition ended.</note> In other books, such as in the very ancient Sarravian fragments of the Octateuch <note>The first eight books of the Old Testament.</note>, two dots were substituted in place of the metobelus.

XIX. I cannot leave this discussion without speaking briefly about the editions of the Seventy interpreters <note>The Septuagint (LXX).</note>, both older and more recent, which have followed the Vatican manuscript as their guide. The first is the Paris edition which appeared under the care of **Jean Morin** in the year 1628 (reissued in 1641 with only the first leaf changed). (**Pierre Morin** also played a part in preparing the Roman edition itself.) In this edition, the text of the Seventy interpreters was first divided into verses according to the standard of the Latin Vulgate. And alongside the Greek text was placed a Latin translation, which **Morin** repeated from the work of **Flaminius Nobilius** (*The Old Testament according to the Seventy rendered into Latin and published by the authority of Pope Sixtus V, with annotations and scholia, by the study and care of **Flaminius Nobilius** with his own preface, assisted by **Antonio Agelli**, **Laelius**, **Bartholomew Valverde**, and **Pierre Morin**. Rome 1588*), in which he had labored to restore the Old Latin or "Itala" version of the Greek text.

The second and third appeared in London in 1653 and 1657. The latter is part of the very famous polyglot work edited by **Brian Walton**. This also has the Old Latin placed beside the Greek text, compiled by the labor of **Flaminius Nobilius**. Furthermore, the editions of Walton and Morin agree in this: that they have the Roman text amended in various places, such as Genesis 19:5, "to us" instead of "to you"; 27:45, "from both of you" <note>original: "apo ton dyo hymon"</note> instead of "from both of us"; Exodus 10:26, "to our God" instead of "to your God"; Numbers 10:19, "Surisadai" instead of "Surisedai": which changes clearly appear to have been made laudably. Other things, however, are handled less correctly, such as Genesis 10:19, "Seboin" <note>ending in -n</note> instead of "Seboim" <note>ending in -m</note>; 41:39 and Deuteronomy 2:16, "since" <note>original: "epeidan"</note> instead of "since" <note>original: "epeide"</note>; Genesis 34:15, "of the son of Esau" instead of "the sons of Esau."

Much inferior to both of these is the third, that is, the second London edition published already before Walton's Bible in the year 1653. How greatly this departs from the Roman model with license and with how many faults it is deformed, **Lambert Bos** shows by citing a great number of examples in the Preface to his own edition, from which **Breitinger** also copied. Nevertheless, that London edition was repeated three times: first at Cambridge in 1665 with a preface by **John Pearson**, then at Amsterdam in 1683 under the care of **Johannes Leusden**, and thirdly at Leipzig in 1697 by the authors **Claverus** and **Klumpfius**. On the other hand, **Lambert Bos**, whose edition appeared at Franeker in Friesland in the year 1709, followed the example of Morin and Walton more closely. While he truly improved the Roman reading in many places, he often abandoned it for no suitable reason and without indicating the Roman reading.

...diversity of the version. Indeed, he also left many things unchanged which it would have been proper to conform to the corrections of the Roman edition itself, whether those made by pen or noted at the end of the book (as in 4 Kings 4:31 <note>original: "IV Βασ. 4, 31." - referring to the book of 2 Kings in modern Bibles</note>). Furthermore, he added a critical apparatus to the text, but it was quite imperfect, as Breitinger has already pointed out in the preface to his new edition of Grabe’s work. Upon this edition by Bos depended those of David Mill of Amsterdam in 1725; of Reineccius at Leipzig in 1730 and 1757 (compare, for example, the readings peculiar to Bos in Genesis 44:20 and 32; Exodus 1:4; Leviticus 23:39; Numbers 20:23, 30:5; Judges 5:16; on the other hand, he did not even correct the errors of the Roman edition in Deuteronomy 11:17 <term>pelachana</term> <note>original: "πελαχᾶνα" - a misspelling of the Greek word for pelican</note> [Leviticus 11:18 correctly has <term>pelekana</term>], 1 Kings 25:10 <term>peplethyménoi</term> <note>original: "πεπληθυμένοι" - meaning 'multiplied' or 'increased'</note>, Leviticus 8:26 <term>kai elaben</term> <note>original: "καὶ ἔλαβεν" - 'and he took'</note>, Numbers 26:35 [39] <term>demos ho Soudalan</term> <note>original: "δῆμος ὁ Σουδαλάν" - 'the people of Soudalan'</note>, etc.), as well as the Oxford edition of 1805. Similarly, the London edition printed at the Valpy house in 1819 is stated by its very title to be "taken from the editions of Holmes (see below) and Lambert Bos." The Venice edition, however, which appeared in 1822, was established according to the Vatican exemplar edited at Rome and the English one printed at London, as the title itself holds. Since the title of the Leipzig edition of 1697 contains exactly the same claim, it seems the Venetian editor primarily followed that one.

Four editions remain: the Leipzig stereotype edition of Leander van Ess, first published in 1824; the Paris edition of Jager in 1839; the Oxford edition of 1848 (in three duodecimo volumes), the title of which includes: "With the addition of the stronger variants of the Alexandrian manuscript"; and finally, the London edition printed very recently by Samuel Bagster and sons. I have not seen the most recent London edition. The Oxford edition is no more corrected than the others, examples of which we shall give later. The Paris edition was augmented by a Latin translation (that of Flaminius Nobilius) and, as stated in the title, by "observations and completions from other manuscripts." However, it suffers from such inconsistency that Lücke (in the *Göttingen Scholarly Announcements* 1840, Vol. I, page 647) rightly said that a man not entirely averse to critical methods could learn only one thing from it: how *not* to edit. Here and there, the Parisian editor did adopt corrections of the Vatican text such as those we reviewed above, as in Genesis 19:5, 20:14 and 16, 23:8, 27:45, 36:37, 41:1; but he left many more untouched, although some were already corrected by pen and others were undoubtedly faulty and absurd; compare, for example, these passages recorded in our index: Genesis 21:21, 33:18, 50:23; Exodus 35:7; Leviticus 8:26; Numbers 6:20, 10:19, 21:14 f., 26:39, 28:4; Deuteronomy 4:43, 14:17; 1 Kings 25:10. He even poorly retained things such as <term>laron</term> <note>original: "λᾶρον"</note> in Deuteronomy 14:15 and <term>laron</term> <note>original: "λάρον"</note> in Leviticus 11:15 <note>The author is noting inconsistent accentuation for the word 'gull'</note>; <term>Aunan</term> <note>original: "Αὐνάν"</note> in Genesis 14:13 and <term>Aunan</term> <note>original: "Αὐνᾶν"</note> in Genesis 14:24: which were indeed retained with the same inconsistency by Reineccius, Holmes, and others ¹).

---

<margin>¹) Likewise in the Oxford edition of 1848. In the same, although it is corrected here and there, as in Genesis 19:5, 23:8, 27:45, 33:18, 36:37, 41:1, nevertheless many more [errors remain].</margin>

# PREFACE

Finally, I must speak of the Leipzig edition which bears the name of Leander van Ess <note>Leander van Ess (1772–1847) was a German theologian known for his translation of the Bible</note>. I dealt with this at length in my previous edition of 1850, where on page 29 and following of the Preface, these words were written:
> "The truth is that an incredible faithfulness was maintained in that work even toward the obvious errors of the Vatican manuscript <note>original: "Vaticani exemplaris"</note>; as has already been indicated in several places above" 
(which I have even now repeated), 
> "so it will be most clear in far more instances if anyone wishes to compare the catalog of corrections just presented with the Ess edition. Furthermore, there is such a great number of errors which that edition claims entirely as its own that it far surpasses the Roman edition. Let us bring forward a few examples from the earlier books" 
etc. But I may no longer repeat these same points; for since I myself recently suggested to the most noble Karl Tauchnitz <note>A famous German printer and publisher</note>, in whose printing house the Ess edition remains in print, that the plates should be refined according to my notes, he has seen to it that this be done diligently. From this, a much more corrected edition is to be published very soon.

XX. I hope the **Summaries** <note>original: "Argumenta"</note> noted in the upper margin will be pleasing to those who use our edition. Writing these was often a matter of no small difficulty, given the constraints of space. Therefore, I did not neglect whatever assistance could be sought from the similar labors of others.

In adding the parallel passages of the New Testament, a distinction has been made between those in which a passage of the Old Testament is alluded to, and those in which a specific saying is either quoted or said to be quoted. To those which are allusions, we have prefixed an asterisk; to the others, we have not. Where something from the Old Testament is praised in the Synoptic Gospels, we have sometimes cited the location of only one, to which it would be easy to compare the twin passages of the rest; but usually, this very thing is indicated by the attached note "pp" (that is, *and parallel passages*).

XXI. **It remains** for us to explain the critical apparatus <term>critical apparatus: a system of notes at the bottom of a page documenting variations in different ancient manuscripts</term>. There is a great number of surviving Greek manuscripts which contain the text of the Old Testament¹); to the more than three hundred reviewed by Holmes <note>Robert Holmes (1748–1805), an English biblical scholar</note>, nearly a hundred others are added which were not yet noted by him. They are found scattered throughout Europe and Eastern lands, especially in Rome, Paris, Florence, Vienna, London, Oxford, and Venice. Most were written in small letters <note>original: "litteris minutis," referring to minuscule script</note> from the tenth century onwards; a few, of which Holmes mentions fifteen, from the fourth—

---

remained untouched, such as Genesis 20:14 and 16; 21:21; 50:23; Exodus 35:7; Leviticus 8:26; Numbers 6:20, 10:19, 21:14 and following, 26:39, 28:4; 1 Kings 25:10; Deuteronomy 14:17. In the final place, regarding the faulty reading *pelachana* <note>original: "πελαχᾶνα"</note>, the variant of the Alexandrian manuscript <note>Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century Greek Bible</note> is not even noted.

1) It is well known that even many more manuscripts of the New Testament survive; for they fill a count of a thousand.

# PROLEGOMENA
<note>original: "Prolegomena" — Preliminary remarks or an introduction to a scholarly work.</note>

...almost until the ninth or tenth century in uncial letters <note>Uncials are a style of writing using large, rounded capital letters, common in Greek and Latin manuscripts from the 4th to 8th centuries.</note>. Of all these, not even ten encompass the entire Old Testament; more than eighty contain either all or parts of the books of the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible.</note>, nearly one hundred and fifty contain the Psalms, nearly forty contain Isaiah and Daniel, nearly thirty contain Job, and nearly twenty contain Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and the Song of Solomon, as well as the book of Baruch, and slightly fewer contain the books of Tobit, Judith, Wisdom of Solomon, Ecclesiasticus <note>Also known as Sirach.</note>, and Maccabees. Collations <note>The process of comparing different manuscript versions to identify variations.</note> of a great many codices were undertaken at vast expense for the use of the Holmesian edition (Volume I, Oxford 1798, edited by Holmes; Volumes II–V, Oxford 1810–1827, edited by Parsons). However, as these appear in the published volumes, they not only differ significantly among themselves in terms of reliability and accuracy, but they were performed so negligently and poorly regarding the primary witnesses themselves that it is a matter of repeated regret that such vast sums of money—rarely supplied through English generosity with such liberality <margin>1)</margin>—have been of so little benefit to sacred criticism <note>The scholarly study and investigation of biblical texts to determine their original form.</note>. Since this has already been sufficiently understood by intelligent men, I shall demonstrate it with only a few examples.

He performed most poorly in that, for the representation of the Codex Alexandrinus <note>A 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible, one of the most important "witnesses" or sources for the text.</note>—a most rich witness—he used nothing but the Grabe edition. For what, after all, could have been easier for an Oxford editor than to follow the actual codex itself, which lies open in London? And he not only rested content with the published Grabe texts (concerning whose meager reliability we shall speak below), but he did not even consult Grabe himself with diligence: for those readings which Grabe had carefully explained in his Prolegomena, because they did not appear in his published text, were entirely omitted by Holmes, even though he thought similar things from other codices should be cited here and there. Furthermore, since he followed only the text of Grabe, he foolishly attributed many things to the codex which are actually due to the editor. Of this kind is 1 Kingdoms 2:29: "from the beginning <note>original: "ἀπ' ἀρχῆς"</note> (so) Alex."; the same applies to "through the outlets" <note>original: "δι' ἐκβολάς"</note> in Zechariah 9:10; "by choice" <note>original: "ἐξ αἱρέσεως"</note> in Genesis 49:5; "of her" <note>original: "αὐτῆς"</note> instead of "of her" <note>original: "αὐτῆς"</note> in 2 Kingdoms 6:17, and similar instances elsewhere <margin>2)</margin>. For since he declares in his Prolegomena that he would distinguish the difference of the codex (if there be any) from the edition by placing "III." for the former and "Alex." for the latter, it is right to interpret those things to which nothing but "Alex." is attached as referring to both <margin>3)</margin>. Finally, too little...

---

<margin>1) In the catalog of patrons presented in Volume I, 1798, the editor added this note: 
> These, therefore, were the patrons by whose authority it was brought about that I could pursue the undertaking this far, and from whose pecuniary subsidies, most munificently contributed to this work from year to year, I had the means whereby I have already been able to expend upon this collation and edition more than seven thousand pounds Sterling (about 50,000 Prussian thalers).</margin>

<margin>2) He is similarly engaged in noting punctuation; since Grabe often did this arbitrarily against the will of the codex itself, Holmes sought it from the published edition alone.</margin>

<margin>3) The examples I have cited are of such a kind that they could be contained in the codex; for in such places, for the sake of avoiding ambiguity, a breathing mark <note>A diacritic used in Greek to indicate aspiration.</note> is placed here and there in the most ancient written books, but in these specific places, they are found nowhere but in Grabe.</margin>

# PRELIMINARY REMARKS

He [Holmes] dealt with little accuracy regarding the readings expressed in "smaller type" in Grabe's edition <note>John Ernest Grabe (1666–1711) published an influential edition of the Septuagint based on the Codex Alexandrinus.</note>. Concerning these, Holmes says: "There are several readings which, as they were omitted in the *Alexandrian* manuscript, are moved into the text of the Alexandrian edition under a smaller typeface." Again, regarding Genesis: "Certain words were introduced into this edition which were not contained in the Alexandrian manuscript, and the editors printed them in a smaller character of letters." However, often where the *Alexandrian* manuscript agrees with the Vatican edition, it is not clear in Holmes' work whether the things written in smaller letters in Grabe refer to supplements to or corrections of the *Alexandrian* manuscript. Nor does he report accurately where he cites both "III" <note>Referring to the Roman/Vatican edition</note> and "*Alex.*", such as in *Exodus* 19:7, where he has: "of the people] Israel III. of the people in smaller character *Alex.*" But the *Alexandrian* manuscript has *Israel* instead of *of the people*, and Grabe's edition displays *of the people* <note>original: "τοῦ λαοῦ"</note> in smaller letters, not just *people*.

Furthermore, as has already been indicated above, Holmes repeatedly reproduced manifest errors of the Roman edition; indeed, because he notes either few or no manuscripts that differ from these, he is guilty of incredible negligence. Thus, as we have already seen, in *Judges* 9:23, he repeated "they set at nought" <note>original: "ἠθέτησαν"</note> and does not cite "they set at nought" from any manuscript. Lest we say this is due to laziness rather than ignorance, we are prevented by the fact that he adds that "they set at nought" is read in the "*Armenian 1, Armenian edition, Georgian, and Moscow Slavonic*" versions. Likewise, in *Joshua* 2:19, he repeats "but you [plural] are innocent" <note>original: "ὑμεῖς δὲ ἀθῷοι"</note>. Since this could clearly not be sustained, most editors long ago published "but we are innocent" <note>original: "ἡμεῖς δ. ἀ."</note>. Yet, according to Holmes' testimony, only four manuscripts differ from the faulty "you." Likewise, in *Deuteronomy* 14:17, he repeats the faulty "pelican" <note>original: "πελακᾶνα"</note>, indicating that 16 manuscripts differ from it; yet it seems that almost all of them differ. Likewise, in *Nehemiah* 10:30, he repeats the absurd "to the [peoples] of the land" <note>original: "τοῖς γῆς"</note> (which the Roman editors already corrected with a pen), indicating that nine manuscripts have it otherwise; likewise, in *Job* 9:4, suspecting nothing wrong, he publishes "in the mind" <note>original: "διανοίᾳ"</note> without the iota subscript, which had faded away in the *Roman* edition.

Finally, how negligently he cared for grammatical matters—which are indeed of great importance in critical work—above all others, I shall prove with a few examples, though I could with many. Regarding "he prayed" <note>original: "προσεύξατο"</note> in *Jonah* 4:2, he reports absolutely no manuscript difference, nor any more for "provoked to anger" in *Sirach* 4:3 or "he rejoiced" in *Sirach* 30:5. Yet it is most certain that in all three of these places, very many manuscripts differ; the *Alexandrian* manuscript itself has the augmented forms <note>The author is noting the omission of the temporal augment 'η' in Holmes' text compared to the manuscript's "προσηύξατο, παρωργισμενην, ηὐφράνθη".</note>. Similarly, in *Genesis* 47:27, he passes over the reading of the *Alexandrian* manuscript "they were increased," but in the same verse he ascribes to the Alexandrian manuscript neither the omission of "in the land" nor "they were possessed" instead of "they possessed," although he cites many other witnesses for those same readings. Likewise, in *Deuteronomy* 12:20, "he shall desire" <note>original: "ἐπιθυμήσει" for "ἐπιθυμήσῃ"</note> in the margin of manuscript 131; in the same book at 12:29, "you shall inherit" and "you shall dwell"; at 17:4, "you shall seek out"; and in *Psalm* 47:4, "he helps"—these are found in many manuscripts...

<summary>This page critiques the textual scholar Robert Holmes for his inaccuracies and negligence in documenting manuscript variations, particularly his failure to correctly note grammatical differences and his reliance on flawed previous editions like the Roman Septuagint.</summary>

XXII. Truly, among many other things which can be usefully learned from the work of Holmes <note>Robert Holmes (1748–1805), who began a massive collation of Septuagint manuscripts</note>, one must highly value the collation of the Vatican manuscript <note>The Codex Vaticanus, one of the oldest and most important Greek biblical manuscripts</note> itself, carried out for individual books, with the exception of the Prophets, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, the Song of Solomon, Wisdom of Solomon, and Sirach. From this, what was easy to suspect for a long time has been clearly proven: that the Roman editors departed from the written copy in quite a few readings—not only in matters of spelling (which the collator of Holmes for the most part neglected <note>1)</note>), as they themselves claimed—but in actual wording. For the sake of example, let us look at the readings through the last four chapters of Genesis, where the Vatican text begins, as transcribed from the manuscript itself by the collator. Let us distinguish those which the Vatican manuscript seems to uphold alone from those which it has in common with others. Of the first kind are: **XLVII, 2.** it omits "his" <note>original: αυτου</note>; **v. 19.** it omits "not" <note>original: ουκ</note>; **v. 26.** it has "upon the land" <note>original: επι γην</note> instead of "upon the earth" <note>original: επι γης</note> (some others have "upon the land" <note>original: επι την γην</note>); **v. 29.** it omits "the" <note>original: του</note> before "dying"; **XLVIII, 3.** it has "my God appeared to me" <note>original: ωφθη μοι ο θεος μου</note>; **v. 4.** it omits "you" the first and second time (the latter joined by manuscript 75); **v. 5.** it has "into Egypt to you" <note>original: εις Αιγυπτον προς σε</note>; **v. 16.** "my name in them" <note>original: το ονομα μου εν αυτοις</note>; **XLIX, 28.** at the end it adds "the father" <note>original: ο πατηρ</note> (one other has "their father" <note>original: ο πατηρ αυτων</note>); **L, 5.** "my father made me swear" <note>original: ο πατηρ με ωρκισε</note>; **v. 13.** "and they took him up there" <note>original: και ανελαβ. αυτ. εκει</note>; **v. 18.** it omits "to you" <note>original: σοι</note>; **v. 19.** it has "I am" <note>original: εγω ειμι</note>. Of the second kind: **XLVII, 11.** it omits the first "his" <note>original: αυτου</note> (likewise the Alexandrian, Coislinian, and more than ten others) and has "in the land of Egypt"; **v. 14.** it has "all the money" <note>original: το αργυριον παν</note> (joined only by manuscript 75); **v. 17.** it omits the first "their" <note>original: αυτων</note> (likewise the Cottonian, Alexandrian, Ambrosian, Coislinian, and many others); **v. 19.** it omits "the" <note>original: τω</note> before "Pharaoh" (likewise the Cottonian, Alexandrian, Coislinian, and many others), likewise **v. 20.** in the third place; **v. 24.** it omits "and" <note>original: και</note> before "you shall give" (likewise the Alexandrian, Coislinian, and many others); **v. 25.** it omits "the" <note>original: τω</note> before "Pharaoh"; **v. 28.** it has "seventeen years years" <note>original: ετη δεκαεπτα ετη</note>, the latter "years" being rejected by three dots <note>A common scribal method for marking a deletion</note>, and in the same place "and they were." **XLVIII, 1.** it omits the first "and" (with several versions); **v. 5.** it omits "land" <note>original: γη</note> (likewise the Cottonian and many others); **v. 10.** it has "but the eyes" <note>original: οι δε οφθαλμοι</note> (likewise the Cottonian and many others); **v. 12.** "Joseph them" (with manuscript 75 alone); **v. 15.** "he blessed" <note>original: ηυλογησεν</note> (likewise the Alexandrian and others) and "before" <note>original: εναντιον</note> (likewise the Cottonian and many others); **v. 17.** it omits "the hand" <note>original: την χειρα</note> (likewise the Cottonian and many others). # PROLEGOMENA.
<note>Introduction</note>

...the face, verse 6. He omits "to Joseph," verse 10. He has "they did," verse 11. "at the threshing floor" (likewise the Cotton, Alexandrian, Ambrose, Coislin manuscripts, and many others), verse 15. "they said," verse 16. "they arrived" and "saying" instead of "they said," verse 20. "so that he might be nourished," verse 21. "and he said," verses 24 and 25. "God [will be with] you" (likewise many others).

XXIII. Therefore, in order to arrive at a critical apparatus of such a kind that both the most ancient state of the Greek Old Testament text may be restored—with as much probability as the ancient witnesses allow—and its history may be accurately learned, one must not start from the work of Holmes <note>Robert Holmes, who began a massive collation of Septuagint manuscripts in the late 18th century</note>, as we have already said above, but from new critical studies regarding the witnesses themselves. It is well established that there are three classes of critical instruments: Greek codices <note>ancient manuscript books</note>, ancient versions <note>translations into other languages</note>, and the Fathers and other ancient writers. All three of these classes must be applied to critical labor with the greatest zeal; but the greatest authority above the rest belongs to the Greek manuscripts themselves. For that reason, at this time, as the plan of this edition requires, having set aside the translations <note>1</note> and the Fathers <note>2</note>—

---

**1)** From the text of the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint or LXX</note> flowed primarily these versions: the Old Latin or *Itala*, established as early as the third or second century; the Egyptian (Sahidic, Memphitic or Coptic, Basmuric) of the third or fourth century; the Ethiopic of the fourth or fifth century; the Gothic of the fourth century; the Armenian of the fifth century; the Georgian of the sixth century; several Syriac versions, especially the one called the "Hexaplaric" made by Paul, Bishop of Tella, at the beginning of the seventh century; the Slavonic of the ninth century; and finally several Arabic versions of uncertain age but not very ancient. Among all these, the Old Latin holds the first place in critical matters; it has indeed already been treated with distinguished labor, but it requires much more. Regarding the others, the Egyptian and Ethiopic versions (to which new light now shines thanks to the studies of the most renowned Dillmann <note>August Dillmann, a 19th-century orientalist</note>) hold primary critical importance, as do the Syriac and Armenian.

**2)** Among these [the Fathers], Origen holds the highest place; his studies concerning the Greek text of the Old Testament attained great fame throughout the whole Christian world. In his great work which he titled the *Hexapla* <note>meaning "six-fold"</note> (likewise the *Tetrapla* if the Hebrew columns are omitted?), he arranged a "synopsis," as they call it, in six columns placed side-by-side: first the Hebrew text in its own letters, then in Greek letters, and then four Greek translations. The first was by Aquila, who stood out as a "most diligent explorer of Hebrew words" for the use of his countrymen, "studiously translating word for word"; the second was by Symmachus, providing a clear and elegant version for Greek ears; the third was that of the Seventy Interpreters <note>the LXX</note> themselves, which Origen augmented from the others (chiefly Theodotion), marking with a star ※ those things which were lacking in the Hebrew, and designating with "obels" or little daggers those things which seemed to have been added superfluously or against the Hebrew. Finally, the fourth translation was by Theodotion, who had primarily acted to correct the Septuagint text from the Hebrew and Aquila. "Some books, however," to continue in the words of Jerome, "and especially those which among the Hebrews are composed in verse, have three other editions added, which they call the Fifth, Sixth, and Seventh translations, which attained authority without the names of the translators." It is greatly to be desired that the studies of collecting all those fragments which still survive here and there from Origen's work—in which Morinus, Drusius, Montfaucon, Bahrdt, Schleusner, and others have previously served sacred literature—be repeated and advanced. Furthermore, that Hexaplaric text of Origen, or the Septuagint text from Origen's revision, was highly valued by scholars and frequently copied, [distinguishing it] from the common or...

# PROLEGOMENA

...and other writers <insert>1)</insert>, we have compiled this apparatus only from those most ancient Greek manuscripts whose readings, having already been published for critical use, could be cited with total confidence. Before we examine these more closely, it is pleasing to state briefly how many and which manuscripts, written chiefly in **uncial letters** <note>Uncials are large, rounded capital letters used in Greek manuscripts from the 4th to 8th centuries AD.</note> before the tenth century, have survived to our times. For it is upon these, when they have been scrupulously examined, that the efforts to restore the text of the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> will primarily depend.

Holmes <note>Robert Holmes (1748–1805), a biblical scholar known for his massive edition of the Septuagint.</note> indeed employed thirteen such manuscripts for his apparatus. For besides the two principal ones, the **Vatican** (II. in Holmes) and the **Alexandrian** (III. in Holmes)—which no others equal in scope, as they encompass nearly the entire Old Testament—he employed three of greater scope for the Pentateuch: the **Ambrosian** (VII. in Holmes) of roughly the fifth century, the **Coislin** (X. in Holmes) of roughly the sixth century, and the **Basilian-Vatican** (XI. in Holmes) beginning from the middle of Leviticus and dating to roughly the ninth century. Likewise, he used three manuscripts of fragments: the **Cotton** (I. in Holmes) of Genesis from roughly the fifth century—which was indeed long ago destroyed by fire, but was collated before the fire chiefly by Grabe and Ussher; the **Vienna** (VI. in Holmes) of Genesis, a purple manuscript <note>Written on parchment dyed purple, usually reserved for imperial or high-status commissions.</note> of roughly the sixth century; and the **Sarravian**, preserved partly in Leiden (IV. in Holmes), partly in Paris (V.), and St. Petersburg (V.), dating to roughly the fourth century. Of these eight (nine in Holmes) manuscripts, the Coislin also encompasses the books of Joshua, Judges, Ruth, and three books of Kings; the Ambrosian also contains nearly half the book of Joshua with two leaves of the prophets, one of Isaiah and the other of Malachi; the Basilian-Vatican also contains all the books from Joshua through Ezra, Nehemiah, and Esther; the Sarravian also contains several fragments of Joshua and Judges (of which the St. Petersburg library holds one leaf). For the remaining books, besides that primary pair, Holmes had five uncial manuscripts: **1.** the **Marchalian** codex, formerly the **Clermont**, now the **Vatican** (XII. in Holmes), of roughly the eighth century, for all the prophets; **2.** the **Dublin** (VIII. in Holmes), a palimpsest <note>original: "rescriptum" — a manuscript where the original text was scraped off and written over.</note> with fragments of Isaiah, of roughly the sixth century; **3.** the **Venetian Marcian** (no. 23 in Holmes) of the eighth or ninth century, for the second part of the book of Job, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, Song of Solomon, Wisdom of Solomon, Ecclesiasticus, all the Prophets, Tobit, Judith, and

---

<margin>the *koine* <note>original: "κοινῇ" — the "common" or standard text.</note> i.e. that which was usually distinguished from the critical studies of Origen by being immune to them. Soon, however, Origen himself became the cause of new corruption because unskilled men, who devoted their labor to transcribing, sometimes confused and sometimes omitted Origen's signs <note>The asterisks and obeli used by Origen to mark differences between the Greek and Hebrew texts.</note>; furthermore, they continuously mixed into the text those things which it is probable that the first transcribers, such as Pamphilus and Eusebius, had noted in the margin from the other columns.</margin>

1) Among these, Philo and Josephus stand out most prominently, in whose writings so many testimonies of the most ancient text of the Seventy Interpreters are found.

# PROLEGOMENA
<note>original: "Prolegomena" — introductory remarks or a formal preface to a scholarly work.</note>

...three books of the Maccabees; 4. the Paris fragments of the Psalms (number 43 in Holmes's list) in a manuscript decorated with many pictures, dating to approximately the ninth century; 5. the Vatican manuscript of the book of Job with pictures, of approximately the same century (number 258 in Holmes's list).

XXIV. To this catalog of the Holmesian <term>uncial</term><note>Uncials are manuscripts written entirely in large, rounded capital letters, typical of the 4th-8th centuries.</note> manuscripts, eleven others are added, outstanding in their claim to antiquity but for the most part of small size. Seven of them (1, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11) were discovered by me personally in the East and brought from there to Europe, and with two exceptions (6 and 11), they have already been published; two others (2 and 5) were first unearthed and published by me from the libraries in Paris and London.

The first is the *Codex Friderico-Augustanus* in Leipzig, of approximately the fourth century; the second is the *Codex Ephraemi Syri* <term>rescriptus</term><note>Also known as a palimpsest; a manuscript where the original text was scraped off and written over.</note> in Paris, of approximately the fifth century, about both of which we shall speak more accurately later. The third is the Verona manuscript of the Psalms, written in Latin letters <note>original: "Latinis litteris" — referring to a Greek text transliterated into the Latin alphabet</note> of the fifth or sixth century, with a Latin translation placed alongside it. The fourth is the purple Zurich manuscript of the Psalms of the fifth or sixth century. The fifth is a London papyrus manuscript with fragments of the Psalter, the age of which seems to exceed the fourth century. The sixth is a manuscript very recently brought by me from the East, in which is the greatest part of the book of Genesis, of approximately the eighth century. The seventh, brought to Europe from that same Eastern journey of mine, is a <term>palimpsest</term><note>A "scraped-again" parchment where the older text is still visible beneath the new.</note> of approximately the sixth century, with the greatest part of the book of Numbers. The eighth is the *Codex Tischendorfianus II* in Leipzig, of approximately the seventh century, with fragments of Numbers, Deuteronomy, Joshua, and Judges. The ninth, tenth, and eleventh, likewise found by me in the East and brought here, contain only a few fragments: the ninth, of approximately the seventh century, contains fragments of Isaiah on six palimpsest leaves; the tenth, seemingly of the same century, contains fragments of the second and third books of Kings <note>In older catalogs, 1 and 2 Samuel and 1 and 2 Kings were often grouped as the four books of Kings.</note> on three palimpsest leaves; the eleventh, of approximately the fifth century, contains fragments of the Psalms on two leaves. Finally, to these could be added another manuscript brought by me from the East and very recently made the property of the British Museum, containing the complete books of Judges and Ruth with the final part of the book of Joshua. For it was written in <term>minuscule</term><note>A smaller, cursive style of handwriting that replaced the uncial script.</note> letters of the ninth century, distinguished above most older manuscripts by the consistency of its Alexandrian forms.

However, we shall illustrate all of these, from the third to the twelfth, with several specimens of their readings. And first, indeed, we must look at the Verona Psalter, which Blanchinus published as an addition to his *Vindication of the Canonical Scriptures etc.* at Rome in 1740, under this title: *The Double Psalter with Canticles according to the Greek Vulgate of the 70 Elders <note>The Septuagint (LXX)</note> and the ancient Latin Itala version.* It comes from a distinguished Greek-Latin manuscript of the Great Chapter of Verona, written in uncial characters before the 7th century. By way of a specimen, I shall describe a part of Psalm 142 from the copper plates of Blanchinus...

Furthermore, this codex also confirms forms such as *I will receive*, *helper*, *to destroy*, *hand*, in this manner: *lempsome, antilemtor, exolethreuse, chiran.* <note>original: "λήμψομαι, ἀντιλήμπτωρ, ἐξολεθρεῦσαι, χεῖραν." The author is noting specific phonetic spellings that reflect how Greek was pronounced or written in late antiquity.</note>

2. (4.) The Zurich Codex of Psalms found a commentator in Johann Jakob Breitinger, who published a small book in Zurich in 1748 with this title: *A letter to Cardinal Angelo Maria Quirini concerning the most ancient Greek book of Psalms in the Zurich library, executed on purple parchment with golden titles and silver letters.* In this commentary, after the grammatical nature of the codex's writing is explained—which indeed agrees in the main points with the **Codex Alexandrinus** and similar ones—the reading of passages is primarily cited in such a way that it can be known how Jerome or Justin might have read them. Thus, Psalm 26:6, with which the Zurich codex begins, has *and now behold, he has lifted up my head*, with the article omitted, contrary to the **Vatican** and **Alexandrian** codices, just as Jerome already [noted]. <note>original: "καὶ νῦν ἰδοὺ ὕψωσεν κεφαλήν μου." Jerome refers to St. Jerome, the 4th-century translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note>

testifies that it was found in the Greek. In the same place, verse 8, he defends the reading already cited by Jerome: *my face sought you* <note>original Greek: εξεζητησεν σε το προσωπον μου</note>, in place of which the Vatican and Alexandrian codices have: *I sought your face* <note>original Greek: εξεζητησα το προσωπον σου</note>. Likewise, in Psalm 27:2, he includes *Lord* <note>original Greek: κυριε</note> after *hear*, against the witness of the Vatican and Alexandrian codices; Jerome himself testifies that this was "added" in the Greek. In Psalm 36:23, along with the Alexandrian codex, he has *greatly* <note>original Greek: σφοδρα</note> after *he will desire*. Jerome again says this was added in the Greek and is not held by any of the translators. In Psalm 38:12, he omits the word *is troubled* <note>original Greek: ταρασσεται</note> along with the Alexandrian codex. Regarding this passage, Jerome writes: *“And you say that in the Greek you did not find 'is troubled.' But even this was added under an obelus <note>A critical mark (÷) used by ancient scholars to indicate doubtful or spurious text</note> in the Septuagint. And from this, an error arises among you and most people, because through the negligence of scribes—the commas and asterisks having been removed—the entire distinction is confounded.”* In Psalm 39:12, with the Alexandrian codex, he has *hasten* <note>original Greek: σπευσον</note> instead of *give heed* <note>original Greek: προσχες</note>, which Jerome says is found in the Greek. In Psalm 40:7, concerning the word *if* <note>original Greek: ει</note>, Jerome says: *“And you say that 'if' is not placed in the Greek, although it is most clearly written both in the Hebrew and in all the translators, and the Septuagint translated it.”* But in truth, the Zurich codex <note>Codex Turicensis</note> omits *if* along with the Alexandrian codex. In Psalm 43:10, after *you will go out*, he adds *O God* <note>original Greek: ο θεος</note> with the Alexandrian, which Jerome says is found in the Greek although it is superfluous. In Psalm 47:9, the Vatican edition has *so also we saw*, the Alexandrian codex has *so we saw*. But Jerome testifies that that *also* <note>original Greek: και</note> was found in the Greek although superfluous. The Zurich codex has *so also we saw*.

4. (6.) Regarding this codex of Genesis, I have briefly set forth an account in my *Sacred and Profane Anecdotes* <note>original: "Anecdotis sacris et profanis"</note> (Leipzig 1855), page 6 and following, from which I shall add these remarks: "It excels so greatly in the quality of its readings that I do not doubt it will serve excellently in restoring the Greek Genesis to its original integrity; for it seems not infrequently to surpass even the *Codex Alexandrinus* <note>A famous 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible.</note> itself, with which it often agrees. Since the *Vatican* book <note>Codex Vaticanus, one of the oldest and most important biblical manuscripts.</note>, from which the Roman edition was derived, lacks almost the entirety of Genesis, I judge this discovered treasure to be of the highest value." Let Genesis 31:31–47 serve as an example of the text. There, forty-five readings differ from the Roman edition. In thirteen places, it agrees with the *Alexandrinus* codex; in nine others, however, it stands against the *Alexandrinus*, joined six times (it is gapped three times) by that *Cottonian* codex <note>The Cotton Genesis, a 4th/5th-century illuminated manuscript largely destroyed by fire in 1731.</note> which perished in the fire (see above). Furthermore, it often agrees with the *Cottonian* against the *Alexandrinus* and the Roman edition, as in verse 32 (where "and he said --- our" etc. <note>original: "και ειπεν --- ημων"</note> are transposed) and verse 40 ("I became" and "frost"). <note>original: "εγινόμην et παγετός"</note> In verse 33, along with the purple *Viennese* codex <note>The Vienna Genesis, written on purple-dyed vellum.</note>, it omits "into" <note>original: "εις"</note> against the Roman edition and the *Alexandrinus*. Also notable is the agreement of our codex and the *Venetian* codex, concerning which Holmes <note>Robert Holmes, an 18th-century biblical scholar.</note>—by whom it is noted as number 72—judges thus: "It is indeed a codex unique in its kind and to be valued most highly; for it contains a text hitherto unknown." Along with that codex, ours alone omits in verse 36 the words from "answering" to "Laban," <note>original: "αποκριθεις usque Λαβαν"</note> and likewise in verse 37, it alone preserves with the same codex the corrupt reading "I might place here" instead of "place here." <note>original: "θεσω δε pro θες ωδε"</note> Since it offers many other things almost alone, such as in verses 45, 47, and 42 where it has "for a pillar," "and he called," and "yesterday," <note>original: "εις στηλην, εκαλεσεν δέ, εχθές"</note> and in verses 37 and 46 where it omits "that" and "there," <note>original: "οτι et εκει"</note> it then possesses clearly unique readings in many places, such as verse 33 "he entered into," <note>original: "εισηλθεν εις"</note> verse 37 "you have searched" <note>original: "ηραύνηκας" (the rest have "ηρεύνησας," three others have "ηρεύνηκας")</note>, in the same place "you have found" <note>original: "εύρηκας" (the rest have "ευρες")</note>, verse 38 "the cows" <note>original: "αι βόες" (instead of "the goats")</note>, verse 41 "to the sheep" <note>original: "τοις πρόβασιν" (the rest have "τοις προβάτοις")</note>, verse 43 "all that you see" instead of "as many things as you see," <note>original: "α σ' οράς όλα pro όσα συ οράς"</note> etc.

On page 1 and following, I have noted these observations: "In its text, it often agrees with the **Alexandrian codex** <note>Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century Greek Bible</note> against the **Roman Vatican edition** <note>Codex Vaticanus, a 4th-century Greek Bible</note>, and not infrequently it aligns with our other most ancient witnesses against both. There are also instances where it alone preserves a reading, and does so correctly. For example, let us look at the very first leaf, which contains Numbers 26:54 through 27:2: *'their inheritance'* up to *'of the leaders and'*. In verse 57, it has *'the clan of Gedson'* (instead of *Gedsoni*) and *'the clan of Kaath'* (instead of *Kaathi*): a spelling which should be compared with *'the clan of Hebron'* in the following verse in the Alexandrian codex. In verse 58, our **palimpsest codex** <note>A manuscript where the original text was scraped off and overwritten</note> aligns everywhere with the Alexandrian; for in both is read: *'and the clan of Hebron; and the clan of Omousi and the clan of Kore'*, likewise *'Ambram'*. In verse 59, again in both is found *'of the wife of Ambram, Jochabed'*, and later *'Ambram'*. In verse 60, both have *'were born'* <note>original: ἐτέχϑησαν</note> instead of *'were begotten'* <note>original: ἐγεννήϑησαν</note>; likewise in verse 62, *'numbered among the sons'* instead of *'numbered in the midst of the sons'*, and *'inheritance in the midst'* instead of *'inheritance among'*. In verse 63, the Alexandrian alone changes *'who'* with *'that'*, and in verse 65 it omits *'Lord'*: both of which are poor readings. At the end of the chapter, both the Alexandrian codex and ours provide *'son of Naue'* <note>Joshua son of Nun</note> instead of *'the one of Naue'*. In verse 1 of chapter 27, the readings of the Alexandrian codex *'Macheir'* and *'Aigla'* are also in our codex, though *'Mannasseh'* and *'Mala'* are not. In verse 2, our codex everywhere has *'before'* <note>original: ἐναντίον</note> instead of *'in front of'* <note>original: ἔναντι</note>. Take these examples of especially notable readings not yet found elsewhere: 14:23. *'which I swore to your fathers'*. For *'your'* <note>singular</note>, the reading is usually *'their'*, except in one codex where it is *'your'* <note>plural</note>. 31:16. *'on account of Beelphegor'*. This same form of the name—at least in this place—is used by **Origen** <note>Early Christian scholar, c. 184–253 AD</note> alone, instead of the common *'Phogor'*. 33:3. *'Ramasse'*, which it has in common only with the **Coptic translator** instead of the common reading *'Ramesse'*.

I will take an example of the reading from the fragments of Joshua, the text of which is highly significant. The passage Joshua 11:6 through 11 is written there as follows:
