**7. (9.)** These fragments of the prophet Isaiah, rescued with great difficulty from Armenian palimpsest leaves <note>A palimpsest is a manuscript page from which the text has been scraped or washed off so that the page can be reused for another document.</note>, I have published in my new collection *Unpublished Sacred Monuments* from page 185 to 198. They are from chapters three, five, twenty-nine, forty-four, and forty-five. I proved that the text is of the highest quality in my *Sacred and Profane Anecdotes* page 9, using these examples: in 3:8–11, it agrees twice with the Alexandrian Codex <note>original: "Alex. codice"; a famous 5th-century Greek Bible</note>, preserving "fruits" <note>original: "γενηματα"</note> in verse 10 and "the path" <note>original: "την τριβον"</note> in verse 12; furthermore, in verse 8 it has "wherefore" <note>original: "διο"</note> and in verse 11 "it shall happen to him according to" <note>original: "συμβ. αυτω κατα"</note> along with very few other manuscripts; in verse 10 it reads "let us bind" <note>original: "δησομεν"</note> with Eusebius alone, and in verse 14 "the Lord himself" <note>original: "αυτοσ ο κυριοσ"</note> with Procopius alone. Likewise, in 5:2–7, it agrees with the Alexandrian six times; in verse 4 it maintains "that he might do" <note>original: "ινα ποιηση"</note> (likewise in verse 7) with a few minuscule manuscripts <note>Manuscripts written in smaller, cursive Greek script</note>, supported by Origen, Basil, and Chrysostom; and in verse 5 "now therefore" <note>original: "νυν ουν"</note> supported, besides some minuscules, by the codex formerly called Claromontanus and now in the Vatican in the margin (XII. Holm.) and by Severus.

**8. (10.)** These palimpsest fragments of the second (from chapters 22 and 23) and third (from chapters 13, 16, 17) books of Kings <note>In older systems, 1–2 Samuel and 1–2 Kings were often called 1–4 Kings; here referring to 2 Samuel and 1 Kings</note> were published in that same collection of my *Sacred Monuments* from page 177 to 184. (Coislin.) and XI. (Basiliano-Vatican). Thus, on the first leaf, through the first ten verses, it maintains against the Roman edition: "and they shall certainly not rise," "against me," "and instructing," "fear of God," and "and" before "to rise up"; likewise with the same and the Alexandrian "it did not pass by," likewise "those rising up" <note>original: "επανισταμενουσ"</note> with II. (Vatican) and XI. (Alexandrian "επανισταμενουσ"), with the Alexandrian "God the guardian," with X. and the Alexandrian "the sun," with the Alexandrian "this house," with only two minuscule manuscripts "power," with one Holmes nr. 64 "among men," and finally "he who gives" <note>original: "ο διδουσ"</note> alone, where the others prefix "Lord" or "Lord God."

the inscription of Psalm 142, which reads as follows: *A psalm of David, when his son Absalom pursued him.* <note>original Greek: ψαλμος τω δαυειδ' οτε αυτον εδιωκεν αβεσσαλωμ' ο υιος αυτου</note> In the Vatican and Alexandrian codices <note>Codex Vaticanus and Codex Alexandrinus are two of the oldest and most important Greek Bible manuscripts.</note> it is written: *A psalm of David when his son pursued him* (the Alexandrian uses a slightly different verb form for "pursued"). <note>original Greek: ψαλμος τω δαδ οτε αυτον ο υιος κατεδιωκει (Alex. -ιωξεν)</note> Our text, however, clearly agrees with the one we cited above from the Verona Psalter, which was written in Latin letters.

10. (12.) Regarding the <term>minuscule codex</term> <note>A manuscript written in smaller, cursive Greek script, common from the 9th century onwards.</note> from the ninth century which I placed in the final position, these things were said in the *Sacred and Profane Anecdotes*, page 7: Few manuscripts of great antiquity have so faithfully preserved the ancient Alexandrian forms as this codex. We have excerpted these examples: <note>The following are technical linguistic variations in Greek spelling and verb endings typical of the Alexandrian dialect, such as "shall receive," "destroyed," "Benjamin," "Seraphim," etc.</note> *lēmpsē, exolethreutheis, benimein, seraphein, sūnsēmon, ēlthosan, idamen, erimmenēn, erūsanto, tois lapsansin, exēraunēsan, exenekon, anebennen.* Indeed, it offers a text that is especially notable and significant, originating from a <term>recension</term> <note>A specific revised version or "edition" of a text produced in antiquity.</note> that has not yet become known. By what right I say this can be judged by the first 27 verses of the first chapter: for through these, it departs from the Roman edition in fifty places, agreeing eighteen times with the Alexandrian codex (as in verse 17, it has *Sepheph* with the Alexandrian alone, and in verse 27, *Magedon* with only the Alexandrian and Codex X in Holmes's edition), thirteen times with other <term>uncial</term> <note>Manuscripts written in large, formal capital letters.</note> codices, furthermore having other things in common with a few minuscule codices, and some with no allies at all. Of this last kind I mentioned are: verse 1, *in the Lord*; verse 5, the first *the* is omitted; verse 7, *seventy men* (which all others omit) *kings*, and in the same place *and having been cut off*; verse 10, *Gesi* (the Roman edition has *Gessi*, the Alexandrian *Geththe*); verse 11, *city of scribes*; verse 14, *he cried out* instead of *he murmured*; verse 16, *into the land of Judah* added after *of the sons*. It has only a single ally in verse 4, *into the hands* (with Codex 58, of which Holmes says: "of what great value it is"), verse 8, *they burned with fire*, and verse 17, *they cut*.

XXV. Now, however, we must pass from these to the three codices which we have employed for our continuous <term>apparatus</term>. <note>The "critical apparatus" is the set of footnotes in a scholarly Bible that lists all the variations found in different manuscripts.</note> We settled on these three because, at the time when we first prepared this edition of the Old Testament, no other of similar age or authority had been published for critical use; certainly, the Verona Psalter seemed by its nature poorly suited for us to transfer into this manual edition ^1^). Moreover, the Alexandrian codex contains the entire Old Testament except for 1 Samuel 12:17 – 14:9; Psalms 49:19 – 79:12; ^2^ ) and a few verses or words elsewhere. In this regard, it has no equal among the ancients except for the Vatican codex, in which the first chapters of Genesis (up to 46) are missing, as well as thirty-three Psalms (105–138) and three books of Macca—

---

1) To repeat the collation of the other most ancient codices from Holmes would have been entirely foreign to this <term>stereotype edition</term>; <note>A printing method using solid plates, implying a standard, widely distributed version.</note> for indeed, none was exhibited in Holmes's work with that diligence which is necessary for our purposes. From the number of minuscule codices, however, the Leipzig fragments of the Pentateuch were not unworthy of being taken into account.
2) For both locations, supplements to our apparatus are provided below.

# PREFACE.

...the books of the Maccabees. In these parts of the text, therefore, the Alexandrian codex <note>The Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</note> has no equal, nor any older rival. Indeed, the books of the Maccabees have furthermore been found in only two uncial <term>uncial: a script written entirely in capital letters, common in early manuscripts</term> codices of roughly the eighth and ninth centuries. As for the contents of the Frederick-Augustus Codex <note>The Codex Friderico-Augustanus, which is actually part of the Codex Sinaiticus</note>: the fragments of 1 Chronicles and 2 Esdras, as well as the entire books of Nehemiah and Esther, were previously known—aside from the Alexandrian and Vatican codices—from only one uncial codex of roughly the eighth century, which is among the Basilian-Vatican collection. Furthermore, the book of Tobit, of which the first chapter and the following portion are in the Frederick-Augustus, has only one other uncial witness besides the Alexandrian and Vatican: a Venetian codex of the eighth or ninth century. Finally, Jeremiah, the greatest part of which is presented by the Frederick-Augustus, is found—besides that "noble pair" <note>Referring to the Codex Alexandrinus and Codex Vaticanus</note>—in two uncial codices of roughly the eighth and ninth centuries. Lastly, fragments of the famous Parisian palimpsest <term>palimpsest: a manuscript page from which the text has been scraped off so that it can be reused</term> from the books of Job, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, Song of Solomon, Wisdom of Solomon, and Sirach, have not been found outside the Vatican and Alexandrian codices except in two uncial codices: one is the Venetian, beginning from chapter 30 of the book of Job (8th century), and the other is the Vatican, containing only the book of Job (roughly 9th century).

But these individual matters must now be discussed a little more extensively.

XXVI. The Alexandrian Codex became the property of the British Museum after it had been given as a gift to the patriarchal chamber (at Alexandria) in the year 1098—as is noted in the codex itself—and again in the year 1628, when it was sent as a gift to Charles I, King of Britain, by Cyril Lucaris, patriarch first of Alexandria and later of Constantinople. The same Cyril Lucaris testifies with his own hand to a tradition from which this codex is said to have been written "by the hand of Thecla, a noble Egyptian woman, shortly after the Council of Nicaea" <note>The Council of Nicaea took place in 325 AD; this would make the manuscript very early indeed</note>. This tradition seems to many to have been devised for the glory of a revered book. Yet if it is true, as the old proverb says, that a rumor is not easily spread unless there is some truth beneath it, the Alexandrian codex has rightly been seen as originating from the famous monastery of Saint Thecla at Seleucia, which was already flourishing in the time of Gregory of Nazianzus ¹); from this, it could easily happen that it was said to be produced by the hand of Thecla herself. Moreover, the form of the letters, the simplicity of the punctuation, the rarity of abbreviations, and finally everything that serves to define the age of a book—whether in the Old or New Testament—is of such a kind that it is believed with the greatest probability to have been written in the fifth century.

Regarding the use of this codex for critical matters, after the merits...

---

<note>1) Just as it is in the *Life of Gregory of Nazianzus*: <note>original: "Τῆς ἐκκλησίας ὑπανεχώρησε, καὶ καταλαβὼν Σελεύκειαν τῷ παρθενῶνι τῆς πανάγνου Θέκλης ἐνδιαιτᾶται etc."</note> "He withdrew from the church, and having reached Seleucia, he lived in the convent of the all-pure Thecla," etc. Nor does Gregory himself say otherwise of himself in his iambic verses: <note>original: "Πρῶτον μὲν ἦλθον εἰς Σελεύκειαν φυγάς, Τὸν παρθενῶνα τῆς ἀοιδίμου κόρης Θέκλας· etc."</note> "First I came to Seleucia as a fugitive, to the convent of the celebrated maiden Thecla," etc. See the beginning of Grabe's Prolegomena.</note>

# LXVI PROLEGOMENA.

Walton, who had arranged for the <term>variant readings</term> to be extracted through the labor of Alexander Huish and inserted into his *Polyglot Bible*, was followed by a man famous for many reasons who earned great merit: Ernest Grabe <note>original: "Ernestus Grabe"</note> $^1$). For it was he who undertook the care of publishing the codex itself. The work appeared at Oxford between 1707 and 1720 in four volumes of the largest format, of which the first and fourth were published by Grabe himself; the remaining two were published by two friends of the deceased, one of whom, Francis Lee, also provided a learned commentary for the third volume. Grabe himself explains most abundantly in his *Prolegomena* <note>Introductory remarks</note> what the method of this edition was. He asserts, moreover, that all the readings of the codex were expressed by him either in the text itself or in the margin, except for those which he felt sufficient to explain in the *Prolegomena*. Of this kind are both all those which seemed to be manifest errors of the scribe, and those which he attributed to "the method of writing of ancient scribes, which differs from our own." Nevertheless, he testifies that he retained a few of those $^2$), such as *sunpropempsai* <note>original: "συνπροπέμψαι"</note> in Genesis 12:20 and elsewhere, as well as generally the <term>movable nu</term> <note>original: "ν ἐφελκ." - a grammatical feature of Greek where an 'n' is added to the end of a word</note> before a consonant. But before he reviews those errors $^3$), he prefixes these remarks: "Because among these <term>slips of the pen</term> <note>original: "sphalmata"</note> some occur which are nevertheless placed elsewhere in the margin of the text, this must be attributed to different reasons or the occasionally conflicting advice of friends; some of whom advised that the outer edge of the book should be stained with fewer marks, while others, on the contrary, advised that very few things should be counted as errors." Added to this is the fact that in places changed by an ancient hand, he usually reported nothing except the emendation of the second hand $^4$); but where an ancient corrector had changed something poorly, he admitted nothing but the original first hand. Although it is very difficult to satisfy everyone in these matters, and it is equally fair to measure an editor by his own plan rather than by the diverse criteria of others, I can nevertheless easily bring forward many instances in which

---

<margin>1) "A Prussian by nation" he is called on the very title page by the editors of volumes II and III, although Francis Lee says in the *Prolegomena* that "his own Germany was not pleasing enough to him."</margin>

<margin>2) In these are placed not only the 'n' assimilated in *em meso* <note>in the middle</note>, *eg gastri* <note>in the womb</note>; the 'n' with assimilation neglected, as in *synkalymma* <note>covering</note>, *enchōrion* <note>native</note>, *enpneon* <note>breathing in</note>; the 'r' omitted in *orthisas* <note>having risen early</note>, *keagras* <note>meat-hook</note>; the neglected aspiration as in *echthros* <note>enemy</note> (also *ekchthros*); the 'm' retained in the conjugation of the verb *lambanō* <note>to take</note>, such as *elēmphthēn*; the final 'n' of accusatives ending in 'a', such as *akridan* <note>locust</note>, *nyktan* <note>night</note>: but also 'e' confused with 'a' in *tesserakonta* <note>forty</note>, *ekatherisen* <note>he cleansed</note>; 'a' with 'e', as in *eraunan* <note>to search</note>; likewise forms such as *diabenneis*, *eripsen*, *krosōta*; and very often the confusion of *hēmeis, hēmōn* <note>we, our</note> etc. with *hymeis, hymōn* <note>you, your</note> etc.</margin>

<margin>3) Among these are: Gen. 1:29 *pan* for *panta*; 11:17 *Phalek* for *Phaleg*; 14:1 *Sellasar* for *Ellasar*; 14:5 *Somaious* for *Omaious*; 24:32 *epesaxen* for *apesaxen*; 25:27 *ablastos* for *aplastos*; 27:1 *Isak* for *Isaak*; 46:20 *Petrephē* for *Petephrē*; Exod. 5:22 *apestalkes*; 8:3 *hē batrachos* etc.</margin>

<margin>4) Thus in Exod. 8:3 he reports only that in the Alexandrian codex, after "your dough," "and in the wells" is added. But the reading of the first hand, "wells" instead of "dough," which is itself in other codices, ought not to have been passed over in silence.</margin>

# PREFACE LXVII

By freely correcting these passages without noting the reading of the manuscript itself either in the margin or in the Preface, he undoubtedly departed from the duty of an editor of a manuscript <note>The author argues that an editor's primary job is to record what is actually written, even if it seems like an error.</note> ^1^). Furthermore, throughout Grabe's four volumes, not a few passages are found poorly transcribed from the manuscript, in which there is neither a genuine fault nor merely ancient spelling ^2^).

And these remarks indeed concern only the Alexandrian manuscript in Grabe's work. Not content with merely publishing that, he strove to imitate the famous edition of Origen <note>Referring to the Hexapla, where Origen used symbols to compare the Greek and Hebrew texts.</note>. Hence, he prefixed asterisks to other parts which seemed to have been added to the text of the Seventy Translators <term>Septuagint</term> from Theodotion or another translator; other parts which exist in the Greek text but not in the Hebrew, he marked with obels, lemnisci, or hypolemnisci <note>These are ancient critical marks: the obel (—) usually indicated a doubtful passage, while the lemniscus (÷) and hypolemniscus marked variations between versions.</note>. There is no reason to look into this matter more accurately in this place; nevertheless, it is evident that the task undertaken by Grabe was both extremely difficult and of such a nature that it hardly suited an edition of the Alexandrian manuscript, and easily provided an opportunity for error ^3^).

---

1) Of this kind are: Genesis 24:21, *prospered* <note>original: "ευωδωκεν"</note> in Grabe for *prospered* <note>original: "ευοδωκεν"</note> in the manuscript; Exodus 15:17, *you worked* <note>original: "κατειργασω"</note> for *you worked* <note>original: "κατηργασω"</note>, as often elsewhere; likewise Genesis 32:7, *with him* <note>original: "μεθ αυτου"</note> for *with him* <note>original: "μετ αυτου"</note>; 1 Samuel 18:13, *from him* <note>original: "αφ αυτου"</note> for *from him* <note>original: "απ αυτου"</note>; 1 Kings 11:18, *with them* <note>original: "μεθ αυτων"</note> for *with them* <note>original: "μετ αυτων"</note>, which kind of silent correction is frequent; likewise Genesis 38:9, *not for himself* <note>original: "ουχ αυτῶ"</note> for *not for himself* <note>original: "ουχ αυτω"</note>; Genesis 42:27, 28, *pouch* <note>original: "μαρσιπος"</note> three times for *pouch* <note>original: "μαρσιππος"</note>; Leviticus 18:4, *to walk* <note>original: "πορευεσθαι"</note> for *walk ye* <note>original: "πορευεσθε"</note> (as in the Roman edition); 18:6, *you shall approach* <note>original: "προσελευσετε"</note> for *he shall approach* <note>original: "προσελευσεται"</note> (as in the Roman edition); Numbers 31:3, *[and] to draw up for battle* <note>original: "[και] παραταξασθαι"</note> for *and draw ye up for battle* <note>original: "και παραταξασθε"</note> (as the Roman edition); Deuteronomy 15:6, *you shall lend* <note>original: "δανειη"</note> for *you shall lend* <note>original: "δανιη"</note> (Roman edition *δανειεῑ*); Deuteronomy 17:16, *he shall return* <note>original: "αποστρεψει"</note> for *he might return* <note>original: "αποστρεψη"</note> (which is itself in the Roman edition); 1 Samuel 8:17, *you shall hide* <note>original: "διακρυψεις"</note> for *you might hide* <note>original: "διακρυσης"</note> (Roman edition *δη κρυψης*); Exodus 25:16, *of a cubit* <note>original: "πηχεως"</note>, but the Alexandrian with the Vatican has *of a cubit* <note>original: "πηχεος"</note>; Exodus 6:5, *whom the* <note>original: "ων οἱ"</note>, but the Alexandrian with the Vatican has *whom the* <note>original: "ὁν οἱ"</note>; Exodus 16:17 sq. four times *as* <note>original: "ὡ"</note> for *the* <note>original: "ὁ"</note>, which the Alexandrian maintains with the Vatican edition, in the same place *the omer* <note>original: "τω γομορ"</note> for *the omer* <note>original: "το γομορ"</note>; Leviticus 25:16, *this* <note>original: "οὗτος"</note> for *thus* <note>original: "οὕτως"</note>, as even the Roman edition has; Leviticus 10:16, *Moses* <note>original: "Μωσηs"</note>, but the Alexandrian with the Vatican has *Moses* <note>original: "Μωυσης"</note>; 2 Kings 17:9, *they clothed themselves* <note>original: "ημφιεοαντο"</note> for *they clothed themselves* <note>original: "ημφιασαντο"</note>, in the same place 16:5, *they were able* <note>original: "εδυναντο"</note> for *they were able* <note>original: "ηδυναντο"</note>; Leviticus 19:29, *it shall be filled* <note>original: "εμπλησθησεται"</note> for *it shall be filled* <note>original: "εμπληθησεται"</note>, and likewise many others, which if they seemed in need of correction to Grabe, certainly what is in the manuscript itself ought to have been placed alongside the correction.

2) These are a few examples from many: Exodus 4:13, *another able* <note>original: "αλλον δυναμενον"</note> for *able another* <note>original: "δυν. αλλον"</note>; 15:15, *I sacrifice to the Lord* <note>original: "θυω τω κυριω"</note> for *I sacrifice to God* <note>original: "θ. τ. θεω"</note>; 16:6, *he said* <note>original: "ειπεν"</note> for *but he said* <note>original: "ειπεν δε"</note>; Leviticus 2:8, *from sacrifices* <note>original: "απο θυσιων"</note> for *from the sacrifices* <note>original: "απο των θυσιων"</note>; 10:10, *and to distinguish* <note>original: "και διαστειλαι"</note>, but *and* is missing; 11:10, *in torrents* <note>original: "εν χειμαρροις"</note> and 11:11, *in the torrents* <note>original: "εν τοις χειμ."</note>, but the manuscript has *the* <note>original: "τοις"</note> in 11:10, but not in 11:11; 13:5, *day* <note>original: "ημερα"</note> for *the day* <note>original: "τη ημερα"</note>; 24:11, *of the woman of Jor.* <note>original: "της γυναικος Ιορ."</note>, but it is *of the woman of the Jor.* <note>original: "τ. γυν. της Ιορ."</note>; 25:10, *each one* <note>original: "εις και εκαστος"</note> for *each one* <note>original: "εις κατ εκαστος"</note>; 1 Samuel 1:21, *in* <note>original: "εν"</note> is not missing; 1:26, *lord* <note>original: "κυριε"</note> is not missing, but *Lord* <note>original: "κυριος"</note> is read instead; 3:7, he reports *and before* <note>original: "και πριν"</note>, but *before* is not in the manuscript; 7:12, *Massephath* is not omitted, but *Massepha* is there instead; 17:11, it is not *they heard* but *he heard* (17:18, *cheese*, not *stryphalidas*); 2 Samuel 2:13, *the* <note>original: "την"</note> is not missing before *fountain*; 1 Kings 10:1, *in* <note>original: "εν"</note> is missing according to Grabe, but the manuscript has *in riddles* <note>original: "αιν ενιγμασιν"</note> etc. A strange thing happened in the final part of the book of Habakkuk, where the *Prayer of Habakkuk* is handed down. For the one who continued Grabe's work did not seek this from the continuous text of the prophet as he should have, but from the collection of hymns joined to the Psalter, in which many things differ from the former.

3) Breitinger diligently followed Grabe's studies concerning the Alexandrian manuscript and the entire text of the Septuagint, and he published in Zurich <note>original: "Tigurum"</note>...

<meta>This page continues the historical overview of the printed editions of the Codex Alexandrinus, specifically discussing the transition from Grabe’s 18th-century edition to the more precise 19th-century facsimile by Baber, while critiquing intermediate versions by Breitinger and others.</meta>

# PREFACE

XXVII. Since these things are so, it is not surprising that nearly a hundred years after the death of Grabe <note>John Ernest Grabe (1666–1711), the first major editor of the Codex Alexandrinus</note>, learned Englishmen, full of immortal praise for their treasure, were seized by a desire to prepare an edition of such a kind that would represent the entire written manuscript as splendidly and faithfully as possible. Hence, at great expense, with type cast to resemble the handwriting of the manuscript, the care of publishing was entrusted to **Henry Hervey Baber**. The work was completed within fourteen years; for the first volume appeared in the year 1812, and the third, along with the Preface, in 1826. It is readily apparent that this true edition of the Alexandrian Codex is immensely superior to Grabe's. Therefore, to the [editors] themselves—

---

<note>The following section discusses the edition by Johann Jakob Breitinger, published in Switzerland</note>
of the Swiss, 1730–1732, in four volumes: "*The Old Testament according to the Septuagint version. Formerly expressed with the greatest zeal and incredible diligence according to the faith of the Alexandrian manuscript, amended and supplemented by John Ernest Grabe, Professor of Sacred Theology. But now illustrated with variant readings of the Vatican copy and other manuscripts, as well as critical dissertations, and significantly enriched.*" In this work, whatever errors Grabe had made were faithfully repeated. Furthermore, he often set the readings of the Vatican edition (the edition, not the manuscript itself; moreover, the phrase "and of other manuscripts" is entirely wrongly placed in the title) against Grabe’s emendations, which were confused with the actual Alexandrian readings. See examples under note 1 on page LXVII: Exodus 6:5; 16:17; 25:16. Leviticus 16:5; 18:4, 6; 25:16. Deuteronomy 17:16. In these cases, while he could not avoid the things Grabe had silently admitted into the text, he ought to have corrected those which Grabe had explained in his Preface. Of this sort are Exodus 14:13, "he will do for you" <note>original Greek: ποιησει υμιν</note> (the Alexandrian with the Vatican reads "for us" <note>ημιν</note>); Deuteronomy 12:9, "your God" <note>θεος υμων</note> (the Alexandrian with the Vatican reads "our" <note>ημων</note>); Joshua 3:10, "before your face" <note>προσωπου υμων</note> (the Alexandrian with the Vatican reads "our" <note>ημων</note>); "your" <note>υμων</note> twice in Joshua 10:19. Likewise "I may send" <note>αποστειλω</note> in 1 Samuel 21:2, in place of which it is correctly noted in the Preface that the manuscript has "I will send" <note>αποστελω</note> (by an error in Grabe it is "I will complete" <note>αποτελω</note>, which very thing Breitinger faithfully repeated). Furthermore, it happened here and there that Breitinger confused Grabe’s small letters with the larger ones—that is, Grabe’s emendations with the readings of the manuscript itself—as in Deuteronomy 29:12, where he offers "you the covenant" <note>σε την διαθηκην</note> as the reading of the manuscript, but Grabe added "you" <note>σε</note> on his own authority and correctly expressed it in smaller letters. After Breitinger, Reineccius also used Grabe’s edition to represent the Alexandrian manuscript in the four-language Bible which he published in Leipzig in 1751, in the preface of which these words are read: "The Greek interpretation of the Seventy has indeed been conformed to the Grabe edition <note>original: ἔκδοσιν</note> of the Alexandrian manuscript, but to the same have been added the variant readings of the Vatican manuscript" (read: edition) "and the more important expositions of other Greek interpreters."

Furthermore, recently in Moscow, an edition of the Seventy interpreters was established based on Grabe’s work, with Grabe’s own emendations admitted into the text without hesitation. To each part—the whole Old Testament was distributed into four parts—appendices were added, in which the readings of the Alexandrian manuscript that Grabe had relegated to the margin are reviewed; but all those things which Grabe added to that manuscript, distinguished from the rest of the text by small letters, were received into the edition itself in a strange manner without any commentary. The title of the book is as follows: *The Books, that is, the Divine Scripture of the Old and New Testament; the Old according to the Seventy, from the ancient Alexandrian manuscript published as accurately as possible, and the New from the edition produced in the year 1810 by the authorization of Cyril, Patriarch of Constantinople; printed by the blessing of the Most Holy Governing Synod of all the Russias, by the Moscow Bible Society. In Moscow, in the year 1821.* <note>The original title is in Greek</note>

# PREFACE
## LXIX

We have employed Baber’s work everywhere for our critical apparatus; for this reason, our findings cannot be judged according to Grabe or, what is even worse, according to Breitinger. Since both of those editions were mostly at hand while we were preparing our apparatus, we have noted very many points in which they must be corrected by Baber; we have provided a few examples of this above.

Explaining his own studies regarding the codex <note>The Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</note> in his Preface, page 34, Baber confesses that "he exhausted himself with plainly Herculean labors in transcribing the books of the Old Testament in types resembling the paleography of the Codex Alexandrinus"; for "he compared the proof-sheets three or four times, and sometimes even six times, with the original manuscript." I know exactly what that is like, having sweated so often in the same kind of labor; and I acknowledge with a grateful heart how well he has deserved of these critical studies. Nevertheless, he by no means satisfied the difficult task he undertook, so that he often departed from the scrupulousness and precision of an editor to the great detriment of his work. For first, in Baber’s edition, there is an incredible abundance of errors, whether of the typesetter or the editor, a large part of which he corrected himself in an appendix, but others he did not correct. Thus, through the book of Genesis alone, more than thirty are noted. At Proverbs 6, three are indicated on half a page, to which is added, unless I am mistaken, a fourth not noted; for *mēde* <note>original: "μηδὲ"</note> was published there instead of *mēde* <note>original: "μηδὲ" — likely a subtle breathing mark or accent error in the Greek typeset</note>. Furthermore, examples of errors which the appendix does not correct are these: 1 Samuel <note>original: "I Βασ." (1 Kings/Kingdoms)</note> 4:10, *chiades* instead of *chiliades* <note>thousands</note>; likewise 16:3, *on than* instead of *ean* <note>if</note> I say; 2 Kings <note>original: "IV Βασ." (4 Kings/Kingdoms)</note> 22:1, he reigned *ilēm* instead of *en ilēm* <note>in Jerusalem</note>; 1 Esdras 8:56, *stiabonta* instead of *stilbonta* <note>shining</note>; Job 3:23, *ēdos* instead of *hē hodos* <note>the way</note>. Since all these are of such a nature that they seem much more likely to have originated from the editor than from the scribe, they are in no way touched upon in the appendix, which treats most other similar cases; finally, they are not found in Grabe’s index of the errors of the codex. Thus also in Genesis 46:10, *Aōd*, and likewise in verse 16, *Aroēdeis*, I held with Grabe as the reading of the codex, since *Aōd* and *Aroēdeis* seem to have flowed entirely from an error of Baber. In Genesis 46:31, the word "the" <note>original: "ο"</note> before "house" <note>original: "οικος"</note>, and likewise in 47:4, the letter "r" in the word "for" <note>original: "γαρ"</note> were supplied by pen in Baber’s edition. Nor does it rarely happen that, with the appendix contradicting the text, what the codex actually contains is either doubtful or plainly not clear. Thus in 1 Esdras 1:23, the edition has *kalchamus*, but in the appendix: "*chalchamus*. So in the manuscript codex." Since Grabe testifies to the latter, I have followed it. Likewise 2 Kings 2:3, while the edition has *epanōthen* <note>from above</note>, in the appendix it is: "*apanōthen*. Thus our codex reads with very many manuscripts." Since Grabe also defends *apanōthen*, *epanōthen* is entirely from Baber’s error. Likewise Jeremiah 31:36, the edition: *cheidaras*, which Grabe and before him Walton also testified to; the appendix: "*cheiradas*. So the manuscript codex has it." I have accepted the former; the fault...

<keywords>Baber, Grabe, Codex Alexandrinus, textual criticism, biblical manuscripts, errata, paleography</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of editorial choices and the comparison between different editions of the Septuagint (the Greek Old Testament), specifically focusing on the work of previous scholars like Grabe and Baber.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA
->(Introduction)<-

...for it seemed best to place these in the appendix, not in the main text. The same reasoning applies to the passage in 1 Maccabees 4:52, where the text reads: *chas-eleou* <note>original: χασ· ελεου</note>, while the appendix reads: "*chas-elou*. Thus in the manuscript." But Grabe <note>John Ernest Grabe (1666–1711), a scholar famous for his edition of the Codex Alexandrinus</note> also testifies to that. Likewise in Genesis 50:8, the text follows Grabe with *syngeneian* <note>kindred/family</note>, while the appendix—erroneously, it seems—reads *syngeneian*; similarly in Esther 6:1, the text follows Grabe with *eispheron* <note>bringing in</note>, but the appendix appears to have the Roman reading *eisperein* by mistake. On the other hand, in 1 Maccabees 8:17, the published *achchos* <note>original: αχχως</note>, although the same Grabe defends it, seemed erroneous, since the appendix stated: "*akchos*. Thus with a double k." Furthermore, a matter remained uncertain to me in 3 Maccabees 7:17. The edition reads: *rodophonon* <note>slaying roses/red</note>, as does Grabe; but the appendix says: "*rodophoron* <note>bearing roses</note>. Thus the manuscript reads." Hence, in my critical apparatus <note>the list of textual variants at the bottom of a page</note> it appears as: "*rodophonon* (?)" Likewise in Genesis 49:21, where Baber <note>Henry Hervey Baber (1775–1869), who published a facsimile of the Codex Alexandrinus</note> edited it as *gennemati* <note>offspring/fruit; see Footnote 1</note>, and Grabe as *genemati*. In this place, we noted nothing regarding the Roman reading *gennemati*; but it was better to accept *genemati* from Grabe, noting the uncertainty <note>Footnote 2</note>, just as in 3 Kings 18:12 <note>1 Kings in modern Bibles</note> we sought "*ouk heuresei* (?)" <note>he will not find</note> from Grabe, in place of which *ouch heuresei* is read in Baber, without Grabe's variation being noted. I pass over other such matters which have taxed us, and which were not resolved by us until everything had been inspected.

Next, however, I by no means approve of Baber's efforts regarding passages that have undergone a second hand <note>corrections made by a later scribe</note>, which he mostly dealt with by saying: "What was written by the first hand is not clear." But he ought to have conjectured probable readings in most, if not all, places from the traces of erased letters and the proportions of the space, cautiously applying Holmes’s apparatus <note>Robert Holmes, known for his massive edition of the Septuagint</note> <note>Footnote 3</note>. For most things do not seem so hidden as to frustrate a strenuous investigation. Whence it happened that here and there we placed in the apparatus what seemed to us a matter of conjecture, adding a question mark or the note "it seems" (*videtur* / *vdtr*). I will prove with a few examples, treated by him in the appendix, how little subtlety he brought to this task. In 2 Chronicles 19:7, he edited *genestho* |||| *phobos* <note>let there be fear</note>, indicating that one letter had been erased. Regarding this place in the appendix he has: "A certain letter written in error was erased." But nothing had been written in error; rather, the article *ho* <note>the</note>, which many manuscripts defend, had been added by the first hand. Likewise in 2 Chronicles 29:10, between *diathesthai* <note>to make</note> and *diatheken kyriou* <note>a covenant of the Lord</note>, he indicates about ten erased letters, which he illustrates in the appendix with these words: "A certain word, perhaps written twice in error, was erased." But at that very place, the Vatican edition adds:

---

1) The same form is found elsewhere in the Alexandrian manuscript, as in Job 39:4.
2) I am no more certain about Leviticus 13:49. Grabe edited *pyrizousa* <note>turning fiery/red</note>; Baber *pyrrizousa* (as it is in the Roman edition), adding the note: "*pyrrizousa* for *pyrizousa*." I followed Baber with no mention made of Grabe's variant.
3) Passages troubled by correctors are very frequent in the New Testament fragments of the *Codex Ephraemi Syri Rescriptus* <note>a 5th-century palimpsest where the original Bible text was erased and written over</note>; but there are few whose more hidden reading I do not seem to have probably elicited. It is easily understood that there is greater difficulty in a palimpsest <note>rescripto codice</note> than in others.

<summary>The author critiques the editorial methods of previous scholars like Baber and Grabe, specifically regarding their handling of manuscript errors and erasures in the Septuagint. He argues for a more rigorous "detective" approach to recovering original text from erased or corrected sections of ancient manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, Septuagint, Codex Alexandrinus, Codex Ephraemi Syri, John Ernest Grabe, Henry Hervey Baber, manuscript erasures, philology, biblical Greek</keywords>

# PREFACE. LXXI

"my covenant" <note>original: διαθηκην μου</note>, it appears that the same phrase has been erased in the Alexandrian manuscript. Likewise, at Jeremiah 9:12, he indicates five letters were erased after "to be passed through" <note>original: διοδευεσθαι</note>, noting in the appendix: "As can be seen with the aid of magnifying glasses <note>literally: 'glass tools'</note>, the scribe had written 'and he said' <note>original: και ειπεν</note> twice." But nothing is more probable than that "it" <note>original: αυτην</note>, which is in the Vatican text, displeased a corrector. Likewise, at 2 Chronicles 6:26 <note>original: II Παραλ. 6, 26</note>, he published "that" <note>original: οτι</note> with this commentary: "A correction by a later hand." But in that place, based on the available space, it ought to be clear whether "whenever" <note>original: οταν</note> or "when" <note>original: οτε</note> was given by the first hand. Furthermore, elsewhere he notes nothing where a note is most greatly desired; as at 1 Esdras 4:7, where he published "they will kill" <note>original: αποκτεν̅νουσι</note>, by which he indicates both an older and a more recent reading, but neither is clear. In which place, since Grabe <note>John Ernest Grabe, an earlier editor</note> had published "they kill" <note>original: αποκτεινουσιν</note>, I conjectured that this very thing belonged to the second hand, but "they will kill" <note>original: αποκτεννουσι</note> belonged to the first hand. Likewise at Numbers 7:8, in Baber's text there is: "lamb-one" <note>original: αμνο-ενα</note>, which cannot have been written this way by the scribe. It had been, unless I am mistaken, "lamb" <note>original: αμνο̅</note>, to which a corrector added "one" <note>original: ενα</note> ¹).

Nor did Baber take more care to correctly distinguish between various correctors, but he set everything down indiscriminately, except that he entangled the matter even more than he explained it with the ambiguous words of his commentary. For there, he sometimes uses "ancient hand," sometimes "very ancient hand," sometimes "a certain most ancient corrector," sometimes "an old corrector," and other similar terms. These descriptions are quite far removed from an accurate examination of the manuscript.

Thirdly and finally, the most imperfect method of the entire appendix must be criticized. Why, pray tell, does he repeat ten thousand times that the manuscript has "he said" <note>original: ειπεν</note> instead of "he said" <note>original: ειπε</note>, or "he did" <note>original: εποιησεν</note> instead of "he did" <note>original: εποιησε</note>, etc.? <note>The author is complaining about the repetitive noting of 'movable nu,' a common grammatical feature in Greek that doesn't change the meaning</note>. However, after he began to report on everything that is faulty or singular, many things of the same kind are again not noted, so that you might doubt whether they are owed to the scribe or the typesetter. There is the same inconsistency in noting those things which were poorly transcribed from the manuscript in Grabe's book. Finally, such things are brought forward here and there which betray an editor poorly acquainted with the subject he treats, such as at 1 Kings 20:9 <note>original: III Βασ. 20, 9</note> "and it had been written" <note>original: και εγεγραπτο</note>: "there is a line by a certain very ancient hand." Evidently, the small line over the *epsilon* <note>the letter 'ε'</note> seems to be entirely from the scribe himself and to have the appearance of a breathing mark, by which the "and" <note>original: και</note> and the "e" <note>original: ε</note> placed next to it (the word "written" follows at the start of the next line) are separated; just as happens here and there in the Alexandrian and other most ancient manuscripts. Likewise at Sirach 46:26, "he might give" <note>original: δοτη</note> is noted in the commentary: "Perhaps for 'he might give' <note>original: δωη</note>." This kind of note is not infrequent.

XXVIII. It has already been said or indicated above that our work, therefore, flowed from Baber's edition, although effort was given so that those things which seemed to be poorly handled by him might be corrected. Which...

---

->1) I have supplied from Grabe that at Numbers 2:3, a corrector restored "the" <note>original: οι</note> from "these" <note>original: ουτοι</note>; likewise that at Joshua 6:5, he transposed "into the city face to face" to the style of the Vatican text: "face to face into the city."<-

<meta>This page continues the editor's technical introduction (Prolegomena) regarding the editorial principles used in transcribing the manuscript, specifically focusing on how orthographic variations and scribal errors were handled.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA.

A few points must be added to the matter at hand. Since the notation of variant readings <note>original: "Lectionum variantium"</note> presents the difficulty that what seems noteworthy to some does not to others, I have preferred to err on the side of including rather than omitting. Indeed, I have noted many things in the writing of the ancient scribe that are faulty, negligent, or peculiar. Yet, things that are in themselves faulty and of no individual importance often lead a more diligent investigator toward probable conclusions or the truth. Nevertheless, care had to be taken lest the apparatus grow excessively by faithfully transcribing every foolish or trivial difference, becoming more burdensome than useful to the reader. For this reason, I have generally omitted the "movable N" <note>original: "ν ἐφελκυστικόν"; a Greek grammatical feature where an 'n' is added to the end of a word to avoid a vowel clash</note> which is added almost everywhere in the Alexandrian manuscript <note>The Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century Greek Bible</note> contrary to standard custom. Likewise, I have omitted instances where *ai* and *e* differ and the difference is of absolutely no consequence, for example: *you shall see*, *you yourselves say*, *in shackles* instead of *in fetters* (4 Kings 25:7); similarly, the frequent confusion of *oi* and *y* (as in Tobit 3:2, *you judge*; Psalm 118:114, *if to you*), *ē* and *i* (as in Proverbs 10:12, *friendship*; 4 Kings 16:17, *of stone*; Genesis 21:23, *to do wrong* instead of *to do wrong*; Leviticus 11, *cloven-hoofed* instead of *cloven-hoofed*; for it appears now one way, now the other in the codex), *ē* and *ei* (as in Genesis 39:9, *has been removed* instead of *has been removed*; Proverbs 17:1, *with pleasure*), *i* and *oi* (as in *of Phoenicia* Isaiah 23:2), and *ē* and *y* (which is very rare, but see Exodus 28:27, *tunic*).

Likewise, I have generally not noted where *n* is not changed before labials and palatals, nor assimilated before liquids, such as: *breathing*, *I have fallen together*, *companion*, *of all tribes*; *you abandoned*, *you have killed*, *dagger*, *to anoint*, *a writing*; or *you remained*, *to mix together*, *mixed together*. Generally, as I have noted, the codex has *in the midst* <note>original: "εμ μεσω"</note> (I do not know whether it is better to write it as two words or one); but *in the midst* <note>original: "εν μεσω"</note> is also found, as in Ezekiel 7:14 and 9:2. Usually—though I have occasionally omitted this, as in Genesis 16:11 and 38:18 ff.—it is written *in the womb* <note>original: "εγ γαστρι"</note>, but *in the womb* <note>original: "εν γαστρι"</note> is not avoided, as in Exodus 2:22 and 2 Kings 11:5. <gloss>1))</gloss> To this category also belong instances which I have generally indicated elsewhere (such as Isaiah 49:12, *from the land*), *from Haran* Genesis 29:1; *poured out* 1 Kings 30:16; likewise, I omitted at Genesis 26:21 and Deuteronomy 28:48 *enmity* and *to enemies*; also several times *of the morning* (as in Joshua 6:15) and *having risen early* (as in Exodus 34:4) for the standard forms of *morning* and *having risen early* (as in Exodus 32:7 and elsewhere), although I have carefully indicated similar forms that recur repeatedly (such as *flesh-hooks* and *flasks*). Above all, however, the confusion between *i* and *ei* must be explained in this place. For this confusion is so frequent and so inconsistent that it seemed useless to record it everywhere with exact—

---

1) A similar inconsistency exists in other words; for example, it usually has *forty* <note>original: "τεσσερακοντα"</note> and *to destroy utterly* <note>original: "εξολεθρευειν"</note>, but occasionally, as in Nehemiah 6:15 and Exodus 16:35, it uses the variant *forty* <note>original: "τεσσαρακοντα"</note>, and in 1 Kings 2:31, 33, *to destroy utterly* <note>original: "εξολοθρευειν"</note>. Likewise, one often reads *to search* <note>original: "εραυναν"</note>, but here and there *to search* <note>original: "ερευναν"</note> is also read. There are other similar cases, all of which have been diligently reported. There will be those to whom even *four* <note>original: "τεσσαρες"</note> (the most frequent form is *τεσσερας*) *oxen* in Numbers 7:7 might seem noteworthy.

<summary>The editor explains his criteria for recording variant readings in the Codex Alexandrinus, noting that he chose to omit frequent and trivial spelling inconsistencies (like itacism and non-assimilation of consonants) to keep the scholarly apparatus manageable.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, Codex Alexandrinus, orthography, Greek phonology, itacism, scribal errors, biblical manuscripts, editorial method</keywords>

# PROLEGOMENA.

...to express most accurately in the critical apparatus. Indeed, these [spelling variations] are reported much more frequently in the later part of the work than in the earlier ^1)), nor were they easily neglected where they held some significance (as the word <term>eidea</term> <note>original: είδεα; "forms" or "appearances"</note> was nowhere omitted, appearing several times in the Vatican text itself, among others) or at least some appearance of consistency (as in <term>alimma</term> <note>original: άλιμμα; "ointment"</note>, <term>katalelimmenos</term> <note>original: καταλελιμμένος; "having been left behind"</note> and similar cases). However, we have neglected <term>Israeletai</term>, <term>Ismaeletai</term>, <term>Moabetai</term> <note>original: ισραηλείται, ισμαηλείται, μωαβείται; archaic/variant spellings of Israelites, Ishmaelites, and Moabites</note> where they were placed immediately next to <term>Israelitai</term>, <term>Ismailitai</term>, <term>Moabitai</term> <note>standard spellings with -i- instead of -ei-</note>; likewise, we were often silent regarding <term>oikteirmoi</term> or <term>oikteirmon</term> <note>original: οικτειρμοί, οικτειρμών; "mercies" or "merciful"</note> instead of the spelling with *-i-*; likewise, we generally remained silent where sometimes <term>phleia</term> <note>doorpost</note>, <term>plemmeleia</term> <note>trespass</note>, <term>douleia</term> <note>slavery</note>, <term>anchisteia</term> <note>right of kinship</note>, <term>panoikeia</term> <note>with the whole household</note> were found, and other times <term>phlia</term>, <term>plemmelia</term>, <term>doulia</term>, <term>anchistia</term>, <term>panoikia</term> were found. Likewise, those which are placed indiscriminately such as <term>leitourgia</term> and <term>leitourgeia</term> <note>original: λειτουργία, λειτουργεία; "ministry" or "service"</note>, or <term>klitos</term> and <term>kleitos</term> <note>original: κλίτος, κλείτος; "side" or "slope"</note> (in a few continuous verses of Exodus 25, they are written as <term>kleitos</term>, <term>kleite</term>, <term>klitesi</term>, <term>kliton</term>, <term>kleitous</term>, <term>klitous</term>) were more often passed over than reported. Likewise, we occasionally omitted <term>Exekeias</term> <note>Hezekiah</note> when it was usually <term>Exekias</term>; likewise often <term>idolon</term> for <term>eidolon</term> <note>idol</note>, although it was reported more frequently.

To this is added a great abundance of errors of such a kind that they serve only to prove that the Alexandrian codex, just like so many other similar monuments, is deformed by many faults; a fact which is already sufficiently attested by those things which some reason persuaded us to report in the commentary. What sort of category of faults I mean, these examples will show: Genesis 3:10, <term>peripantos</term> for <term>peripatountos</term> <note>walking</note>; 10:9, <term>tigas</term> for <term>gigas</term> <note>giant</note>; elsewhere <term>ken</term> for <term>gen</term> <note>earth</note>, <term>oigon</term> for <term>oikon</term> <note>house</note>; 13:17, <term>autes</term> written twice; 24:14, <term>en tou</term> for <term>en touto</term> <note>in this</note>; 37:3, <term>ega</term> for <term>egapa</term> <note>he loved</note>; 41:27, <term>epta este</term> (for <term>ete</term> <note>years</note>); Exodus 14:25, <term>auxonas</term> for <term>axonas</term> <note>axles</note>; 15:21, <term>anabater</term> for <term>anabaten</term> <note>rider</note>; Leviticus 25:13, <term>estoepi</term> for <term>en to etei</term> <note>in the year</note>; 1 Samuel 7:12, <term>ana beson</term> (for <term>meson</term> <note>middle</note>); Genesis 28:22, <term>apodekatosoi</term> for <term>apodekatoso</term> <note>I will give a tenth</note>; Genesis 40:20, <term>epoiou</term> for <term>epoiei</term> <note>he made</note>; Deuteronomy 10:8, <term>epecheusthai</term> for <term>epeuchesthai</term> <note>to pray</note>; several times in Genesis <term>Abran</term> for <term>Abram</term>; likewise <term>ois</term> (for <term>eis</term>) <term>oikon</term> <note>into the house</note>, <term>on</term> (for <term>en</term>) <term>odyne</term> <note>in pain</note>, <term>dianebai</term> for <term>diabaine</term> <note>cross over</note>, <term>n</term> (for <term>e</term>) <term>kardia</term> <note>the heart</note> ^2)), <term>eplethunthn</term> (1 Samuel 1:12) for <term>eplethunen</term> <note>she multiplied/continued</note>; likewise <term>eossou</term> for <term>eos ou</term> <note>until</note>, <term>oi douloissou</term> <note>your servants</note> etc.; likewise Joshua 4:23, <term>epoiesan kurios o theos</term> <note>the Lord God they made (plural verb with singular subject)</note>; Genesis 47:24, <term>to pemptos</term> <note>the fifth (neuter article with masculine noun)</note>; 19:31, <term>pros ten neotera</term> <note>to the younger (accusative)</note>; 7:9, <term>ton</term> (and <term>ten</term> precedes and follows it) <term>kiboton</term> <note>the ark (masculine article with feminine noun)</note>.

---

1) Throughout several chapters of Genesis, we also mostly passed over <term>iden</term>, <term>idon</term> <note>he saw, they saw</note> placed for <term>eiden</term>, <term>eidon</term>. Although these seem to be written indiscriminately (cf. <term>eiden</term> 9:22, 29:32, likewise ** <term>eiden</term> 1:4, 31:2), and even <term>eidein</term> 44:23, <term>eidontes</term> 37:3 and similar forms are found, nevertheless we will supply what was omitted in this place. Therefore, it is <term>iden</term> in the Alexandrian: VIII, 13 (a rewritten passage), XIII, 10, XVIII, 2, XIX, 28, XXI, 19, XXII, 4, 13, 14, XXIV, 30, 63, 64, XXVI, 8, XXIX, 10, XXX, 8, XXXI, 42, XXXII, 25, XXXIII, 1 (rewritten passage), 5, XXXIV, 2. Likewise <term>idon</term>: VII, 1, IX, 23, XVI, 13, 14, XXXII, 30, XXXIII, 10. Likewise IV, 4, <term>epiden</term>. In the Roman edition itself of Genesis XII, 15, <term>idon</term> is held, and it is credible that the same form is found much more frequently in the Vatican codex.

2) Regarding faults of this kind, one must see diligently that the error of the eyes [of the editor] has not imputed them to the codex.

<meta>This page continues the introductory technical discussion (Prolegomena) regarding the transcription of the Codex Alexandrinus, specifically focusing on scribal errors and the order of the biblical books.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA

Exodus 7:17, "I will change" <note>original Greek: μεταβασιλει</note> instead of "I will change" <note>original: μεταβαλει</note>; Genesis 41:51, "to forget" <note>original: επελαθεσθαι</note>; likewise entirely foolish errors in Genesis 38:16, "to me" <note>original: προς με</note> instead of "to you" <note>original: πρ. σε</note>; 27:6, "Isaac" instead of "Jacob"; 27:17, "Rebekah's" instead of "Jacob"; 1 Samuel 15:5 <note>original: I Βασ.</note>, "wars" <note>original: πολεμων</note> instead of "cities" <note>original: πολεων</note>; Deuteronomy 28:31, "wine" <note>original: οινος</note> instead of "donkey" <note>original: ονος</note>; likewise the confusion of "you" <note>plural</note> and "us" etc., in places such as 1 Maccabees 3:22, "but we <note>ημεις</note> do not fear" <note>where the verb is 'you all fear'</note>; Jeremiah 3:22, "we will be you"; Joshua 9:19, "our clothes and your shoes"; and in the same chapter, verse 30, "we were afraid for your souls from your face" <margin>1)</margin>.

On the other hand, we have often thought it necessary to record, for sound reasons, things which might seem of no value to a casual observer, such as 2 Samuel 22:40 <note>original: II Βασ.</note>, "by power" <note>original: δυναμι</note> instead of "by power" <note>original: δυναμει</note>, where conjecture might waver between the two forms. Likewise 1 Samuel 4:15, "of them" <note>original: αυτων</note> instead of "of him" <note>original: αυτου</note>; for in that "of them" a different reading may lie hidden. Likewise Deuteronomy 13:15, "destroying you shall destroy" <note>original: αναιλων αναιλεις</note> instead of "removing you shall remove" <note>original: αναιρων ανελεις</note>; I recorded this so it would be apparent how easily one could pass from one form to the other. Likewise 2 Chronicles 18:26 <note>original: II Παραλ.</note>, "to put away" <note>original: αποθεσθαι</note> instead of "put away!" <note>imperative; original: αποθεσθε</note>, which I displayed because of the twin passage in 1 Kings 22:27 <note>original: III Βασ.</note>. Likewise, in various places, "he will deliver me" <note>original: εξελουμαι</note> or "deliver me" <note>original: εξελου μαι</note> is recorded instead of "deliver me" <note>original: εξ. με</note>, although it has scarcely any significance other than to show that a drowsy scribe was thinking of the future tense form because of the similarity in sound. Likewise Jonah 4:15, I transcribed "her tossings" <note>original: σαλους αυτης</note> adding the note "(? her own tossing?)" <note>original: σαλου εαυτης</note>. For unless perhaps the editor of the manuscript confused the letter *sigma* with *epsilon*, the scribe must be judged to have confused the very same things.

Furthermore, to pass over other things which do not seem to require commentary, it should be noted that those words which were written as abbreviations <term>nomina sacra: sacred names abbreviated in ancient manuscripts to show reverence</term> in the manuscript have mostly been written out in full by us, such as **Christ, God, Father, Mother, Man, Heaven, Spirit, Jerusalem, Israel**. This pertains especially to **David** <note>original: δ̄ᾱδ</note>, which you might doubt whether it should be rendered as *Dauid* (as we have given it) or *Daueid*.

Finally, the order of the books must be presented, which could not be indicated in the commentary as being different in the **Codex Alexandrinus** from the **Vatican edition**. I am able to transcribe this list, which was placed before the text in the manuscript itself by an ancient hand. It is as follows: **Genesis of the World, Exodus from Egypt, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy, Joshua son of Nun, Judges, Ruth.**
At this point is added: **Together 8 books.**
It continues: **1 Kings, 2 Kings, 3 Kings, 4 Kings <note>These correspond to 1 & 2 Samuel and 1 & 2 Kings in modern Bibles</note>, 1 Chronicles, 2 Chronicles.**
Again is added: **Together 6 books.**
There follow: **16 Prophets** in this manner: **Hosea 1, Amos 2, Micah 3, Joel 4, Obadiah 5, Jonah 6, Nahum 7, Habakkuk 8, Zephaniah 9, Haggai 10, Zechariah 11, Ma-**

---

<margin>1) Of another kind are grammatical errors <term>soloecismi: errors in concord or syntax</term> such as "every herb," "every craftsman," "every house," "every labor," "every word" <note>where the Greek word 'every' does not match the gender of the noun</note>, placed sometimes in the Alexandrinus, sometimes in the Vatican, sometimes in both manuscripts, and also preserved here and there in the Roman edition: things of this sort have been recorded by us everywhere.</margin>

-># PROLEGOMENA<-

XXIX. Regarding the so-called Prayer of Manasseh: since it is found joined with the Septuagint <note>The Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> in various places, both in ancient handwritten books and in printed editions (first in Walton’s Polyglot), I shall here transcribe it from the Codex Alexandrinus <note>A 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible</note>—where it is read among the hymns attached to the Psalter—for the sake of supplementing the Vatican edition, should anyone wish to do so <sup>5)</sup>. **The Prayer of Manasseh.** O Lord Almighty in heaven, God of our fathers, of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and of their righteous offspring; who made heaven and earth with all their order; who shackled the sea by the word of your command; who closed the abyss and sealed it by your fearful and glorious name; at whom all things shudder and tremble before the face of your power, for the magnificence of your glory is unbearable, and the wrath of your threat against sinners cannot be endured; yet the mercy <note>The manuscript reads "oil" (elaion) instead of "mercy" (eleos) due to a common spelling error</note> of your promise is immeasurable and unsearchable; for you are the Lord Most High, compassionate, long-suffering, and very merciful, and relenting over the evils of men. (Elsewhere, more is added at this point in this manner: You, O Lord, according to the fullness of your goodness, have promised repentance and forgiveness to those who have sinned against you, and in the fullness of your mercies you have appointed repentance for sinners unto sal-
<meta>catchword: sal- (salvation)</meta>

***

1) After this in the text, Baruch, Lamentations, and the Letter of Jeremiah are distinguished.
2) Inscribed in the text as: *the priest*; subscript: *1 Esdras*.
3) The inscription of the book itself is: *priest*. Nehemiah follows this book, not separated from the one preceding it.
4) Prefixed to the Psalter are: 1. *Of Athanasius, Archbishop of Alexandria, on the Psalms.* (This is Athanasius’s letter to Marcellinus.) 2. *Arguments* (so the manuscript) *of Eusebius Pamphili.* 3. *Summaries of the Psalms.* 4. *Daily canons of the Psalms.* 5. *Nightly canons of the Psalms.* Following at the end of the Psalter are: *Hymns 1 through 14.* 1 is the Ode of Moses from Exodus 15:1ff. 2 is the Ode of Moses from Deuteronomy 32:1ff. Under 3 through 9 are presented the prayers of Hannah the mother of Samuel, Isaiah, Jonah, Habakkuk, Hezekiah, Manasseh (see above), and Azariah. 10 is the *Hymn of our Fathers* from Daniel 3:52ff. 11 is the *Prayer of Mary the Mother of God* from Luke 1:46ff. 12 is the *Prayer of Simeon* from Luke 2:29ff. 13 is the *Prayer of Zechariah* from Luke 1:68ff. 14 is the *Morning Hymn*. It begins: *Glory to God in the highest and on earth peace*; it ends: *Extend your mercy to those who know you.*

# LXXVI PREFACE.

-> (salvation.) <- <note>original: τηρίαν; this is the conclusion of the preceding sentence from the previous page.</note> You therefore, O Lord, God of the righteous, have not appointed repentance for the righteous, for Abraham and Isaac and Jacob, who did not sin against You; but You have appointed repentance for me, the sinner, because I have sinned more than the number of the sands of the sea. For my iniquities have multiplied, (elsewhere is added: *O Lord, my iniquities have multiplied*) and I am not worthy to gaze upon and see the height of heaven because of the multitude of my injustices, being bowed down by a heavy iron chain so that I might lift up my head <note>original: ἀνανεῦσαί; literally "to look up" or "nod back"</note> because of my sins (elsewhere: *so that I may not lift up my head*), and there is no relief for me. For I have provoked Your anger and done what is evil before You (elsewhere is added: *not having done Your will and not having kept Your commandments*), setting up abominations and multiplying offenses. And now I bend the knee of my heart (elsewhere is added: *my*) beseeching the kindness that comes from You. I have sinned, O Lord, I have sinned, and I acknowledge my iniquities. I ask (elsewhere: *But I ask*) beseeching You: Forgive me, O Lord, forgive me; do not destroy me along with my iniquities, nor in Your anger reserve evils for me forever, nor condemn me to the lowest parts of the earth. For You are, O Lord (elsewhere: *God*), the God of those who repent, and in me You will show Your (elsewhere: *show all Your*) goodness, because You will save me, unworthy as I am, according to Your great mercy. And I will praise You always throughout the days of my life; for all the power of the heavens hymns You, and Yours is the glory forever, amen.¹)

XXX. I pass now to the second most abundant instrument of our apparatus <term>apparatus: the collection of manuscripts and variants used to reconstruct a text</term>, the **Codex Friderico-Augustanus**. I discovered this in 1844, when, after surveying the most famous libraries of Europe, I visited Eastern lands and the monasteries that flourish there, among the discarded remains of torn volumes. I brought the discovered treasure from the East to my fatherland that same year, and having honored it with the name of **Frederick Augustus**, King of Saxony—under whose auspices I had undertaken the journey—I yielded it with a testimony of gratitude to those who then held the supreme council for administering the fatherland. When, by their will, it had been deposited in the public library of the University of Leipzig, I prepared a most accurate and splendid edition under this title: ***Codex Friderico-Augustanus, or Fragments of the Old Testament from the Greek codex which is easily the most ancient of all surviving in Europe. Discovered in the East, brought to the fatherland, and edited in the manner of the codex by Constantine Tischendorf. Leipzig 1846.*** ²) In the preface to that work—to pass over other matters—I discussed its homeland, its antiquity, and its correctors...

2) Beyond the *Preface*, the entire work was produced from the lithographic press of Uckermann.

# PROLEGOMENA

This has been explained more fully in the notes and remarks. Regarding these matters, I will speak briefly here, and I can refer anyone seeking more information to the edition of the manuscript itself.

As for the homeland of the manuscript, I wonder if it might have been that very part of Egypt from which the translation of the "Seventy Men" <note>The Septuagint, the Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> is said to have first come forth; if not that, it certainly seems to have been produced in one of the monasteries closest to Lower Egypt. See Section 10.

I spoke concerning the age of the manuscript in Section 11. There, I began by exposing the perverse freedom taken by otherwise learned men in estimating the age of ancient manuscripts; some of them seek to diminish the claim of antiquity in every way, while others seek to extol it in every way, yet both often remain ignorant of the actual facts. For no one can truly know unless they have diligently practiced this type of study; which, above all else, requires that one has seen with their own eyes whatever papyri and parchments of the highest Greek antiquity have survived. Having set these things forth, the signs of extreme antiquity visible in our manuscript are treated in such a way that I might compare similar ancient manuscripts which I have personally seen, such as the Vatican Biblical codex <note>Codex Vaticanus</note>, the Vatican Dio Cassius, the Borgian fragments of John, the Vienna Genesis and both Vienna manuscripts of Dioscorides, the Paris and Leiden fragments of the Pentateuch, the Florentine Institutes, the Codex Alexandrinus, and the rewritten manuscript <term>palimpsest: a manuscript where the original text has been scraped off and written over</term> of Ephraem the Syrian. By this comparison, the result is that there is no manuscript that precedes the Friderico-Augustanus in age, and very few—such as the Vatican Bible and those fragments of the Pentateuch—that nearly equal it. The arguments for this fact are urged primarily as follows:

1. The style of the uncial letters <note>original: "litterarum uncialium" — a script written entirely in large, rounded capital letters</note> is so simple, elegant, and consistent that it agrees with some of the Herculaneum papyri more than any other Greek manuscripts.

2. The writing has absolutely no "initial letters," as they are called <note>large decorative letters at the start of paragraphs</note>. Just as this is the case in the Herculaneum scrolls and other papyri, as well as in the Vatican Biblical codex and the most famous fragments of the Pentateuch, it is already quite different from very ancient manuscripts like the Alexandrinus, the Dio Cassius, the Ephraem the Syrian, the Vienna Genesis, the Cambridge manuscript, and other codices which are thought for good reasons to have been written around the fifth century.

3. The punctuation is so simple and rare that, for example, through two columns of the second leaf a point is found nowhere, in the third once, and in the fourth twice (see Section 5); I do not know if this feature is shared with any manuscript besides the Vatican Bible.

4. Unique to the Friderico-Augustanus among all manuscripts is the fact that it has the text on every page divided into four columns; by this feature, it comes closest to the appearance of the papyrus scrolls such as those yielded by Herculaneum. How serious an argument for antiquity this is can be understood from the fact that other manuscripts...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely continuing the argument about column counts on the following page.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA

The most ancient manuscripts, and these alone—such as the biblical *Vaticanus*, the *Dio Cassius*, the two very old copies of the Samaritan Pentateuch which I saw at Nablus in Syria, (as well as the Syriac codex recently brought to London from Nitria, which a subscription proves to be from the fourth century, and the very ancient fragments of the Latin Pentateuch at Lyon)—utilize a three-column layout. <note>The author is using the number of columns per page as a diagnostic tool to date the manuscript to the 4th century.</note>

There are other less specific features to consider, among which the method of the correctors is of great importance. While it is probable that the second and third of these correctors touched the codex some centuries later, they themselves do not seem to be later than the end of the sixth century. Having weighed all these points cautiously and diligently, I concluded that the **Friderico-Augustanus** codex appears to have been written around the middle of the fourth century, and I hold no different opinion now. If anyone wishes to examine this matter both learnedly and scrupulously, they would do a most welcome service; however, I grant no weight to a vague desire for doubting or to ignorance, however arrogant it may be.

Regarding the nature of the text, I did not discuss it at great length in the *Prolegomena* of the codex, nor is it my intention to do so here. But this much I have never doubted—which has recently been clearly shown by others—that the **Friderico-Augustanus** codex must be considered among the most excellent tools for restoring the text of the Septuagint. <note>The Septuagint (LXX) is the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>

XXXI. The contents of the **Friderico-Augustanus** codex are as follows:

On the first 4 leaves is 1 Chronicles 11:22 – 19:17. <note>original: "Παραλ." (Paralipomenon)</note> From "...Benaiah, he smote..." <note>original: "-βασαηλ ουτος επαταξεν"</note> to "...and fought against him." <note>original: "και επολεμησεν αυτον"</note> There follows 2 Esdras 9:9 ("the Lord our God") <note>original: "κυριος ο θεος ημων"</note> to the end of the book; likewise the entire books of Nehemiah and Esther, along with the book of Tobit up to 2:2, "a poor man of our brothers." <note>original: "πτωχον των αδελφων ημων"</note> Leaves 5–19. Then from leaf 20 to leaf 42 is read Jeremiah 10:25, "upon the families" <note>original: "επι γενεας"</note> to the end of Jeremiah. Finally, Lamentations is added up to 2:20, "priest and proph-." <note>original: "ιερεα και προφη-"</note> To these parts of the sacred text has very recently been added a fragment presented by me in the new collection of *Unpublished Sacred Monuments*, containing Isaiah 66:12–24 and Jeremiah 1:1–7, from which I shall provide variant readings at the end of this section ¹).

There is indeed a serious diversity of multiple hands in this codex. The one who first followed the original scribe of the codex seems to be from the number of those who were called ancient <term>diorthotae</term> <note>Greek: διορθωταί; professional "correctors" or proofreaders in ancient scriptoria</note>, that is, those whose duty it was to recognize and check newly written works quite carefully against the exemplar from which they were copied. We have marked the contributions of this hand with two asterisks (**) in the apparatus, but they are almost exclusively things that had to be amended because they were written incorrectly. There follow—

---

1) I have indicated in the *Prolegomena* of the *Unpublished Sacred Fragments*, section VII, page XL, that this fragment belongs to the other parts of the Codex Friderico-Augustanus, which were discovered by me in the East and are much more extensive than my Leipzig portions. I would wish those who are interested to consult that passage.

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the manuscript's scribal history, specifically focusing on the third and fourth hands (correctors) and their relationship to the Hexapla of Origen.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA

The third and fourth hands, or the second and third correctors (marked in the apparatus as \*, \* and \*\*). Both touched upon many passages and especially expanded the text with additions. Much agrees between the two; for just as the third corrector changed or deleted what the second had written in various places, so those things which he neither changed nor deleted seem to be approved by the third himself $^1$). For both have this in common: they sought material from the *Hexapla* <note>original: "opere Origenis hexaplari"; a massive six-columned edition of the Old Testament compiled by Origen in the 3rd century</note> of Origen to insert into the text. This is evidenced by a double note of the second corrector, which was revised by the third. For at the end of the book **2 Esdras**, the following was added by the third hand:

**"It was compared against an exceedingly ancient copy corrected by the hand of the holy martyr Pamphilus; at the end of which copy was a certain subscription in his own hand, which read as follows: 'It was transcribed and corrected against the Hexapla of Origen; Antoninus compared it; I, Pamphilus, corrected it.'"**

Indeed, these same words are repeated more fully, precisely, and correctly by the same hand at the end of the book of **Esther** in this manner:

**"It was compared against an exceedingly ancient copy corrected by the hand of the holy martyr Pamphilus; and at the end of the same ancient book—which began from the first book of Kings and ended at Esther—there was a certain subscription in the same martyr's own hand, written in the margin** <gloss>the 'o' was corrected from 'ω'</gloss> **which read as follows: 'It was transcribed and corrected against the Hexapla of Origen, which had been corrected by him; Antoninus the Confessor compared it; I, Pamphilus, corrected the volume in prison, through the great grace and bounty of God; and if it be not too bold to say, it is not easy to find a copy equal to this one.' — However, that same ancient book differed from this volume regarding certain** <gloss>'certain' (tina) was corrected from 'the' (ta)</gloss> **proper names."**

From this note, it becomes clear why many more annotations are found up to the end of the book of Esther than through the fragments of Jeremiah. As for the first four leaves from the books of Chronicles—which the note testifies were present in Pamphilus’s exemplar—the reason why these same correctors did not touch them is understood from what is found written by the third hand at the end of the fourth leaf, marked with three crosses. It reads:

**"Up to the sign of the three crosses is the end of the seven extra leaves that do not belong to Esdras."**

They testify, therefore, that those four leaves (along with three others long since lost) were placed in the wrong location, not so much...

---

> 1) Since there is great difficulty in distinguishing between the second and third correctors, both in the manuscript itself and in its edition, those details which we have painstakingly extracted and placed in the apparatus of this book may be considered as a commentary. Furthermore, in many proper names and other words containing the letter *i*, it cannot be said whether the diaeresis points <note>the two dots (ï) used to separate vowels</note> are due solely to the corrector or if the corrector merely restored what he already found. I doubt whether that same mark was everywhere correctly transcribed from the Codex Alexandrinus by Baber (Grabe, for his part, provided what seemed right to himself).

# PREFACE.

## LXXX

inserted into the manuscript rather than **introduced by a scribe's error and perhaps written twice.** For that reason, I believe, the person who performed the revision passed over it. Finally, a fifth hand, which was primarily concerned with restoring—or rather, defacing—faded letters throughout, added things which we have included in the critical apparatus <note>The "apparatus" is the list of textual variations at the bottom of a scholarly page.</note> only once: we have marked these with the symbol \*\*\*. See Jeremiah 52:12.

Furthermore, in noting the readings of this manuscript, we have followed the same rule as in the Alexandrian manuscript <note>Codex Alexandrinus, a 5th-century Greek Bible</note>, except that even fewer of those details which pertain less to such an apparatus (see section XXVIII) have been omitted. I will add only a few things not unworthy of mention. For more often than is expressed in the apparatus, the vowels *oi* and *u* are found confused, as in Nehemiah 9:6: *soi* <note>original: "σοι"</note> (which is the same in the Alexandrian <note>1)</note>) instead of *sy* <note>original: "συ"</note> (*you yourself are*); likewise Jeremiah 22:6 (*soi moi*); Esther 1:6 *schyniois*; Jeremiah 15:18 *loipountes*. Repeatedly in the verb *pheugein* <note>to flee</note> and similar words, the letter *g* has been dropped (by the first hand), as in Jeremiah 27:28 *pheuonton*; 45:19 *pepheuoton*. A similar pattern is found in *bibion* <note>2)</note> <note>a misspelling of biblion, "book"</note> twice in Jeremiah 28:60, 63; and several times *Ioumaia* for *Idoumaia* <note>Edom/Idumea</note>. To the confusion of *ei* and *i* belong these examples, which could have been reported in their proper place: 1 Chronicles 12:26 *Leuei* (also in the Alexandrian in this place); 15:4 *Leueitas*; Lamentations 1:6 *euprepia*. It has already been noted above that *Ephraim*, not *Ephraïm*, is written in the Codex Friderico-Augustanus at 1 Chronicles 12:30, following the style of the Alexandrian.

XXXII. At this point, let me supply the variety of readings found in a fragment of the same Codex Friderico-Augustanus recently published by me.

In Isaiah 66:13, the manuscript has: *so I will comfort you*; 14. *your heart... to those who worship him... he will threaten*; 15. omits the second and third occurrences of *his*; 17. \* *swine's flesh*, \*\* *flesh of swine*; 18. *I know their thoughts... their tongues*; 19. *Tharsis and Phut and Lud... and Tubal... neither have they seen my works and my glory, and they shall declare my glory*; 21. *I will take for myself priests*; 22. *before me, said the Lord, so*; 23. *from month to month... and all flesh shall come to worship before me... said the Lord God.* The subscription <note>The title written at the end of a book</note> is: *The Prophet Isaiah*. In Jeremiah 1:1 the inscription is: *Jeremiah*. 1. \* *who shall dwell*, \*\* *who dwells*; 2. *which came to... Josiah*; 3. *Josiah* (but later *Josia*)... *until the completion of the eleventh year of Zedekiah.*
