# LXXXI
## PREFACE
<meta>bottom-label: SEPTUAGINT <note>The Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>.</meta>

they were accustomed ¹). These fragments, written in a hand that is entirely uniform though not identical ², were mixed in with the fragments of the New Testament, for which this codex has gained such great fame over these last two centuries. For while the studies of many learned men had been directed toward these [New Testament parts] before I published them myself in the year 1843, the fragments of the Old Testament lay almost buried and forgotten. Aside from the fact that Wetstenius <note>Johann Jakob Wettstein (1693–1754), a famous biblical scholar</note> inspected them here and there—as when he cites the reading *omeirontai* <note>original: "ὁμείρονται" - meaning 'to long for'</note> at 1 Thessalonians 2:8 from Job 3:21 in Codices A and C—and the fact that the author of the Royal Library catalog correctly indicated which books of the Old Testament most of the leaves belonged to, no one was found who sought the honor of exploring this treasure of sacred antiquity. Therefore, having successfully completed my labors regarding the New Testament fragments, I deemed it all the more my duty to rescue these venerable remains of the Old Testament from their long darkness and bring them into the light. Whatever, therefore, could be painstakingly unearthed of the ancient text—which had been intentionally erased many centuries ago—I have entirely unearthed and accurately published in the year 1845 under this title: *The Rescript Codex of Ephraem the Syrian, or Fragments of the Old Testament from the most famous Greek Parisian Codex, seemingly of the fifth century after Christ.*

In the Preface of that edition, and even more so in the one we prefixed to the New Testament fragments ³, we have set forth more fully the antiquity of the Codex Ephraemi (which takes its name from the treatises of Ephraem the Syrian, translated into Greek, which were written over the ancient script <note>This describes a "palimpsest," where a newer text is written over a scrubbed-away older one</note>). See there, then, the arguments by which we were led to say it should be assigned to approximately the middle of the fifth century, so that it seems to slightly precede the Alexandrian Codex, if it does not at least equal it in age.

Likewise, regarding the homeland of the codex, much was said in the Preface to the New Testament to prove that the codex was written in Alexandria or certainly in Egypt; from there it migrated to Palestine, Syria, or Asia Minor; and finally, having been brought to Constantinople, it experienced the hand of the person in the twelfth century who wrote the new text over the deleted old ones. From Constantinople, however, Andreas Johannes Lascaris <note>A Greek scholar who brought manuscripts to Italy</note>, sent by Lorenzo de' Medici to purchase books—

---

1) On this matter, compare the passage in the letter of Bianchini to Garbelli in the *Vindication of the Canonical Scriptures*, page 219: *“Of the sacred Volumes, seven books—namely the Psalter, Proverbs, Song of Songs, Ecclesiastes, Job, Wisdom, and Ecclesiasticus—were anciently written in verse. And I have no doubt (to use the words of the Venerable Cardinal Tommasi in the Preface to the Vulgate Edition of the Psalter) that this was done by the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint translators</note> themselves according to their own most ancient Hebrew Codices in the first five Books, and in the two latter by their own Authors, etc.”*
2) Cf. Preface to my edition of these fragments, sections 1 and 2.
3) I published these at Leipzig in 1843. Both sets of fragments joined together have this title: *The Rescript Codex of Ephraem the Syrian, or Fragments of both Testaments from the most famous Greek Parisian Codex, etc. Leipzig, 1845.*

<meta>This page continues the introductory prolegomena regarding the history and physical state of the Codex Ephraemi Syri Rescriptus, a 5th-century palimpsest.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA

sent for the sake of <note>The text refers to Andrew John Lascaris, a famous Greek scholar who collected manuscripts for the Medici family.</note> the East, he brought it into Italy, from where, after it had come into the possession of Catherine de' Medici, it was made part of the Royal Library of Paris.

Emendations of the ancient text found through these fragments of the Old Testament are fewer than those found through the New Testament. The hand to which they are owed is very similar to that of the first corrector of the New Testament, whom I have conjectured lived around the seventh century.

The text of the Ephraem codex holds a middle ground between the Vatican and Alexandrian manuscripts. However, how greatly it approaches the pure writing of the Seventy interpreters <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>—whom it professes to follow in the subscription of Proverbs ¹)—is sufficiently understood from the fact that it is, as it appears, free from Hexaplaric studies <note>The Hexapla was Origen’s massive six-columned edition of the Old Testament; "free from Hexaplaric studies" suggests the text wasn't altered to match Origen's later scholarly revisions.</note>.

**XXIV.** A catalog of the fragments which survive in the Ephraem codex must be presented. Since in these very fragments many parts have perished everywhere to such an extent that they could in no way be read, I will indicate what has been lost at the same time, lest anyone assume the Ephraem codex agrees with the published Roman text where it has not actually been read.

**Job 2:12 — 4:12.** ...having rent each <note>original: "-ρήξαντες ἕκαστος"</note> — in your words <note>original: "ἐν λόγοις σου"</note> **5:27 — 7:7.** but you <note>original: "σὺ δὲ"</note> — to see <note>original: "ἰδεῖν"</note> (More words are missing after "for yourself" 5:27; likewise many are missing here and there up to "my strength" 6:12, a few up to "of all" 6:18; from there up to "meditate" 6:30 most are missing; likewise some up to 7:7.) **10:9 — 12:2.** remember <note>original: "μνήσθητι"</note> — you are men <note>original: "ἔστε ἄνθρωποι"</note> (From the beginning up to "or even" 11:2 something is missing in every line; again two words after "blameless" 11:4. The rest are mostly intact.) **13:18 — 18:9.** I know <note>original: "οἶδα ἐγὼ"</note> — snares <note>original: "παγίδες"</note> (From "my... instead" 16:8 up to "uninhabited" 18:4 many things are missing here and there.) **19:27 — 22:14.** which the eye <note>original: "ἃ ὁ ὀφθαλμός"</note> — does not judge the kid- <note>original: "οὐ κρίνει νεφ-"</note> (mostly intact.) **24:7 — 30:1.** naked <note>original: "γυμνοὺς"</note> — in part <note>original: "ἐν μέρει"</note> (Between "unjustly" 24:10 — up to "of orphans" 24:20 a few are missing here and there; likewise 25:1, 26:1 ²); likewise after "they shall sing" up to "and not" 26:5, 6.) **31:6 — 35:16.** I have stood <note>original: "ἔσταμαι"</note> — his anger <note>original: "ὀργὴν αὐτοῦ"</note> (Between "his" 31:23 and "and if indeed rejoicing" 31:29 some things perished; the rest are mostly intact.) **37:5 — 38:17.** he will thunder <note>original: "βροντήσει"</note> — of death <note>original: "θανάτου"</note> (38:1 perished because it was written in red ink <note>Red ink, or minium, often fades or disappears more easily than black carbon ink in ancient manuscripts.</note>.) **40:20 — 42:17.** ...you will [fear] <note>original: "-σεις δὲ φοβείαν"</note> — king <note>original: "βασιλεύς"</note> (Very few things perished between 40:20 and 42:4.)

**Proverbs 1:1 — 2:8.** and to understand <note>original: "νοῆσέ τε"</note> (the preceding parts were written in red ink and have completely vanished) — to guard <note>original: "διαφυλάξαι"</note> **15:29 — 17:1.** better <note>original: "κρείσσων"</note> — with strife <note>original: "μετὰ μάχης"</note> **18:11 — 19:26.** but the glory <note>original: "ἡ δὲ δόξα"</note> — shall be <note>original: "ἔσται"</note> (Between "his heart" 19:3 and "unpunished" 19:5 very many things are missing.) **22:17 — 23:25.** but your [instruction] <note>original: "τὴν δὲ σήν"</note> — the [one who bore you] <note>original: "ἡ τε-"</note>

2) "And Bildad the Shuhite answering says," "And Job answering says": these and similar phrases, because they were written in red ink, have almost everywhere completely vanished.

How we have been occupied in noting the variant readings has already been said; for what we said regarding the Codex FA <note>Codex Sinaiticus</note> on that matter, the same holds true for the Codex of Ephraem. From this, "in the middle," "licking," "larynx" and similar words have been accurately noted. To "will be carried together" (noted in Job 28, 16. 19.) many others of that kind are added, such as "living together" in Wisdom of Solomon 8, 9. 16., "self-controlled" in the same place 8, 21., "he abandoned," etc., although "he will abandon" in Sirach 4, 19. and others also appear. <note>The editor is discussing specific Greek spelling variations (orthography) found in the manuscript, such as the use of 'n' before 'b' or 'k' instead of 'm' or 'g'.</note>

# PREFACE.

are read throughout. To *early* <note>original: ορθινη</note> (noted in Wisdom of Solomon 11:23) add *those rising early* <note>original: ορθριζοντες</note> in Sirach 4:12; but in Sirach 6:36 it is *he rises early* <note>original: ορθριζει</note>. The confusion of the letters *eta* [η] and *ei* [ει] has been reported often, such as *suffering* <note>original: παθηνους</note> in Job 29:25; *or* <note>original: οιη</note> twice in Job; *or what* <note>original: η τι</note> in Ecclesiastes 6:10; *or beautiful* <note>original: η καλη</note> twice in Song of Solomon 1:15: in all of which places the variation seemed to have some importance. It is different in *rebuke* <note>original: επιτιμησιν</note> in Ecclesiastes 7:5; *conscience* <note>original: συνηδησει</note> in Ecclesiastes 10:19; *neighbor* <note>original: πλησιον</note> in Song of Solomon 1:15; *will smile* <note>original: μηδιασει</note> in Sirach 21:10; *you shall fear* <note>original: δεισεις</note> in Sirach 7:6; *declare* <note>original: διηγησαι</note> in Job 33:5; *craftsmen* <note>original: δημιουργων</note> in Wisdom of Solomon 15:13, and a few other places. On the other hand, *filth* <note>original: ρειπου</note> and *palm trees* <note>original: φενικων</note> are reported in Job 14:4 and 40:25. About five times the letters *oi* [οι] and *u* [υ] are confused, just as we reported *is outraged* <note>original: λοιμαινεται</note> at Proverbs 27:13 (add *is outraged* in Proverbs 23:8), *to you* <note>original: σοι</note> for *you* <note>original: συ</note> in Job 15:4, and *he will open* <note>original: διανυξει</note> in Job 11:5.<note>1)</note>

## XXXV. Supplement of various readings sought from the Holmesian work for 1 Kings 12:18 — 14:9.<note>2)</note>

**12:18.** *the Lord*: five manuscripts omit ... *they feared*: four manuscripts *he feared* ... *greatly*: Manuscript X and six others omit; Manuscript XI and many others (i.e., more than ten) place it before *the Lord*.
**19.** *they said*: many manuscripts *he said* ... *to Samuel*: four others *to the Samuel* ... *for the*: four others *concerning the* ... *your God*: Manuscript XI and six others *the God of you* ... *and by no means*: three others *lest*.
**20.** *heart*: four others *the heart*. **21.** *turn aside*: four others *deviate* ... *which [plural masculine] not*: Manuscripts X, XI, and many others *which [plural neuter] not*, five others *not* ... *which not*: Manuscripts X, XI, and many others omit *which* ... *that nothing*: ten others *that they are not gods*. **22.** *that not*: Manuscripts X, XI, and many others *because not* ... *he will cast away*: three others *he will destroy*. **23.** *and far be it from me*: four others *but may it not happen to me* ... *to the Lord*: four others *before (the) Lord* ... *to cease*: Manuscript XI and six others *to let go* ... *to cease from praying* (others *to pray*): four others *from ceasing* (or *to cease*, so also for *to cease* Manuscript X in the margin) *praying* ... *concerning you*: four others add *before (the) Lord* ... *I will show*: four others *I will make known*. **24.** *and in*: three others omit *and* ... *which*: five others *as many as*, four others *that*. **25.** *you shall be added*: five others *you shall perish*.
**13:2.** *for himself* (five others *for him*) *Saul*: Manuscript II and two others *Saul*.

At 88, 25 (Wisdom of Solomon 16:17), since I doubted whether I had correctly edited *more* [πλεον] instead of *more* [πλειον] from the manuscript, the celebrated Hase wrote back to me that now indeed, due to the blackness of the spot, nothing at all can be discerned.

### XXXVI. Supplement of variant readings for
Psalms 49 — 79. I shall take these from the Tischendorf Codex V. in the Leipzig University Library, dating from the twelfth century <gloss>1)</gloss>, which I have described more fully in my *Sacred and Profane Anecdotes* <note>original: Anecdotis meis sacris et profanis</note> page 31 and following. This manuscript agrees primarily with the Alexandrian Codex <note>A famous 5th-century Greek Bible manuscript</note>, and in places where both the Vatican and Alexandrian codices depart from the standard text, it usually has many other witnesses in agreement. I will note all the more significant readings, and simultaneously indicate which readings are supported by only a few others, and which are recommended by the distinguished authority of the codices.

**49, 1.** David instead of Asaph **3.** before him **4.** to judge **6.** God **6. and 15.** omits "musical interlude" <note>original: διάψαλμα; often translated as 'Selah'</note> **10.** of the field instead of the thicket **15.** of your affliction **16.** you declare **18.** with an adulterer **19.** of wickedness and deceits **21.** before your face your sins **22.** and by no means **23.** many others: "the way which..." "my salvation" **50, 1.** from the beginning omits "To the end..." many others: "Bathsheba the wife (others omit) of Uriah" **6.** you shall conquer **10.** many others: "you will make me hear..." "bones" (alone) **13.** your Holy Spirit **51, 5.** rather than to speak **6.** omits "the" **7.** many others: "he will destroy..." "from your tent" **8.** omits the first "and" **9.** has "in the multitude" **5. and 7.** omits "musical interlude" **52, 2.** they were corrupted **5.** in eating **6.** they were terrified with fear instead of "they feared a fear" **7.** in the Lord's returning **53, 3.** you will judge me **5.** and they did not set before [them] and omits "musical interlude" **54, 1.** many others: "To Asaph" **4.** many others: "iniquities" **8. and 20.** omits "musical interlude" **9.** has "God who saves" and "from the storm" **11.** both iniquity and labor **13.** if the enemy, if he reproached me, likewise "he who hates me" and "he spoke boastfully" **15.** as together and "it was sweet to me" **16.** let it indeed come **20.** because they did not fear **22.** their hearts drew near, likewise "their words" **24.** but I, Lord, hope in you **55, 3 and seq.** is "all the day; for many are they who fight me from on high; by day I will not be afraid, but I hope in you" **5.** omits "all the day" and has "in God I have hoped" **7.** has "they watched my soul" **8.** you will thrust **10.** after "backwards" a major punctuation mark is placed, having "in whatever [day]" etc. joined with "behold I knew." **12.** many others omit "the" **13.** has "from death, my eyes from tears, and my feet from slipping; I will be well-pleasing before the Lord" etc. **56, 2.** has "in you" (with very few [others]) ... many others: "let it pass" **3. and 7.** omits "musical interlude" **5.** has "weapons" **7.** a snare and "they fell into" **8.** I will sing in my glory **57, 2.** judge uprightly ... many others omit "the" **3.** iniquity **6.** of the voice and "is charmed by the charmer," **7.** he will crush **8.** like water ... many others: "let them be weak" **9.** melted wax and "fire upon them"

## SIGNS AND ABBREVIATIONS USED IN THE APPARATUS.

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| FA | signifies | the <term>Codex Friderico-Augustanus</term> <note>A 4th-century Greek manuscript of the Bible, now known as part of the Codex Sinaiticus.</note> |
| Ax or A | - - | the <term>Codex Alexandrinus</term> (see below). |
| C | - - | the <term>Codex Ephraemi Syri Rescriptus</term> <note>A 5th-century palimpsest where the biblical text was erased and overwritten with the works of Ephrem the Syrian.</note> |
| \*, \*\* etc. | - - | the first hand, second hand, etc. <note>Refers to the original scribe versus later correctors.</note> |
| add | - - | adds. |
| om | - - | omits. |
| in f. | - - | at the end. |

Furthermore, all readings in the <term>apparatus</term> <note>The list of textual variations at the bottom of a page.</note> to which no sign is prefixed belong to the **Alexandrian codex**.

**XV.** Correction of accents, of which many examples of every kind are provided. Diacritical marks <note>marks such as the diaeresis (¨) used to separate vowels</note>. *eiselthōn* and *eiselthōn* <note>original: "εἰσελθῶν et εἰσελθὼν"; a technical distinction in Greek accentuation for the word "having entered"</note>. Enclitic words <note>words that "lean on" the preceding word for their accent</note>. Continuous and separate writing of compound adverbs. Variety of breathing marks: *hautou* and *autou* <note>original: "αὑτοῦ et αὐτοῦ"; distinguishing between "his own" (reflexive) and "his"</note>. *ouk* <note>original: "οὐκ"</note> before aspirated syllables and similar cases. Iota subscript and adscript <note>small 'i' sounds written either under or next to a vowel</note>. *nu ephelkustikon* <note>the "movable n" added to the end of Greek words for easier pronunciation</note> before a consonant. *eikosi*, not *eikosin* <note>original: "εἴκοσι, non εἴκοσιν"; the number twenty without the movable 'n'</note> . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . XX. On the notation of twin arguments and passages of the New Testament . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LII.

XXI. On the critical apparatus. Greek manuscripts of the Seventy <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note> interpreters. How poorly the apparatus of the Holmes edition <note>Referring to Robert Holmes (1748–1805), who began a massive collation of Septuagint manuscripts.</note> fares is proved by several examples . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LII — LV.

XXII. On the readings of the Vatican manuscript <note>Codex Vaticanus, one of the oldest and most important Bible manuscripts.</note> not accepted by the editors. Cited from Holmes for Genesis 47 — 50 . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LV. LVI.

XXIII. On the three kinds of critical tools: versions, fathers, and manuscripts. In note 1, page LVI, there is a brief explanation of the versions established for the Septuagint text; likewise in note 2, regarding the *Hexapla* of Origen <note>A massive six-columned edition of the Old Testament compiled by the scholar Origen.</note>. Holmes' manuscripts written before the tenth century, thirteen in number . . . LVI — LVIII.

XXIV. Eleven uncial <note>Manuscripts written in large, block-like capital letters.</note> manuscripts not yet used by Holmes. The nature of each of these is shown by examples of readings. The first is the Verona Psalms (page LVIII ff.), the second the purple Zurich Psalms (page LIX ff.), the third the London papyrus Psalms (page LX ff.), the fourth a manuscript of Genesis very recently discovered in the East by the editor (page LXI), the fifth a palimpsest <note>A manuscript where the original text was scraped off and written over.</note> manuscript of Numbers discovered and published by the editor (page LXI ff.), the sixth the Tischendorf-Leipzig fragments of the Octateuch <note>The first eight books of the Bible.</note> (page LXII ff.). The seventh, eighth, and ninth are palimpsest fragments of Isaiah, the books of Kings, and the Psalms, very recently brought to light by the editor (page LXIII ff.). Added is the tenth, containing the books of Judges and Ruth and the final part of the book of Joshua, written in minuscule <note>Small, cursive-style handwriting.</note> letters of the ninth century, now in London. Regarding the remaining two, it is explained specifically later . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LVIII — LXIV.

XXV. On the authority and scope of the three oldest manuscripts used for the continuous apparatus: the Alexandrinus, the Frederico-Augustanus, and the Ephraemi Syri rescriptus <note>A famous palimpsest where the biblical text was overwritten with the works of Ephrem the Syrian.</note> . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXIV. LXV.

XXVI. On the Codex Alexandrinus. Its age and place of origin. Ernest Grabe's edition is insufficiently accurate. In note 3, page LXVII, the edition of Breitinger (based on Grabe's) is discussed, as well as Reineccius's four-language Bible and the Moscow edition of the year 1821 . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXV — LXVIII.

XXVII. The edition of Henry Hervey Baber. That it is deformed by an incredible abundance of errors, partly corrected and partly uncorrected. On Baber's meager study regarding passages that underwent a second hand <note>Corrections made by a later scribe.</note>. On the imperfect method of the appendix . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXVIII — LXXI.

XXVIII. How the readings of the Codex Alexandrinus were applied to our apparatus; which ones were omitted. In note 1, page LXXII, several passages from the book of Genesis are supplemented where the Codex Alexandrinus has <note>original: "ιδον, ιδεν, επιδεν"</note> *idon, iden, epiden* instead of <note>original: "ειδον, ειδεν, επειδεν"</note> *eidon, eiden, epeiden* <note>These are spelling variations of the Greek word "to see."</note>. Scribal abbreviations in the same manuscript and the order of the individual books . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXXI — LXXV.

XXIX. The Prayer of Manasseh is cited from the Codex Alexandrinus, with the variety of readings added . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXXV. LXXVI.

XXX. On the Codex Frederico-Augustanus <note>Part of what is now known as Codex Sinaiticus.</note>. On the editor's studies concerning it. On its country of origin, its age, and the excellence of its text . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXXVI — LXXVIII.

XXXI. Which parts of the sacred text the Codex Frederico-Augustanus contains. On the correctors of the manuscript; on its very significant subscription <note>A concluding note at the end of a manuscript.</note> . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXXVIII — LXXX.

XXXII. The variety of readings found in a fragment of the Codex Frederico-Augustanus very recently brought to light is supplemented . . . . . . . . . LXXX.

# INDEX OF THE PROLEGOMENA

**XXXIII.** Concerning the *Codex Ephraemi Syri Rescriptus* <note>A famous 5th-century palimpsest where the original biblical text was erased and overwritten with the works of Saint Ephrem the Syrian.</note>. **XXXIV.** The fragments of the *Codex Ephraemi* are accurately reviewed, with the gaps existing within them indicated. Concerning the notation of variant readings. In Note 1 on page LXXXIV, the errors made in the previous edition of the *Codex Ephraemi* are pointed out . . . . . . . . . . LXXXII — LXXXIV.

**XXXV.** A supplement of variant readings sought from the work of Holmes <note>Referring to Robert Holmes, who began a massive collation of Septuagint manuscripts in the late 18th century.</note> for the passage 1 Kings 12:18 — 14:9 <note>original: "I Bas." referring to 1 Basileion, the Greek Septuagint title for 1 Kings (or 1 Samuel in some traditions).</note>, where the *Codex Alexandrinus* is lacking . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . LXXXIV — LXXXVI.

**XXXVI.** A supplement of variant readings for Psalms 49 — 79, taken from the *Codex Tischendorfianus V* <note>A manuscript discovered or held by the scholar Constantin von Tischendorf.</note> in the University Library of Leipzig . . . . LXXXVI — LXXXIX.
